# TOO-45 follow-up queue

What comes after the R6 replacement stages are committed. Split into **change challenges** (measuring instruments, implemented in throwaway copies and discarded) and **real defects** (candidates for actual tickets).

## Change challenges — run after the R6 stages are committed

These are **experiments, not deliverables.** Each is implemented in throwaway copies of pre-TOO-45 master and the post-R6 branch, measured, judged, and thrown away. Nothing lands in the repo.

**The discipline that makes them worth anything:** the throwaway implementations must be written *as if they were the real ticket* — same care, same tests, same handling of awkward cases. The moment an implementer takes a shortcut because "it's only an experiment", it will take that shortcut in whichever tree makes shortcuts easier, which is exactly the signal being measured.

Full detail per challenge in [[change-challenges]], produced by an agent deliberately kept blind to this ticket's analysis.

| order | challenge | axis | why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **CC-1** per-session budgets / rate limits | decision-relevant state; session identifier becomes load-bearing for policy | subsumes the session-layer scenario Arnon sketched |
| 2 | **CC-4** govern a tool with structured arguments | domain-model extensibility, dispatch fan-out | attacks the decision path from the opposite end to CC-1 |
| 3 | **CC-2** expiring / windowed rules | time-dependence, clock seams, rule identity vs pattern string | **run immediately after CC-1 as a controlled contrast** — they share the time axis but differ on cross-process state, so the delta isolates "cannot express time" from "cannot express state". Running either alone confounds them. |
| — | **semver pattern type** | duplicated vocabulary | **calibration control.** Everyone predicts it is trivial. The prefix tuple is hand-copied across ~7 sites in 4 modules with no single source of truth, and a missed site fails *silently* into `fnmatch`. Its real cost calibrates how much to trust intuition on the rest. |

Lower-ranked: CC-5 (resident long-lived process), CC-3 (rewrite tool input, not just judge it), CC-8 (decision derivation + counterfactual), CC-6 (remote org-managed policy), CC-7 (multi-user host safety).

**Flagged as likely uninformative** by the designer, recorded so nobody re-proposes them: a new `no_match_fallback` value, another governed command tool, a fifth log stream, enabling JSONLines, and anything measured by renaming.

**CC-3 carries a caveat:** it may not discriminate if the parsing IR is lossy in both a good and a bad version by design. Settle that first with a one-hour round-trip property test — decompose then reassemble every corpus command — before committing to the full challenge.

### The session-layer framing (Arnon's, worth carrying into CC-1)

A session-scoped override is naturally **an additional configuration layer** alongside the existing hierarchy, not a bolted-on policy source. That reframes the challenge into a sharper question: **is the layer abstraction open for extension, or welded to filesystem discovery?**

Measured beforehand, as a prediction to score: `Configuration` holds `layers: Tuple[ConfigLayer, ...]` and tests already hand-construct layers with zero file I/O, so injection is possible *at the type level*. But `load_configuration()` is "the single public entry point" and "performs all discovery and parsing internally" — discovery and construction are fused, with no supported production path to inject. The vocabulary is file-shaped throughout (`ConfigLayer` is "one **discovered** configuration source"; `Provenance` requires `file_format` ∈ {json, toml} and `source_type` ∈ {claude, toolguard_hook}). And `Provenance` is documented as **"display-only… without exposing file/format decisions as control flow"** — a session layer carrying an expiry makes provenance-shaped metadata control-relevant, **falsifying a stated invariant** rather than merely extending a type.

**Prediction on record:** this half will barely discriminate between the trees, because the layer model is essentially unchanged between them — D1a moved orchestration out of `Configuration` but left it as the layer holder, and the load-vs-query split was the plan's "candidate step", identified and never done. The *consuming* half (precedence in the cascade, provenance on the verdict, explaining the override in the audit trail) should discriminate, because the branch has one chokepoint and a structured verdict. If that split holds, one experiment isolates what was fixed from what was not.

## Real defects found, ranked — ACCEPTED BY ARNON 2026-08-06

**Status: accepted for fixing.** Sequencing: after R6's S3 and S2 land, because `hook.py` is contested until then (S3 removes `_verdict_from_decision` from it, S2 moves `decide()` out) and defect 3 lives there. Two agents editing the real tree concurrently is the shape that produced today's contaminated-baseline incident — not worth the risk to save twenty minutes.

**Open question for Arnon: which ticket?** These are pre-existing defects that TOO-45 did not cause and were found only because a blind reviewer went looking at the product. My recommendation is a separate ticket for defects 1-4, since mixing unrelated bug fixes into an architecture-overhaul commit is exactly what makes a refactor hard to review or revert. Defect 5 is a one-line docstring correction of a false claim and can ride along with the R6 stages, consistent with the several other false-comment corrections this ticket has already made.


All found by the blind challenge designer, then verified independently by me. **None of these were surfaced by the seven directed report agents** — a methodological result in itself: every prior agent read this ticket's analysis and inherited its frame; the blind one looked at the product instead.

**1. `log_writer` can `sys.exit(1)` (lines 198, 476) — the audit path can take down enforcement.** Claude Code treats only exit code 2 as blocking, so a full disk or a permissions error on the log directory means the hook exits non-blocking and **the tool call proceeds unjudged**. A fail-open with no timing component. Highest-ranked of these.

**2. The join key between matching and rule entries is the pattern string itself, and it is known non-unique** — `merge_entries` already exists to handle same-pattern entries with contradictory metadata. R2 removed the index-parallel arrays but left the string join.

**3. "Once per session" has been wanted three times and never implemented.** Three copy-pasted date-marker files plus three `hook.py` module globals whose own docstrings concede they cannot work in a per-call process. Worth noting this is the exact trap already recorded in long-term memory, sitting in the codebase in triplicate, and missed by every directed pass.

**4. Documentation defect:** `docs/config-sync.md` documents markers in `/tmp/toolguard-warnings/`; the code writes `<log_dir>/.toolguard-warned-*`.

**5. `resolve.py:2` claims "Pure, side-effect-free permission resolver layer"** while matching reads live disk state (`normalization.py:47-50, 81` — `exists()`, `is_symlink()`, `resolve()` — reached from `permissions.py:146` and `:194`). The narrower line-7 claim ("no logging, no stdin/stdout, no `sys.exit`") remains true.

**6. `session_id` is read by nothing on the decision path**, while `permission_mode` *is* read and is documented three times as "diagnosis only, never affects the verdict". A clean observational/decisional split that CC-1 deliberately breaks — useful context, not a defect.

## Deprioritised deliberately

**The check-to-use race in path matching.** Real, but Arnon's call (2026-08-06) is not to complicate the design for it: sub-second hook execution on a single-user machine makes a file changing underneath the check unlikely, and the system takes the strictest result across several layers, so something *could* slip but has more than one chance to be caught. Recorded as noted-and-deprioritised, not open. **Do not rat-hole on it.**

**Worth keeping from that finding, because it is not about races:** a verdict is not purely a function of config and input, since matching reads live disk state. The golden corpus implicitly assumes determinism and nobody has checked that assumption. Cheap to test; it concerns the verification infrastructure rather than the product.

## From the architectural-judge back-test, 2026-08-10

Eight defects in **committed, already-reviewed** punch-list code. Evidence and verification in [[architecture-judge-backtest]]. Ranked; disposition is a recommendation, Arnon's call.

| # | defect | where | recommendation |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `migrate()` collapses four `LockUnavailable` reasons into one `DECLINED_LOCKED`; `auto_migrate` then announces "another migration is already running" for all of them **and consumes the day's claim**, silently disabling auto-migration until tomorrow. The comment defending the consumed claim is true only of the timeout branch. | `permission_migration.py:1250`, `auto_migrate.py:174` | **fix before push.** User-visible false statement plus a silent disable. Add `DECLINED_UNAVAILABLE`, or carry the reason on the outcome. |
| 2 | `_dispatch` does `getattr(error_log, rule.log_fn_name)`, so `log_warning`/`log_error` have zero static callers from the reporter — invisible to pyright's `incomingCalls` and to `callers_of`. Test-patching mechanics driving a production indirection. | `error_reporter.py` `_ROUTING` / `_dispatch` | **fix before push.** Directly contradicts the standing principle that what static analysis cannot see, a reader cannot either. Bind the callable and patch the reporter in tests instead. |
| 3 | `hook.py` constructs the Reporter and keeps four hand-rolled `log_error`/`log_warning` calls; `hook.py:96-100` is a second copy of the severity routing table. Only remaining static callers in the package. | `hook.py:98,100,431,944` | **fold into #07 or its own item.** Small and mechanical. |
| 4 | `OncePer.run` executes a config-layer closure handed to it by `auto_migrate`, so an observability module runs config code at runtime with no import edge. `--layers` reports clean. Same class as the cycle #03 removed. | `once_per.py`, `auto_migrate.py` | **decide.** Benign form (callee opaque to the facade) but it weakens what a clean `--layers` means. Argue it or remove it — the standard applied to #03. |
| 5 | `is_builtin` means both "toolguard ships knowledge of this" and "govern by default"; `test_tool_spec.py:82` pins `DEFAULT_GOVERNED_TOOLS == BUILTIN_TOOLS`, so the first understood-but-not-governed tool fails a test. | `tool_spec.py` | **fold into TOO-51.** It is the mechanism that makes TOO-51 harder the longer it waits. |
| 6 | `TOOLS_BY_NAME` is a live mutable public dict; `BUILTIN_TOOLS`/`FILE_KIND_TOOLS`/`KNOWN_TOOL_NAMES` are import-time snapshots. Tests patch the dict, exercising a state production cannot reach. | `tool_spec.py`, `test_hook.py`, `test_hook_eval.py`, `test_verdict_corpus.py` | **fix with 5.** Read-only mapping plus one accessor. |
| 7 | The golden verdict corpus cannot see payload-key changes: the in-process corpus replays with the target already extracted, and the e2e path goes through a **sixth** hardcoded copy of the tool→key map. | `fixture_loader.py:679` | **verification-infrastructure item.** Pairs with the determinism gap above; the corpus's blind spots now number four. |
| 8 | `hook.COMMAND_TOOLS` has zero readers anywhere and is a mutable `set` among frozensets. | `hook.py:58` | **delete.** Trivial. |

**Near-miss, no action needed but worth remembering:** the #10 spec instructed pointing `tools/architecture_fitness.py`'s canary at the new registry *and deleting the comment explaining why it was deliberately independent*. Probe and probed would then share a source, making a wrong payload key invisible. It was never carried out — the coder silently declined. Nothing in review caught it.

False: `permission_resolution.apply_parse_failure_floor` and `_apply_ask_floor` both return an already-`deny` verdict untouched (`if not parse_failures or decision == "deny"`), and that exemption is defended by the regression test `TestDenyUnderBrokenConfigKeepsProvenance`. The reassurance it makes ("no rule including deny/hard_deny is silently lost") stays true; only the universal is wrong. | `toolguard/config.py`, the `Issue` built in `validation_issues` | It **was** corrected and then reverted to keep #07 comments-only. REGEX's exemption is by design (its authors control `re.DOTALL` explicitly); nothing establishes GLOB's or NATIVE's exemption as intentional rather than an oversight in a guard whose whole purpose is closing a fail-open bypass. ### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

Found because **the comments clustered**, which is the signal. None is actionable under #07's comments-only scope.

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Configuration.validation_issues` | Its docstring **enumerates seven rules**, and the body carries a comment per rule. | Seven private methods, each named for its rule and needing far less explanation, plus a delegating accumulator. |
| R2 | `compound.judge_unit` | No enumeration, but **eleven inline comment blocks across its conditionals**, each explaining one branch. Arnon caught this from the shape after two reviewers and an editor missed it. | Extract the branches; the existing comments become their docstrings almost verbatim. |
| R3 | `once_per_store.reap` | Docstring enumerates four unrelated sweep actions (delete expired claims; remove the store's own previous filename; remove legacy per-kind marker files; remove a legacy per-project db), matching four separate try/except blocks in the body. | Four private sweep functions plus a delegating `reap`, each named for what it removes. |
| R4 | `log_writer.log_discovery` | Body carries two explicitly numbered inline comment blocks (`# 1. Append the plain-text change record...`, `# 2. Mirror to the main resolution log...`), each with its own multi-line rationale -- the numbering-inside-a-function form of the same signal, found during the #07 comment sweep, 2026-08-11. | Two private steps (`_append_discovery_record`, `_mirror_discovery_to_resolution_log`) plus a delegating `log_discovery`, each keeping only its own step's comments. |
| R5 | `hook.main` | Pre-sweep docstring enumerated an 11-step algorithm; the body sequences roughly that many distinct concerns (arg parsing, TTY guard, `--eval` dispatch, Reporter setup, env config load, hook-input parse, config load, three announce/validate/divergence calls, governed-tool guard, tool-kind dispatch, output, four `except` handlers) inline in one function. Found during the #07 comment sweep, 2026-08-11 -- the numbered docstring is gone (it was also stale: it listed startup validation before stdin parsing, but the body runs them in the opposite order, since validation needs the parsed `cwd`), but the underlying shape it was describing is unchanged. **Independently confirmed by the cold-review pass, 2026-08-11**: ten inline comment blocks over a straight-line body, the same "comments cluster" signal; the reviewer's own count settles on ~5 named phases (bootstrap reporter, load config, run startup side effects, dispatch, emit), close enough to this row's "setup / dispatch / except" split that they are the same finding, not two. | Candidate: extract the setup phase (Reporter, env config, hook-input parse, config load, the three announce/validate/divergence calls) into one helper returning a small context object, leaving `main` as guard clauses plus dispatch plus the four `except` handlers. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |

| R6 | `permissions.match_command` | No enumeration, but the body walks five distinct matching shapes in one function: extended-syntax (`[regex]`/`[glob]`/`[native]`) bypass, the newline defense-in-depth guard, the `**/<component>/**` path-component special case, the `cmd:args` colon-split path (with its own `base_cmd` normalization sub-step), and the plain whole-string fnmatch fallback. Found during the #07 comment sweep, 2026-08-11 -- each shape's docstring paragraph reads almost like a standalone function description already. | Candidate: one private matcher per shape (e.g. `_match_extended`, `_match_path_component`, `_match_cmd_args`, `_match_plain`), `match_command` as a thin dispatcher over `parse_pattern`'s result. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |
| R7 | `normalization.normalize_path` | Docstring/body walk four numbered steps in sequence (collapse leading slashes; resolve symlinks; collapse an under-home path to `~/...`; conditionally add `./` to a bare relative path), each with its own body comment. Found during the #07 comment sweep, 2026-08-11. | Candidate: four private steps (e.g. `_collapse_leading_slashes`, `_resolve_symlinks`, `_collapse_home_prefix`, `_add_relative_prefix`), each taking and returning the path string, with `normalize_path` as a thin pipeline over them. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |
| R8 | `session_start._format_summary` | Docstring enumerates five independent sections; the body is five separate `if` blocks, each appending one section to `sections`, mirroring the enumeration one-for-one. Found during the #07 comment sweep, 2026-08-11. | Candidate: five private section builders (e.g. `_broken_config_section`, `_unrecognized_fallback_section`, `_conflict_section`, `_running_from_source_section`, `_stale_install_section`), each returning `Optional[str]`, with `_format_summary` filtering `None`s and joining. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |

**The generalisable rule, now in the standard and in the global CLAUDE.md:** where comments cluster inside a function is a refactoring signal, whether or not they are numbered.
| 3 | `get_command_breakdown` has no caller anywhere in `toolguard/` or `tools/`; only `test/unit/test_compound.py` exercises it. Its own docstring called it a "utility for debugging and logging", which overstates a function nothing currently uses for either. | `toolguard/compound.py` | Found during the C1 review-fix pass, 2026-08-11. Either dead code to delete or a missing call site (e.g. a diagnostic CLI/skill that should be calling it). The overstated docstring clause was removed as part of fixing an unrelated self-contradiction in the same sentence, not as a code-level fix. |

## Doc-drift and split-not-explain candidates found during #07 on `rule_entry.py`

**Two new rows for the "Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep" table above** (same "comments cluster" signal, per the comment standard's "Comment distribution is a refactoring signal" section):

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| R9 | `rule_entry.merge_entries` | Docstring numbers its three cases directly (bare-vs-structured drop, compatible union merge, contradiction). Same shape as the `validation_issues` worked example. Found during the #07 comment sweep, 2026-08-11. | Candidate: three private per-case functions (e.g. `_drop_bare`, `_union_merge`, `_report_conflict`) plus a delegating grouper, each needing far less inline commentary once named for what it does. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |
| R10 | `rule_entry.normalize_entry` | A six-way bulleted enumeration (non-empty plain string / empty string / valid structured dict / structured dict missing or malformed `match` / structured dict rejected under `is_native` / anything else) mirrors the function's own branch structure one-for-one. Found during the #07 comment sweep, 2026-08-11 -- weaker signal than R9: `_reject`'s existing extraction already removes most of the branch-body duplication a split would otherwise address, and the enumeration reads more like an exhaustive type-dispatch contract than accumulated complexity. | Candidate: `_normalize_string_entry` / `_normalize_dict_entry` (or one helper per branch) with a thin dispatcher. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |

**`technical-notes.md`'s "Single source of truth for tool-wrapper stripping" section (line 497) is stale**, and this file was off-limits to edit during this pass. It attributes `_strip_tool_wrapper`, `is_tool_wrapper`, and `_TOOL_WRAPPER_RE` to `toolguard.config` (as `config._strip_tool_wrapper` / `config.is_tool_wrapper`). All three moved to `toolguard/rule_entry.py` when that module was created (commit `5dc4816`, TOO-19 Phase 0) -- `config.py` now only re-exports them. Found during the #07 comment sweep on `rule_entry.py`, 2026-08-11. Proposed replacement text (verbatim, ready to paste in once the file is back in scope):

> ### Single source of truth for tool-wrapper stripping
>
> Permission patterns are authored wrapped as `Tool(inner)` (e.g. `Bash(git *)`)
> but matched on the unwrapped inner pattern. `rule_entry._strip_tool_wrapper` is the
> single, purely STRUCTURAL strip (`re.fullmatch(r'[A-Za-z0-9_]+\((.*)\)')`, deliberately
> not `re.DOTALL` -- see that constant's own docstring for why): it needs no
> hand-maintained tool list and handles inner parentheses (`Bash(foo(bar))` ->
> `foo(bar)`). Divergence detection recognises tool-scoped native permissions via the
> shared `rule_entry.is_tool_wrapper` predicate, which uses the same
> `_TOOL_WRAPPER_RE` -- there is no duplicated regex anywhere in the codebase.
> `config.py` imports and re-exports both. New governed tools require no change to
> any prefix list.

Also worth folding in while that section is edited: `RuleEntry`'s three deliberately-different comparison semantics (`.pattern`, `.identity()`, `merge_entries()`'s own consolidation rules -- see `RuleEntry.identity`'s docstring in `rule_entry.py`) and the `synthesized_pattern` / `real_patterns()` write-path contract (a synthesized fallback pattern must never reach `verified_write_config`'s `expected_patterns`, or it wrongly looks like a dropped rule and refuses an otherwise-safe write) have no home in `technical-notes.md` today. Both are currently carried entirely as inline docstrings in `rule_entry.py`; a short new subsection there would let those docstrings shrink to one-line pointers, but per the "never write a pointer to something not there yet" rule this was not done in this pass (`technical-notes.md` was off-limits), so the content stays inline for now.

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `patterns.py`

**Three more rows for the "Code-level defects found during #07" table above.** All found during the pass-2 comment sweep on `toolguard/patterns.py`, 2026-08-11, and all deliberately NOT fixed there (comments-only). Item 12 in that table already records the dead `PatternType.DEFAULT` branch and is not repeated here.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| 15 | **NATIVE matching is a silent false-negative machine, and on a deny rule that is a bypass.** `match_pattern`'s NATIVE branch locates each literal segment with `str.find` from just past the previous match and never backtracks, so an end-anchored pattern is checked against the *first* qualifying occurrence of its final segment rather than the last. Characterisation, brute-forced against a reference implementation (`".*".join(re.escape(s) for s in pattern.split("*"))` under `re.fullmatch`): a pattern ending in `*` always matches correctly; a pattern NOT ending in `*` fails whenever its final segment is found before the command's end. **Every deviation is a false negative** -- there are no false positives. Plausible failing deny rule: `NATIVE '*id_rsa'` vs `'cat id_rsa.pub id_rsa'` returns `False`. Re-verified live 2026-08-11 over 3,720 pattern/command pairs (148 mismatches, 40 distinct patterns, zero among patterns ending in `*`), reproducing the earlier 7,623-pair run (416 mismatches, 46 distinct patterns). | `toolguard/patterns.py:match_pattern` (NATIVE branch) | The branch comment was rewritten to state this as a rule rather than a mechanism, but the behaviour is unchanged. A fix is a one-line change in kind (search the *last* occurrence when the pattern is end-anchored, or compile to a regex as the reference does) -- but it makes previously-non-matching deny rules start matching, so it needs its own ticket and a regression sweep of existing configs. **Note the reviewer's proposed characterisation "fails whenever its final literal also occurs earlier in the command" is a necessary but NOT sufficient condition** -- it over-fires on 204 of the 3,720 pairs, e.g. `NATIVE 'a*a'` vs `'aa'` matches correctly even though `a` occurs earlier, because the earlier occurrence was consumed by a preceding segment. The condition that holds exactly is the one recorded above. |
| 16 | **`except ValueError, TypeError` in the GLOB branch is largely dead, and duplicated one level up.** `PurePath.full_match`'s Python 3.14 body contains no `raise` statement on any path, and 17 malformed glob patterns (`[`, `[a-`, `[!`, `\`, `***`, `[[`, `{`, `?[`, `//`, `""`, ... ) all returned normally rather than raising -- verified live 2026-08-11. A `None` pattern cannot arrive via `parse_pattern`, which would have raised on `.strip()` first. The identical catch is **duplicated** at `toolguard/file_matching.py:113`, wrapping the very `match_pattern` call that already swallows both exception types. | `toolguard/patterns.py:match_pattern` (GLOB branch, the `try`), duplicated at `toolguard/file_matching.py:113` | Not a behaviour bug -- dead defensive code. Decide whether to drop one or both. Related: the malformed-pattern claim in `match_pattern`'s `Returns:` block was narrowed to REGEX only during the sweep, because it is measurably false for GLOB (`GLOB '['` vs `'['` returns `True`: the malformed pattern compiles as a literal and matches). |
| 17 | **Two non-`str` inputs escape the guard entirely.** `expand_tilde` is called *outside* the `try` in the GLOB branch, so a non-`str` pattern raises from there: `match_pattern(GLOB, b'/a', '/a')` raises `TypeError` and `match_pattern(GLOB, 1, '/a')` raises `AttributeError` -- verified live 2026-08-11. These are type-hint violations and low severity on their own, but the function's `Returns:` block implies it never raises, and the `except` on the line below is there precisely to make that true. | `toolguard/patterns.py:match_pattern` (GLOB branch, the two `expand_tilde` calls) | Either move the `expand_tilde` calls inside the `try`, or accept that a type-hint violation is allowed to raise and say nothing. Low priority; recorded because it makes the `try`/`except` in item 16 look like it covers more than it does. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `auto_migrate.py` and `error_log.py`

**Five more rows for the "Code-level defects found during #07" table above.** Found during the comment sweep on `toolguard/auto_migrate.py` and `toolguard/error_log.py`, 2026-08-11, and all deliberately NOT fixed there (comments-only).

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| 18 | **`log_crash` can raise, contradicting its own "Never raises" docstring.** `errors_dir = Path.home() / ".toolguard" / "errors"` is evaluated *before* the `try`, and `Path.home()` raises `RuntimeError` when the home directory cannot be resolved. Verified live 2026-08-11 by patching `error_log.Path` so `Path.home()` raises: `log_crash` propagated `RuntimeError`. This matters because every call site is inside one of `hook.main`'s top-level `except` clauses, i.e. precisely the place that must not be made worse. | `toolguard/error_log.py:log_crash` (the `Path.home()` line, above the `try`) | One-line fix: move the `errors_dir` assignment inside the `try`. The "Never raises" sentence was removed during the sweep and replaced by a claim scoped to write failures only, so the comment is no longer false -- but the code is still one line away from being able to honour the stronger promise. |
| 19 | **`_log_entry`'s `log_dir.mkdir(...)` is outside its `try`, while the file write is inside it.** A failed write prints a warning and returns; a failed `mkdir` (unwritable parent, path exists as a file) propagates `OSError` out of `log_warning`/`log_error`/`log_conflict`. `hook.py` calls all three directly (lines 89, 91, 371, 397, 429, 880), not only through `error_reporter.Reporter._dispatch`, which does catch it. The asymmetry looks unintentional. | `toolguard/error_log.py:_log_entry` | Decide whether an unwritable log directory should be survivable at these call sites the way an unwritable log *file* already is. |
| 20 | **`auto_migrate` and `config_divergence` disagree on which patterns are ignored.** `run_auto_migration` uses `ignored_allow_patterns` **only** when `takeover_config['enabled']` is true (`ignored_patterns = []` otherwise); `check_and_warn_divergence` applies `ignored_allow_patterns` unconditionally and adds `additional_ignored_patterns` only when enabled. So with takeover disabled, a pattern can be filtered out of the divergence *warning* and still be picked up by the *migration* that the warning gates. | `toolguard/auto_migrate.py:94-97` vs `toolguard/config_divergence.py:245-250` | Two copies of one policy that have drifted. Fix is to share one helper; deciding which behaviour is correct is a product call. |
| 21 | **The `backup_dir` default literal is duplicated.** `auto_migrate` writes `config_sync.get("backup_dir", "logs/config-backups")` while `config._CONFIG_SYNC_DEFAULTS` already declares the same default -- and `permission_migration.migrate` has a third copy (`project_root / "logs" / "config-backups"` when `backup_dir is None`). Three homes for one default. | `toolguard/auto_migrate.py:81`, `toolguard/config.py:_CONFIG_SYNC_DEFAULTS`, `toolguard/permission_migration.py:migrate` | The `config_sync` dict handed to `run_auto_migration` always comes from the config module's resolver, so the local `.get` default is unreachable in production and can simply become `config_sync["backup_dir"]` -- or the literal can be imported from one place. |
| 22 | **`auto_migrate.load_config_sync_settings` has no production caller** -- only `test/unit/test_auto_migrate.py`. It is a one-line pass-through to `config.config_sync_settings_from_sources`; the live hook path reaches the settings via `Configuration.config_sync_settings()` instead. Same class as items 2/3/12. | `toolguard/auto_migrate.py:load_config_sync_settings` | Either dead code to delete (and drop the `config_sync_settings_from_sources` mention from `config.py`'s "still-public because auto_migrate uses them" list) or a missing call site. |

**Already recorded, not a new row:** the backtest table's defect 1 (`DECLINED_LOCKED` covering four `LockUnavailable` reasons while `auto_migrate` announces "Another migration is already running for this project") is confirmed by this pass. The comment that defended the consumed day's claim on that branch has been removed -- it was true only of the timeout reason -- but **the notice string itself is unchanged and still false for the other three reasons**, per the comments-only rule.

**Proposed `technical-notes.md` correction** (file off-limits to this pass): the log-stream table at line 377 describes the conflict log as *"Config conflicts (allow-over-deny overrides), human/LLM-readable, ON by default."* Two problems -- allow-over-deny is only one of the two things `log_conflict` records (`hook._log_takeover_enabled_conflict` writes cross-level `takeover_mode.enabled` disagreements to the same stream), and "ON by default" implies a switch that does not exist anywhere in the config surface. Proposed replacement cell: `Config conflicts, human/LLM-readable: allow-over-deny overrides and cross-level takeover_mode.enabled disagreements.`

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `normalization.py` (2026-08-11)

All five executed against the live module, 2026-08-11, during the pass-2 comment fixes. Comments-only pass -- none was fixed. Rows are lettered `N*` rather than numbered, because the numeric sequence in the tables above has already been claimed twice over by concurrent per-file passes. **The `project_root` existence asymmetry (row 13) and the four-step refactoring signal (R7) are the same file and are deliberately not repeated here.**

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| N1 | **The home directory itself normalizes to `~/.`** -- a form no permission rule would ever be written as. `Path('/home/arnon').relative_to(Path.home())` is `Path('.')`, which the f-string turns into `~/.`. Verified live: `normalize_path(str(Path.home()))` -> `'~/.'`, and `normalize_path('/home/arnon/')` -> `'~/.'` likewise. | `toolguard/normalization.py:normalize_path`, the home-collapse step | A pattern author writing `~` or `~/` for the home directory will not match the normalized variant. Fix is a special case for the empty relative part. |
| N2 | **A URL argument is turned into a relative path.** `normalize_command('curl http://example.com/x')` -> `'curl ./http://example.com/x'`: the token contains `/`, so it takes the path branch and gains a `./` prefix. Mitigated only because `permissions.match_command` also tries the raw, un-normalized command as a variant -- the normalized variant is simply junk that never matches. | `toolguard/normalization.py:normalize_command`, the `"/" in token` test | Related to but distinct from row 11 (which is the pattern-side `:`-split trap for URLs). Same authoring surface, opposite side of the match. |
| N3 | **Numeric arguments pass the file-extension heuristic.** `normalize_command('echo 1.5')` -> `'echo ./1.5'`; `normalize_command('echo version 3.14')` -> `'echo version ./3.14'`. The heuristic asks only for a short alphanumeric suffix after the last dot, and digits are alphanumeric. | `toolguard/normalization.py:normalize_command`, the `parts[1].isalnum()` test | Same mitigation and same severity as N2. A version number, a float, a port-free `1.2.3` -- all become `./`-prefixed. |
| N4 | **The symlink `for _ in range(3)` loop is dead beyond its first iteration, and the infinite loop it was written to prevent cannot occur.** Instrumented `Path.resolve` (2026-08-11): a 3-deep symlink chain resolves in **one** `resolve()` call, because `resolve()` already follows the whole chain; a link to a dangling target makes **zero** calls, because the `exists()` gate rejects it first. `resolve()` returns a still-symlink path only for a cycle, and a cycle's `exists()` is `False` (ELOOP), so the second iteration is unreachable in every case. Confirmed live: a two-link cycle makes zero `resolve()` calls and is returned unchanged. | `toolguard/normalization.py:normalize_path`, the symlink step | Reduces to a single `path_obj = path_obj.resolve()`. The false comment claiming the loop "rarely runs more than once" was removed in this pass rather than corrected -- so nothing in the file now explains or defends the loop. |
| N5 | **`normalize_command` flattens newlines into spaces**, because it is `command.split()` + `" ".join()`. Verified: `normalize_command('echo a\nb')` -> `'echo a b'`, and `normalize_command('echo  a   b')` -> `'echo a b'`. This means the normalized variant `permissions.match_command` builds has had its newlines silently removed. **Safe today only by adjacency**: `match_command` computes `command_has_newline` from the **raw** `command_str` before building the variants, and `continue`s past the DEFAULT branch on a newline -- so the de-newlined variant is never reached for a multi-line command. Nothing in `normalize_command` guards this, and nothing records the dependency. | `toolguard/normalization.py:normalize_command`; relied on by `toolguard/permissions.py:match_command` | Not a bug today; a latent fail-open if the newline guard is ever moved, relaxed, or computed from a variant instead of the raw string. Pairs with row 10 (which covers the REGEX/GLOB/NATIVE side of the same guard). The whitespace-collapse behaviour is now stated in `normalize_command`'s `Returns:` docstring, which it never was before. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toml_scan.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- none was fixed. Rows lettered `T*`, since the numeric sequence above has been claimed several times over by concurrent per-file passes. `toml_scan.py` contains no user-facing strings, so there are no false-string findings here.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| T1 | **`perm_type` is interpolated into a regex without `re.escape`**, while its sibling `_section_header_re` escapes `section_name` in the structurally identical position. `re.search(rf"(?m)^[ \t]*{perm_type}\s*=\s*\[", ...)`. Not a live bug -- the only call site passes the literals `"allow"`, `"deny"`, `"ask"` -- but the asymmetry is the kind a later caller trips over, and the parameter is not typed as a `Literal`. | `toolguard/toml_scan.py:_locate_subsection` | One-word fix (`re.escape(perm_type)`), or narrow the parameter to a `Literal["allow", "deny", "ask"]`. Recorded rather than fixed per the comments-only rule. |
| T2 | **`find_multiline_structured_entry_line` returns "first" only within its own scan order, not source order.** It iterates `("allow", "deny", "ask")` and returns on the first offender found, so with offending entries in more than one array it reports the `allow` one even when the `ask` one comes earlier in the file. Verified live 2026-08-11: a file whose `ask` array holds a multi-line entry starting at line 3 and whose `allow` array holds one starting at line 7 returns **7**. The pre-sweep docstring claimed "the first multi-line structured entry found in a whole TOML file's *text*", which is false; the docstring now states the scan order instead. | `toolguard/toml_scan.py:find_multiline_structured_entry_line`; message built in `toolguard/config.py:_multiline_structured_entry_diagnostic` | Cosmetic in effect -- any offending line is actionable and fixing it is necessary either way -- but the user is pointed at one of several offenders with nothing saying there are others. Decide whether to sort by position, or to report all of them. |
| T3 | **`split_array_elements` tracks `{`/`}` depth but not `[`/`]`**, unlike `_find_array_close` in the same module, which counts both into one depth. So a comma inside a bare nested array at the top level of a permission array splits it: `split_array_elements(' ["a", "b"], "c",')` yields three elements (`"a"`, `"b"`, `"c"`), verified live 2026-08-11. Harmless today -- permission arrays hold strings and inline tables, and a nested array only ever appears *inside* an inline table, where `brace_depth > 0` already protects it. | `toolguard/toml_scan.py:_ArrayScanState.brace_depth` / `_scan_array_char` | Latent, not a bug. The asymmetry with `_find_array_close` is now noted on the `brace_depth` field comment. Fix, if wanted, is to treat `[`/`]` the way `_find_array_close` already does. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `session_start.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- none was fixed. Rows are lettered `S*` because the numeric sequence above has been claimed several times over by concurrent per-file passes. **The broken-config banner overclaim is already row 14 above and is deliberately not repeated here** -- the pass-2 reviewer re-flagged it independently, which is corroboration, not a second finding.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1 | **The dynamic-conflict check can scan a directory nothing writes to, and the sweep removed its only trace.** `_detect_conflicts` computes `log_dir = config.project_root / "logs"`. The PreToolUse hook that *writes* those conflict logs takes `env_config.get("log_dir")` (`hook.py:77-82`), which honours `TOOLGUARD_LOG_DIR` (absolute or project-relative, then `.resolve()`d) and derives its default from `toolguard.env_config.find_project_root` -- **a different function** from `toolguard.config.find_project_root` (the first wraps `path_utils.resolve_project_root(strict=True)`, the second wraps `path_utils.require_project_root`). With `TOOLGUARD_LOG_DIR` set, or whenever the two root-finders disagree, session_start scans a directory nothing writes to and the dynamic-conflict nag silently never fires. The deleted comment "same logic as the PreToolUse hook" was false, which is why deleting it was right -- but the divergence itself is real and now has no trace in the code. | `toolguard/session_start.py:_detect_conflicts` vs `toolguard/hook.py:77-82` | Fix is to share one log-dir resolver between the writer and the reader, rather than to re-derive it. Silent failure mode: no error, no warning, just a nag that never fires. |
| S2 | **A takeover conflict can be double-reported in one summary, and the log-derived half does not self-clear.** `hook._log_takeover_enabled_conflict` (`hook.py:405-429`) writes the static `takeover_mode.enabled` disagreement into the *same* `toolguard-conflict-YYYY-MM-DD.md` file that `_check_dynamic_conflicts` counts. So one takeover conflict surfaces twice in a single session-start summary -- once as the live static-conflict bullet, once inside the conflict-log entry count. Worse, the static half is recomputed live and self-clears when the config is fixed, while the logged half persists in the file indefinitely. The module docstring previously described the conflict log as holding only "overrides that can only be detected at tool-use time", which hid both consequences; it now names both writers. | `toolguard/session_start.py:_check_dynamic_conflicts`, `toolguard/hook.py:_log_takeover_enabled_conflict` | Options: give takeover conflicts their own log stream, tag conflict entries by kind so session_start can exclude the ones it already detects live, or accept the duplication and say so. The non-self-clearing half is the part users will report as a bug. |
| S3 | **Return types are unannotated while the docstrings state precise shapes.** `_recent_conflict_logs` is annotated `-> list` with no element type (it returns `list[Path]`), and `_check_dynamic_conflicts`, `_detect_conflicts`, `_detect_broken_config_files` and `_detect_unrecognized_fallbacks` have no return annotation at all -- yet each docstring names an exact shape (`(relative_path_str, entry_count)`, `(static_conflict, dynamic_conflict)`, a tuple of `(path, message)` pairs, a tuple of `UnrecognizedFallbackSetting`). Nothing checks that the prose and the code agree. | `toolguard/session_start.py`, five function signatures | Annotating them would let pyright enforce what the docstrings currently assert on trust, and would let three of the five docstring `Returns:` blocks shrink or disappear. Code change, out of scope for the comments-only pass. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `config_divergence.py` / `config_validation.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- none of these was fixed. Rows are lettered `V*` because the numeric sequence above has now been claimed several times over by concurrent per-file passes.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| V1 | **A project that does not set `governed_tools` gets a spurious warning for every tool it does configure.** `Configuration.validation_issues` builds its merged view with `merged_config["governed_tools"] = []` and only ever appends what a layer explicitly declares, so when no layer declares any, `validate_permissions` sees an empty list and reports *`Tool "Bash" appears in permissions but is not in governed_tools list`* -- while `Configuration.governed_tools()` is meanwhile defaulting to `DEFAULT_GOVERNED_TOOLS` (`Bash`, `Read`, `Write`, `Edit`) and governing them. Verified end-to-end 2026-08-11 with `HOME` redirected to a temp dir and a project whose only config is `[permissions] allow = ["Bash(ls:*)", "Read(/tmp/**)"]`: `governed_tools() -> ('Bash','Read','Write','Edit')` **and** two warnings saying Bash and Read are not governed. | `toolguard/config.py:1689-1711` (the merged view) with `toolguard/config_validation.py:64-66` | The merged view should seed `governed_tools` with `DEFAULT_GOVERNED_TOOLS` when no layer declares any, matching `Configuration.governed_tools()`. Related: `validate_permissions`' own `config.get("governed_tools", ["Bash"])` fallback is both stale (it predates the four-tool default) and unreachable from that caller, since the key is always present. |
| V2 | **`get_native_permissions` raises on a settings file whose top level is not a JSON object.** The `except` around `json.load` does not cover the `config.get("permissions", {})` that follows, so a `settings.local.json` containing `[]` propagates `AttributeError: 'list' object has no attribute 'get'` out of the function -- and hence out of `check_and_warn_divergence`, on the live hook path. Verified live 2026-08-11. Malformed JSON, a missing file and an unreadable file are all handled; a well-formed non-object is not. | `toolguard/config_divergence.py:get_native_permissions` | One-line fix: treat a non-`dict` payload the same as an unparseable file (warn and return the three empty lists). |
| V3 | **`except (json.JSONDecodeError, IOError, Exception)` is a tuple whose first two members are unreachable.** `Exception` is a base of both, so the tuple is exactly `except Exception`. Not a behaviour bug -- flagged because the tuple reads as a deliberate, narrow catch and is not one, and because the docstring wording I wrote for the `Returns:` block had to be checked against `Exception`, not against the two names in front of it. | `toolguard/config_divergence.py:44` | Either narrow it to `(json.JSONDecodeError, OSError)` and let anything else propagate, or write `except Exception` and mean it. |

### Comment defects in files this pass was not allowed to touch

| where | defect |
|---|---|
| `toolguard/hook.py:_run_divergence_check` (docstring) | Claims *"writing to the structured error log itself would make a config-layer module depend on `toolguard.error_log`, which sits in the runtime layer."* **`error_log` is no longer in the runtime layer.** TOO-45 moved it to `observability` (`.pyscn.toml:176-202`), which sits **below** `config` in the declared order `foundation <- observability <- config <- engine <- api <- runtime <- tooling <- support`, so `config_divergence -> error_log` would be a perfectly legal downward import today. `config_divergence` already imports `toolguard.error_reporter`, another observability module. The identical claim appeared twice in `config_divergence.py` (module docstring and `DivergenceCheckResult`) and was deleted there rather than corrected, per rule 0. The arrangement itself may still be worth keeping for other reasons -- but the stated reason is false, so either supply the real one or drop the justification. |
| `toolguard/config.py:validation_issues` (docstring) | *"Returns: Tuple of `Issue`, in the detection order shown above"* is false for the last two bullets. Detections 8 and 9 (unsupported tool / tool absent from `governed_tools`) both come out of `validate_permissions`' single `for tool in tools_in_permissions` loop over a **`set`**, so they interleave in `str`-hash order. Verified live 2026-08-11 across `PYTHONHASHSEED` 0-5 with three tools: the two categories appeared in three different orders, including an "ungoverned" issue ahead of both "unsupported" ones. Only the coarse ordering (parse failures first, permission validation last) holds. |

### Refactoring candidate

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| R12 | `config_validation.validate_permissions` | Weak signal, recorded for completeness. The pre-sweep docstring numbered four checks, and the body carried three multi-line comment blocks over ~55 lines. Unlike R1/R9 the enumeration did not mirror the branch structure -- two of the four items are simply whatever `normalize_entry` reports, and the body is really two phases (collect tools + entry issues; then judge each tool). | Candidate: name the two phases (`_collect_tools`, `_tool_issues`) if it is touched for another reason. Not worth a dedicated change on its own. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `once_per.py` / `file_lock.py` / `env_config.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- none was fixed. Rows lettered `E*`. No false *strings* were found in these three files; the two rows below are typing/reachability findings tripped over while verifying docstrings.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| E1 | **`get_env_config` is annotated `-> Dict[str, any]`** -- the builtin `any()` function used where `typing.Any` was meant. Valid at runtime (annotations are not evaluated as types here) but meaningless to a checker, and it silently defeats any type information the return value could carry. `Optional` and `Dict` are already imported from `typing` in the same file; `Any` is not. | `toolguard/env_config.py:get_env_config` | One-word fix (`Any`, plus the import). Left alone per the comments-only rule; the docstring now spells out the dict's keys and value types instead, which is all a reader currently gets. |
| E2 | **`OncePer._maybe_sweep`'s `or project is None` guard is unreachable.** `_maybe_sweep` is only called when `_claim` saw `ClaimStatus.CLAIMED`, and `once_per_store.claim` returns `UNGUARANTEED(_REASON_NO_PROJECT)` for a `None` project, so a `None` project can never reach it. Harmless defensive code; recorded because no comment was added to explain a condition that can never fire. | `toolguard/once_per.py:_maybe_sweep` | Either delete the clause or leave it as belt-and-braces -- but it should not need a comment either way. |

### Comment defect in a file outside this pass's scope

`toolguard/session_warnings.py`'s module docstring is entirely change history: it explains that "the once-per-day throttling facade that used to live here moved to `toolguard.once_per` (TOO-45 punch-list #01, conceptual overhaul, second pass)" and then describes `once_per`'s internal housekeeping behaviour. Both are exactly what the #07 standard's rule 0 (claims reaching outside the file) and "Cut -- change history in any form" call for removing; the module's actual subject, the takeover notice, gets one line. Found while verifying `once_per.py`'s claims about who throttles what, 2026-08-11. `session_warnings.py` was not in scope for this pass. (Distinct from row 8 in the table above, which is about the *string* that module prints.)

All five comments were cut as restatement during the #07 pass, 2026-08-11, but the shape they were labelling is unchanged. | Candidate: `_resolve_project_root(start_dir)` and `_resolve_log_dir(project_root, env_vars)` as private steps, leaving `get_env_config` as a short assembly of the returned dict. ## Code-level defects found during #07 on `rule_sort.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- nothing below was fixed. Rows lettered `S*` to avoid colliding with the concurrently-numbered tables above.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| S1 | **A genuine `None` entry round-trips into TOML as the *string* `"None"`.** `_render_toml_scalar`'s final fallback stringifies-and-quotes anything it does not recognise, and `None` reaches it: a JSON `toolguard_hook.json` may hold a literal `null` in `permissions.allow`, `normalize_entries_preserving` deliberately preserves that element as `RuleEntry(raw=None, synthesized_pattern=True)`, `to_source()` returns `None`, and `render_toml_entry` emits `"None"`. Executed 2026-08-11: `render_toml_entry(RuleEntry(pattern="Bash(x)", raw=None))` -> `'"None"'`. This is the exact corruption `rule_entry._UNSET` exists to prevent one level up ("a genuine `raw=None` would render as the literal string `\"None\"`, silently corrupting the write") -- the sentinel keeps `None` distinguishable from unset, and then the renderer collapses the distinction anyway. `42`/`True`/`["a", 1]` all render correctly; `None` is the one wrong case, because TOML has no null. | `toolguard/rule_sort.py:_render_toml_scalar` (final `return`), reached via `render_toml_entry` | Not a false string (no literal involved) and not fixable inside a comments-only pass. Decide what a JSON `null` element should become on a JSON->TOML migration: dropping it, refusing the write, or an explicit error beats writing a rule named `"None"`. The renderer's docstring now states the behaviour plainly rather than implying every shape is handled correctly. |
| S2 | **Emptying a permission sub-list silently discards every comment attached to it.** `reassemble_permissions_section` does `if not entries: continue` before touching `parsed_structure[perm_type]`, so a caller that writes back `{"allow": []}` for a file whose `allow` list carried top, per-rule and bottom comment blocks loses all of them along with the `allow = [` line. Executed 2026-08-11: reassembling `allow = ["Bash(a)"]` + a trailing comment with `{"allow": []}` returns `'[permissions]\n# note\n'`. Arguably intended (an empty list should write nothing), but it is the same comment-loss class the trailing-comment capture was added to close, and nothing records the trade. | `toolguard/rule_sort.py:reassemble_permissions_section`, the `if not entries: continue` guard | Decide and record: either keep the comments as a bare `allow = [ ... ]` block, or accept the loss deliberately. The `Returns:` docstring now states the behaviour so it is at least visible. |

### Comment defects in files outside this pass's scope

* `toolguard/toml_scan.py`'s module docstring calls `rule_sort.py` "a ~1300-line tooling module". It is **964 lines** (2026-08-11, pre-sweep) -- the number is stale and, per rule 1, a line count is not something a docstring should be carrying at all.
* `toolguard/tools/config_access.py:494` defines `_split_tool_pattern`; `rule_sort.SyntheticPattern`'s pre-sweep docstring named it as an example of a consumer's string operations. That cross-file reference was cut here, but it is worth knowing the pattern existed: a private function in another module was being used as documentation of this one's contract.

### Refactoring candidate surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| S-R1 | `rule_sort.reassemble_permissions_section` | ~90-line straight-line body with four inline comment blocks, each labelling a distinct phase of one loop iteration: classify parsed items into top/bottom/per-rule comment anchors; key the new entries by `(pattern, occurrence)`; sort; emit (choosing original line vs. fresh render). The docstring correspondingly carries two named sections ("Round-trip guarantee", "Duplicate-pattern keying") describing two of those phases. Same "comments cluster over a sequence of concerns" signal as R4/R5/R11. Found during the #07 comment sweep, 2026-08-11. | Candidate: `_classify_parsed_items(parsed_items)` returning the top/bottom/`rule_comments`/`rule_lines` bundle, `_key_entries(entries)` returning the `(entry, key)` pairs, and `_emit_subsection(...)`, leaving the public function as a loop over the three perm types plus the trailing-comment append. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `rule_entry.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- none of these was fixed. Rows lettered `D*` because the numeric sequence above has been claimed several times over by concurrent per-file passes. The doc-drift items from pass 1 on this file (the stale `technical-notes.md` "Single source of truth for tool-wrapper stripping" section, and R9/R10) are in the earlier `rule_entry.py` section and are not repeated here.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| D1 | **A newline-bearing *plain* pattern is accepted with no issue, scoped to its tool, and permanently inert -- silently.** Executed live 2026-08-11: `normalize_entry("Bash(a)\nEvil(b)", is_native=False)` returns a `RuleEntry` with **zero issues**; `entries_for_tool((entry,), "Bash")` **keeps** it (it starts with `Bash(` and ends with `)`); `entry.stripped_pattern` is `'Bash(a)\nEvil(b)'` -- the wrapper is *not* stripped, because `_TOOL_WRAPPER_RE.fullmatch` correctly refuses to span the newline. The rule then matches nothing. **There is no bypass**: `permissions.py:111-125` independently excludes newline-bearing commands from DEFAULT matching. The defect is the **silence**. A structured entry with the same value is rejected with an `error`; the plain-string form is accepted without a whisper. An author who writes `"Bash(rm -rf /)\nBash(dd *)"` into a **deny** list gets a rule that is accepted, scoped, displayed as configured, and enforces nothing. An inert `allow` is harmless; an inert `deny` is a rule the operator believes is in force. | `toolguard/rule_entry.py:normalize_entry` (plain-string branch) | Suggested fix for a ticket: emit a `warning`-level `Issue` for a plain string containing `\n` or `\r`, mirroring the `error` a structured entry already gets. Not fixed here -- adding an `Issue` is a code change. |
| D2 | **`RuleEntry.is_structured` has no production consumer.** Grep 2026-08-11 across `toolguard/`, `tools/` and `test/`: the only readers are `test/unit/test_rule_entry.py:121,730,731`. Public API with zero non-test callers -- and `merge_entries` deliberately does *not* use it (it discriminates on `bool(entry.metadata)` instead, because a union-merge result records no `raw`), which needed its own inline comment to explain. Same class as items 2/3/12/22 above. | `toolguard/rule_entry.py:is_structured` | Either delete it with its tests, or find the intended consumer. If it stays, the inline comment in `merge_entries` explaining why the code avoids it stays too. |
| D3 | **Cosmetic: `_additional_context_issues` is annotated `-> tuple`** with no element type, while the rest of the module uses `Tuple[...]` throughout (`Tuple[Issue, ...]` is what it actually returns). | `toolguard/rule_entry.py:_additional_context_issues` | One-word fix. The sibling half of this row -- the docstring's single-backtick `` `deny` `` where the file uses double backticks (reST renders that as emphasis, not a literal) -- **was** in scope and was fixed during this pass; only the annotation remains. |
| D4 | **`config_types.py:15` imports the private `_strip_tool_wrapper` across a module boundary** (`from toolguard.rule_entry import RuleEntry, _strip_tool_wrapper`) and uses it at `config_types.py:328`, on exactly the bare-pattern-string case the public `strip_tool_wrapper` exists to serve. The two genuine public callers (`toolguard/tools/takeover_audit.py:243,274`) do go through the public name. Found while checking whether `strip_tool_wrapper`'s docstring directive ("use this for a pattern that never went through a `RuleEntry`") was honoured -- it is not, so the directive was cut rather than corrected. | `toolguard/config_types.py:15,328` | Re-point the import at the public `strip_tool_wrapper`. Note this is the same private-import class R6 already reasoned about for `takeover_audit` (see [[r6-reassessment]]), on a different consumer. |

**Strengthening R9 rather than a new row** (do not double-count): R9 already flags `merge_entries` for its three numbered cases. Worth adding to it -- the signal is broader than the numbering. The function is **148 lines** and *seven* distinct branches each carry their own comment block: grouping, the dedup fast path, structured selection, case 1, the case-2/3 decision, conflict emission, and the union merge. That is the same shape as `compound.judge_unit` (R2), which Arnon caught from the shape alone; those seven comments would read as the docstrings of seven extracted functions. The three-case split in R9 is the *minimum* version of the fix, not the whole of it.

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `install_update.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- nothing below was fixed. Rows are lettered `IU*` because the numeric sequence and the `S`/`V`/`E`/`D` letters have each been claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Rows 18 and 19 in the numbered table above came from **pass 1 on this same file**; they are cross-referenced below rather than repeated. Line numbers are post-sweep.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| IU1 | **`_read_direct_url_json` can return a non-dict and crash `detect_install` with an uncaught `AttributeError`.** The function's only shape guard is `json.loads` succeeding, so valid JSON that is not an object -- `[1, 2]`, `"x"`, `3` -- is returned as-is. `detect_install` then tests `data is not None` (true) and immediately calls `data.get("url", "")`, which raises. The `except ValueError, TypeError:` around `json.loads` catches *parse* failures, not *shape* failures, so nothing downstream absorbs it. A `direct_url.json` whose top level is a JSON array is admittedly not a shape pip or uv writes, but the function is documented as returning "parsed JSON", not "a dict", and the caller assumes a dict. | `toolguard/install_update.py:_read_direct_url_json` (the `return json.loads(raw)`), consumed at `detect_install:191` | One-line fix: `if not isinstance(data, dict): return InstallInfo(kind=InstallKind.UNKNOWN)` at the top of the `data is not None` branch (line 190), or narrow `_read_direct_url_json` itself to return `None` for a non-dict payload -- which also makes its `Optional[dict]` annotation true. Same class as V2 (`get_native_permissions` raising on a non-object settings file). |
| IU2 | **`_check_local` re-runs `git rev-parse HEAD` for a value it already has, and `InstallInfo.installed_commit` is write-only for LOCAL.** `detect_install` calls `local_repo_head(repo_path)` on both LOCAL paths and stores the result as `info.installed_commit` (lines 208/212 and 225/228). `_check_local:325` then calls `local_repo_head(repo)` again and never reads `info.installed_commit`. Cost is one redundant git subprocess per local-install check; the subtler cost is a documented dataclass field that no production code reads on the only kind that populates it, so a reader cannot tell which of the two values is authoritative. | `toolguard/install_update.py:detect_install` (both LOCAL branches) vs `_check_local:325` | Either read `info.installed_commit` in `_check_local` and keep the field, or stop populating it for LOCAL and let `_check_local` own the lookup. Note the two are not quite interchangeable today: re-reading HEAD gives `_check_local` its own "could not read HEAD from this checkout" error path, which the stored value would have to reproduce. |
| IU3 | **Cross-reference, do not double-count: row 18 above** -- `_check_local`'s non-editable branch is missing a `\n`, so the reinstall alternative renders as a trailing shell comment on the install command (`  uv tool install --force /path  # or: uv tool upgrade toolguard --reinstall`). Confirmed again during pass 2. Worth adding to row 18: the defect is **unguarded by the tests** -- `test/unit/test_update_check.py` asserts only `assertIn("uv tool install --force", out)`, which passes either way, so fixing the string will not break a test and nothing would have caught the regression. | `toolguard/install_update.py`, `_check_local` non-editable branch | String defect, out of scope for a comments-only pass. |
| IU4 | **Cross-reference, do not double-count: row 19 above** (`installed_origin` has zero non-test callers; re-confirmed by grep across `toolguard/`, `tools/` and `docs/`, 2026-08-11). New in pass 2: **`test/unit/test_update_check.py:772` carries a false comment** -- `# Legacy shim test: installed_origin is still used by some existing callers.` There are no such callers, in or out of the repo. Also fixed in-file during this pass: the docstring's `.. deprecated::` directive carried no version argument, which is not valid reST; it is now plain `Deprecated: superseded by ...` prose. | `test/unit/test_update_check.py:772`; `toolguard/install_update.py:installed_origin` | The test tree was out of scope for this pass. If `installed_origin` is deleted, that comment and its test class go with it; if it stays, the comment is simply wrong and should go. |

### Documentation drift, for `/documentation-review`

* **`docs/configuration.md:989-990` is now the only place stating the 0/1/2 exit-code contract without the `--upgrade` caveat.** It says flatly *"Exit codes: `0` = up to date, `1` = update available, `2` = could not determine"*. With `--upgrade` on a git install that is behind, `_check_git` returns whatever `run_upgrade` returned -- `uv`'s own exit code -- so the process can exit with a code outside that set. The module docstring stated this before the sweep and states it more precisely after it; the user-facing doc is now the one that is wrong, which inverts the usual direction of this defect.
* **`docs/architecture.md:36` lists only `update_check.py` in the package tree.** `install_update.py`, which holds all of this logic after the R5c split, and `install_provenance.py` do not appear at all -- so the tree names the thin CLI entry point and omits the two modules a reader would need to open.

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `error_log.py` / `auto_migrate.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows lettered `EL*` because the numeric sequence above has been claimed several times over by concurrent per-file passes. **EL1 and EL2 sharpen rows 18 and 19 of the numbered table above rather than replacing them** (pass 1 recorded the raise; pass 2 establishes what it costs at the call sites). **EL3 sharpens row 20.** Every claim below was executed live, 2026-08-11.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **EL1** | **HIGHEST SEVERITY IN THIS QUEUE -- a fail-open in the hook.** `log_crash` evaluates `errors_dir = Path.home() / ".toolguard" / "errors"` *above* its `try` (`error_log.py:139-140`), so a `RuntimeError` from `Path.home()` propagates. Its three call sites are `hook.py`'s three top-level `except` clauses (lines 1311, 1322, 1335), and in each one `log_crash` runs **before** `_report_crash_fault` and `_emit_decision`. An exception escaping it therefore skips both, and the hook exits **without emitting a decision on stdout** -- precisely the fail-open those handlers exist to prevent (each carries its own comment saying the deny "must reach Claude on stdout"). Verified live by patching `error_log.Path` so `home()` raises: `log_crash` propagated `RuntimeError` with nothing on stderr. | `toolguard/error_log.py:139-140`, reached from `toolguard/hook.py:1311,1322,1335` | **One-line fix: move the two `errors_dir` lines inside the `try`.** The severity is not in `log_crash` -- it is that the one function guaranteed to run on every crash path is the one function on that path that can raise. Supersedes row 18's "one line away from honouring the stronger promise" framing: the promise is not cosmetic, it is what keeps the hook fail-closed. |
| **EL2** | **Same shape one level down, at `_log_entry`.** `log_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)` is the first statement of the body (`error_log.py:88`), outside the `try` that wraps the write and twelve lines above the stderr echo. Executed live: an uncreatable `log_dir` raises `NotADirectoryError`/`PermissionError` out of `log_warning` **with nothing echoed to stderr**, while a failed *write* is swallowed and does echo. `hook.py` imports `log_warning`/`log_error`/`log_conflict` directly (`hook.py:36`) and calls them at lines 89, 91, 371, 397, 429 and 880, bypassing `error_reporter.Reporter._dispatch`'s catch -- so an unwritable log directory raises into the same top-level handlers as EL1. | `toolguard/error_log.py:_log_entry` | Fix with EL1: the asymmetry (directory creation outside the `try`, file write inside it) is the same defect in both functions. `_log_entry`'s docstring now states the asymmetry plainly rather than reassuring the reader that the stderr echo always survives. |
| **EL3** | **`auto_migrate` and `config_divergence` disagree on ignored patterns, and the disagreement is reachable on the DEFAULT path.** `config_divergence.check_and_warn_divergence` (lines 193-197) applies `ignored_allow_patterns` **always**, adding `additional_ignored_patterns` only when takeover is enabled; `auto_migrate.run_auto_migration` (lines 91-95) uses `[]` unless takeover is `enabled`. `hook._run_divergence_check` (876-884) runs the first, then gates the second on `divergence.divergent_patterns` being non-empty. **With takeover disabled -- the default -- a pattern listed in `ignored_allow_patterns` is excluded from the warning, but if any *other* pattern is divergent the gate opens and `run_auto_migration` recomputes divergence without the ignore list, migrating the ignored pattern too, with no warning ever naming it.** The user opted a pattern out of the nag and got it silently migrated by the very migration that nag gates. | `toolguard/auto_migrate.py:91-95` vs `toolguard/config_divergence.py:193-197`, gated at `toolguard/hook.py:876-884` | Sharpens row 20, which recorded the drift but not that it is reachable with takeover off. Fix direction: **one shared helper builds `ignored_patterns` for both call sites**, so the two can no longer disagree; which behaviour is correct is still a product call. |
| **EL4** | **`auto_migrate.py:129-130`'s notice string is false on three of four paths.** *"Another migration is already running for this project; skipping."* is true only of `REASON_TIMEOUT`. `migrate()` itself branches on the four `LockUnavailable` reasons correctly (`permission_migration.py:1251-1261`); `auto_migrate` collapses them into one message. | `toolguard/auto_migrate.py:129-130` | Strings are code -- not edited. Same finding as back-test row 1 and the "already recorded" note under the pass-1 section; repeated here only because the exact line numbers moved. The *comment* that defended the consumed day's claim on this branch was already removed in pass 1. |
| **EL5** | **`once_per.Repeat.UNSAFE`'s own docstring is now the surviving home of a stale claim.** It reads *"Repeating the action is unsafe (concurrent, uncoordinated writers)."* `auto_migrate.py:144` is the **only** `Repeat.UNSAFE` call site in the package, and since punch-list item 15 that writer is coordinated by an OS file lock -- a second process gets `DECLINED_LOCKED`, not a concurrent write. Pass 1 correctly deleted the same justification from `auto_migrate.py` (per the standard: delete an unsupported justification, never invent a replacement); the claim survives at the enum. | `toolguard/once_per.py`, `Repeat.UNSAFE` | Recorded, not edited -- `once_per.py` is being swept separately. Note the *behaviour* `UNSAFE` selects is still wanted and is now stated at `run_auto_migration` ("when the once-per-day guarantee itself cannot be verified, the migration is skipped rather than run"); only the parenthetical reason is stale. Whoever sweeps `once_per.py` should delete the parenthetical rather than supply a new reason. |
| **EL6** | **`config_divergence.py:44`: `except (json.JSONDecodeError, IOError, Exception)` is exactly `except Exception`.** | `toolguard/config_divergence.py:44` | **Duplicate of V3 above** -- re-found independently by this pass, which is corroboration, not a second finding. Do not double-count. |
| **EL7** | **`auto_migrate.load_config_sync_settings` adds no behaviour at all.** It is a one-line pass-through to `config.config_sync_settings_from_sources` with no production caller (row 22 above). Worth asking not just "who calls it" but "why does it exist" -- it does not adapt, default, validate or rename anything. Its 11-line docstring, whose `Returns:` block restated the callee's own, was cut to a single line in this pass, which makes the emptiness visible. | `toolguard/auto_migrate.py:load_config_sync_settings` | Sharpens row 22: the answer is probably deletion rather than finding it a caller, since any caller would be better off calling `config_sync_settings_from_sources` directly. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `once_per.py` / `file_lock.py` / `env_config.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows lettered `OP*`; the numeric sequence and the `E`/`S`/`V`/`D`/`EL`/`IU` letters have each already been claimed by concurrent per-file passes. **OP1, OP2 and OP6 are cross-references to rows recorded elsewhere, re-confirmed by execution here -- corroboration, not new findings. Do not double-count.** Every claim below was executed live, 2026-08-11.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **OP1** | **Cross-reference, do not double-count: row E1 above.** `get_env_config` is annotated `-> Dict[str, any]` -- the **builtin** `any`, not `typing.Any`. Re-confirmed by execution rather than by reading: `get_env_config.__annotations__["return"].__args__[1] is any` -> `True`, and the annotation reprs as `typing.Dict[str, <built-in function any>]`. Worth adding to E1: `typing.Dict[str, any]` does **not** raise at subscription time, so nothing catches it at import, and `uv run ruff check .` passes clean over the file -- there is no automated signal for this class of typo anywhere in the toolchain today. | `toolguard/env_config.py:132` | Fix is `Dict[str, Any]`. `Any` is **not** currently imported in this file (`from typing import Dict, Optional`), so the fix is two lines, not one. |
| **OP2** | **Cross-reference, do not double-count: row E2 above.** `_maybe_sweep`'s `or project is None` is unreachable **twice over**, not once: `once_per_store.claim` returns `UNGUARANTEED(_REASON_NO_PROJECT)` for a `None` project (`once_per_store.py:361-362`), so the status check on the preceding line already short-circuits; **and** `_maybe_sweep` is only reached when the *outer* claim returned `CLAIMED`, which itself requires a non-`None` project. Either guard alone makes the clause dead. | `toolguard/once_per.py:176` | Unchanged recommendation: delete the clause or leave it as belt-and-braces, but it should not acquire a comment. |
| **OP3** | **`file_lock.exclusive`'s `os.close(fd)` is unguarded in the outer `finally`.** An `OSError` from the close escapes `exclusive` as a plain `OSError`, not as `LockUnavailable` -- so the docstring's `Raises:` block and the class docstring's "one exception type ... a caller that must not proceed catches one thing" are both defeated on that path. **Contract-only, no correctness impact**: it fires *after* `release(fd)` has already run and after the `yield` has returned, so the lock is released and the caller's critical section has completed. Left as-is deliberately; the surrounding docstring wording was not weakened to accommodate it, because doing so would trade a true-in-practice statement for a hedge nobody needs. | `toolguard/file_lock.py:165` (the outer `finally`) | Lowest severity in this section. If touched: wrap the close in `try/except OSError: pass`, matching `_release_windows`'s existing shape in the same file. Not worth a ticket on its own -- fold into any other `file_lock` change. |
| **OP4** | **`file_lock`'s "foundation layer: stdlib only, no toolguard imports" property is enforced by nothing, and the prose asserting it was deleted rather than restored.** Pass 1 cut a module-docstring line making that claim. **It should become an architecture-fitness rule, not restored prose.** Verified 2026-08-11: `grep 'file_lock\|foundation' tools/architecture_fitness.py test/unit/test_architecture.py docs/architecture.md` returns **zero hits in all three files** -- the module is not named in the fitness tooling, the architecture tests, or the architecture doc. The property is currently true (`file_lock.py` imports only `os`, `time`, `contextlib`, `pathlib`, `typing`, `fcntl`, `msvcrt`) and guards **no demonstrated cycle** -- nothing has ever tried to import toolguard from it. | `toolguard/file_lock.py` (property), `tools/architecture_fitness.py` / `test/unit/test_architecture.py` (the missing enforcement) | A comment asserting an invariant nothing checks is exactly the shape that rots. Either add a fitness check that `file_lock` (and any other declared foundation leaf) imports nothing from `toolguard`, or accept that the property is incidental and say nothing anywhere. Do **not** re-add the prose. |
| **OP5** | **Retirement item, recorded because a dangerous version of it was deleted from `once_per.py`.** `once_per_store._LEGACY_MARKER_PREFIXES`, `_LEGACY_PROJECT_DB_FILENAME` and `_LEGACY_STORE_FILENAME` (and the three `reap()` sweep blocks that consume them) can be deleted once no pre-upgrade marker file can plausibly remain on any machine. **That is where deletion is safe.** `once_per._maybe_sweep` is **not**: pass 1 correctly deleted a TRANSITIONAL instruction there reading *"Delete this whole path once enough time has passed"*, which was actively wrong -- `reap()` performs the expired-claim `DELETE` **and** the legacy sweep in one function with one call site, so following that instruction literally would remove the claim-table cleanup and let the store grow without bound, silently. The retirement intent belongs against the `once_per_store` legacy constants, which is why it is recorded here. **Do not restore it to `once_per.py`.** | `toolguard/once_per_store.py:78,83,89` (constants) and `reap()`'s three legacy blocks | Pairs with R3 above (which proposes splitting `reap` into four named sweeps) -- doing R3 first makes this deletion a matter of removing three of the four functions rather than editing one body, and removes the trap that produced the deleted instruction. |
| **OP6** | **Cross-reference, do not double-count: rows V3 and EL6 above.** `config_divergence.py:43`'s `except (json.JSONDecodeError, IOError, Exception)` is exactly `except Exception`. Independently re-found by this pass while checking whether `env_config.load_env_file`'s own bare `except Exception` had a narrower sibling elsewhere. Third independent sighting; the finding is stable, the fix is still open. | `toolguard/config_divergence.py:43` | No new recommendation. |
| **OP7** | **`_Period`'s `scope` parameter takes a context argument that no scope function uses.** `_Period.scope` is typed `Callable[[Any], str]` and every call passes `context` through (`self._period._scope(context)`, four sites), but the only period constructed is `day = _Period("day", lambda context: once_per_store.day_scope(), ...)`, whose lambda ignores its parameter -- and every production caller leaves `context` at its `None` default. A five-line docstring paragraph defending the shape against a hypothetical future session-scoped period was cut in this pass (arguing with a hypothetical maintainer; the standard's "Cut" list). Recorded here so the code question survives the prose. | `toolguard/once_per.py:_Period`, `day` | Either simplify to a zero-arg `Callable[[], str]` and drop `context` from the four call sites and the three public method signatures, or keep the parameter and let CC-1 (per-session budgets, above) settle it -- CC-1 is the experiment that would actually need a session-scoped period. Note the public methods' `context` parameter is part of `OncePer`'s signature, so this is not a purely internal simplification. |

**Refuted, recorded so it is not re-proposed:** a reviewer proposed adding a hazard note on `day` warning that its `timedelta(days=7)` retention must exceed the period, and that someone "tidying" it to `days=1` to match the daily period would silently break the throttle. **Measured 2026-08-11 and the specific hazard is FALSE.** The true invariant is *retention >= the period's duration*, and `timedelta(days=1)` satisfies it exactly: `expires_at` is computed from `now` (`once_per_store.py:364`), the scope is a calendar day (`day_scope`), and any retry inside the same calendar day is strictly earlier than `claim_time + 24h`, so the `ON CONFLICT ... WHERE claims.expires_at < excluded.created_at` guard never re-fires. Executed: a ttl **shorter** than the window does re-grant the claim inside its own window (`claimed -> claimed` with `ttl=0.15s`), and `days=7` does not (`claimed -> held_by_someone_else`); a boundary sweep over claim times from `00:00:00.000000` to `23:59:59.999999` shows `days=7` and `days=1` never reclaim, while `23h59m` and `1h` always can.
