| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **UR1** | **The SECOND leg of the `allow`-not-`ask` argument is false too, and INS5 does not cover it.** The docstring justified `allow` with, verbatim, *"by the time any of these would fire, the user has already given explicit, global consent by starting a guided uninstall conversation -- the meaningful consent moment is 'yes, uninstall toolguard,' not each individual step of carrying that out."* Nothing scopes these grants to an uninstall conversation: `cmd_seed_self_perms` appends them to `[permissions] allow` in `toolguard_hook.toml` at **install** time (`installer.py:831-834` builds the candidates, `:848` appends them), where they are standing rules live in every session. `Bash(cd:*)` in particular fires on ordinary navigation all day, which is exactly what the module's own `cd` rationale string says it is for -- the two sentences contradict each other in one docstring. **Deleted, not replaced** (per the sweep's leave-the-gap rule); recorded here so it is not re-introduced. | `toolguard/tools/uninstall_readiness.py` module docstring (was "Design guard-rails" bullet 1) | With UR1 and INS5 both gone, the surviving argument for `allow` over `ask` is only the observed ask-does-not-prompt failure -- which is real (Arnon's own commit `4215208` and `docs/uninstall.md` Step 2 both state it) but is now the whole case. Worth deciding whether that alone still justifies `allow` for the four `rm`/`uv tool uninstall` entries, or whether ticket 18's fix should land first and the entries then move to `ask`. |
| **UR2** | **The self-permission table omits `toolguard-install`, which a bundled skill runs via `Bash`.** `skills/toolguard-maintenance/SKILL.md:72` runs `toolguard-install skills-status --format json` as its **pre-flight, before pass 1**, and `:83` offers `toolguard-install install-skills`. Neither is in `_SELF_PERMISSIONS`, so `missing_self_permissions()` never reports it and `seed-self-perms` never seeds a rule for it -- under takeover the skill's own first command falls to `no_match_fallback` like any unmatched command. This is the same chicken-and-egg bootstrap the module's opening paragraph describes, in the one command that runs before anything else. The module docstring's claim to be *"the SINGLE SOURCE OF TRUTH for which toolguard tools a skill runs via `Bash`"* was false for exactly this reason and was **deleted** in this pass. | `toolguard/tools/self_permission.py:_SELF_PERMISSIONS` | Not a one-line add: `toolguard-install` is *mutating* (`write-config`, `register-hooks`, `install-skills`, `seed-*` all write), so by the module's own risk rule it would be an `ask` entry -- and a pre-flight that prompts every run is its own problem. Options: an `ask` entry; a narrower pattern for the read-only subcommands only (`toolguard-install skills-status:*`), which the DEFAULT prefix matcher supports; or a decision that the pre-flight is expected to prompt. Needs Arnon. |
| **UR3** | **`docs/uninstall.md` Step 2 carries the refuted defence in user-facing prose.** Lines 59-63: *"these are narrow, single-purpose, literal patterns, seeded as `allow` (not `ask`) rather than gated behind a prompt, because by the time you are running this file the user has already given consent by asking for the uninstall in the first place"*. Both halves are the claims INS5 and UR1 refute, addressed to the agent performing a teardown. `docs/` is frozen for this sweep. | `docs/uninstall.md:59-63` | Fix with INS5/UR1 and ticket 18 in one pass, so the module docstring, the help string and the runbook stop disagreeing. |
| **UR4** | **`_SEED_SELF_PERMS_HELP` calls `required_self_permissions()` "the single source of truth" for the rules it seeds, in the function that proves otherwise.** `cmd_seed_self_perms` assembles candidates from FOUR sources: `required_self_permissions()`, `FILE_KIND_TOOLS` composed into `Read/Write/Edit(~/.toolguard/**)`, the locally-declared `_UV_BIN_PATH_PREPEND_ALLOW`, and `required_uninstall_readiness_permissions()` -- plus `required_self_integrity_hard_deny_patterns()` for `[hard_deny]`. The help text does enumerate all of them, so it is not misleading about *what* is written; the "single source of truth" label is what is wrong, and it is applied twice (once for self-permissions, once for uninstall readiness). | `toolguard/tools/installer.py:_SEED_SELF_PERMS_HELP:737-751` | Strings are code; not edited. The equivalent claim in `self_permission.py`'s docstring was deleted this pass (see UR2), so the two now disagree until this is reworded. Drop the label, keep the enumeration. |
| **UR5** | **Correction to INS5 and to proposed ticket 18: it is FIVE seeded patterns, not four.** Enumerated and executed 2026-08-12 through the real `match_command` over `required_uninstall_readiness_permissions()`'s own output -- every `Bash` entry, with its token count, and both failure shapes tested per entry: `uv tool uninstall toolguard:*` (4 tokens), `rm <claude_dir>/toolguard_hook.toml:*` (2), `rm <claude_dir>/toolguard_hook.local.toml:*` (2), `rm -rf <claude_dir>/skills/toolguard-security-audit:*` (3), `rm -rf <claude_dir>/skills/toolguard-maintenance:*` (3) -- **all five** swallow an extra argument and **all five** match a suffix glued to their last token. Ticket 18's table has four ROWS but lists the security-audit pattern twice (two different witness commands) and never names `toolguard_hook.local.toml` or the maintenance skill dir at all; INS5 inherited the count. The plain `rm` entries are affected exactly as the `rm -rf` ones are: `rm <hook.toml>:*` vs `rm <hook.toml> /etc/passwd` -> `True`. Also confirmed in the same run: middle tokens ARE space-guarded (`uv tool uninstall toolguard:*` vs `uv toolx uninstall toolguard` -> `False`), single-token bases are guarded (`cd:*` vs `cdx /tmp` -> `False`; `toolguard-audit:*` vs `toolguard-audit-evil --wipe` -> `False`), and the two `Write`/`Edit` literal paths do NOT over-match (`.../settings.json` vs `.../settings.json.bak` and `.../settings.jsonx` -> both `False`). Blast radius: the five multi-token `Bash` entries, last token only. | `toolguard/tools/uninstall_readiness.py` (all five multi-token `Bash` patterns) ; count to correct in `proposed-tickets/18-...md` and in INS5 above | Nothing to fix in this module -- ticket 18's `match_command` fix is the fix. Recorded so the "which entries" question does not have to be re-measured, and so ticket 18 is not filed with a table that misses two of its own examples. The module docstring deliberately states no count for the same reason. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **"This is a deliberate change from an earlier `ask`-gated design (Arnon, TOO-15)"** (`uninstall_readiness.py` module docstring) -- **cut as unsupported history, not corrected.** `git show 4215208:toolguard/tools/uninstall_readiness.py` (the commit that created the file) has `list_type="allow"` on all eight entries; `git log -S` over `docs/` finds no earlier uninstall-readiness text at all. There is no `ask`-gated version of this rule set in the repository's history. It may refer to a design discussion rather than to code -- unverifiable either way, and change history is git's job, so it went rather than being re-worded.
- **Rewriting the four over-matching patterns.** Out of scope by the sweep's rules (strings are code) and wrong on the merits: the synthesized trailing `*` comes from `match_command`, not from these strings, so narrowing them would move the defect rather than fix it. The docstring now says where the cause is.
- **`self_integrity.py`'s `^rm\b` anchor gap (bare `sudo`, absolute path) does not apply to these files.** Checked: `uninstall_readiness.py` has no `[regex]` pattern at all -- its `rm` entries are DEFAULT prefixes, and `recommended_protections.py`'s 16 patterns are all file-kind (`Read`/`Write`/`Edit`), matched by `file_matching`, not by an anchored regex. Nothing to carry over.
- **`[native]` end-anchor under-match (ticket 17) does not apply either.** None of the three modules emits a `[native]` pattern.
- **"`[hard_deny]` ... is the strongest guarantee toolguard offers"** (`recommended_protections.py`) -- the superlative was cut and the mechanical half kept: the pool is checked before the normal cascade, so a `[permissions]` allow at any level does not override it (`resolve.py:228`, `config.py:_pool_hard_deny_entries`). The dropped half also quietly ignored the `hard_deny.allow` carve-out list, which is a real override path of a different kind.

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **UR-R1** | `self_permission.py` and `uninstall_readiness.py` as wholes | **The two modules are the same module twice.** Frozen dataclass of `(tool, pattern, list_type, probe, rationale)`; a `*Status` dataclass of `(permission, current_verdict, needs_action, recommendation)`; a `_status_for` classifier; an `evaluate_*` loop that calls `decide` on each probe; a `missing_*` filter over `needs_action`; and two `*_to_dict` serializers -- in the same order, with the same docstring shapes. The comments do not cluster *within* either file (both are flat tables and one-line helpers), which is why this shows up as duplication between them rather than as a long function. `recommended_protections.py` is a third, degenerate instance: same table shape, no evaluation half. | One `PermissionRecommendation` record plus a generic `evaluate(config, table)` / `missing(config, table)` / `to_dict(...)`, with the three tables as data and `needs_action` supplied as a per-entry predicate (read-only vs mutating vs always-allow are the only three rules in play today). Worth pricing against the risk: these tables are what a governed install writes into a user's config, and the three `_status_for` variants are the only place their differences live. Not attempted here -- comments-only pass. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/log_harvest.py`, `transcript_harvest.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `LH*`; the numeric sequence and `AE`/`C`/`D`/`DG`/`E`/`EL`/`GA`/`HR`/`INS`/`IU`/`M`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`UR`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the modules on this branch, 2026-08-12.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **LH1** | **Transcript harvesting silently drops every governable non-builtin tool call.** The payload key comes from the registry-wide `TOOLS_BY_NAME` (five entries), but the harvest gate is `if tool not in BUILTIN_TOOLS: continue` (four entries). Executed 2026-08-12: `KNOWN_TOOL_NAMES - BUILTIN_TOOLS == {'mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command'}`, and that tool has a full `ToolSpec(kind=COMMAND, payload_key='command')`. `docs/configuration.md:103,224` and `docs/install.md:255` tell users to add exactly that tool to `governed_tools`; when they do, its calls appear in the daily log (`log_harvest` picks them up, as tool `'Bash'`) but **never** in the transcript corpus. Tools declared through `additional_supported_tools` are missed the same way and are not in the registry at all, so they would also fall to `_command_for_tool`'s `"command"` default. The gate reads as if it wanted `KNOWN_TOOL_NAMES`. | `toolguard/tools/transcript_harvest.py:276` (gate); payload lookup at `:219` | One-token change on its face (`BUILTIN_TOOLS` -> `KNOWN_TOOL_NAMES`), but it widens what `replay`/`redundancy`/`consolidate` see, so it wants its own ticket and a test. The prose was corrected in this pass: `harvest_transcript_file`'s docstring now names `BUILTIN_TOOLS` and says outright that it is narrower than the recognised set, and `_command_for_tool`'s `tool: Known (builtin) tool name` -- which papered over the same conflation -- now says "a registered tool name". |
| **LH2** | **A multi-rule `Violated Rules` line parses into a mangled `rule_text`.** `log_writer._render_markdown_entry:269` joins rules as ``` `a`, `b` ```, but `_VIOLATED_RULES_RE` is ``` `(.+)` ``` with `re.DOTALL` and a greedy `.+`, so it captures everything between the outermost backticks. Executed 2026-08-12 on a section carrying ``` - **Violated Rules**: `rm -rf *`, `sudo *` ```: `LogEntry.rule_text == 'rm -rf *`, `sudo *'` -- one string with the separator and inner backticks embedded, not two rules. Single-rule entries (the common case, and every entry the hook writes today via `_log_non_allow_decision`) are unaffected, which is why this has never shown up. | `toolguard/tools/log_harvest.py:_VIOLATED_RULES_RE` | Not fixable in prose: `rule_text` is a single `Optional[str]` field, so representing several rules is a shape change, not a regex change. Worth deciding whether the corpus needs the rules split at all before touching it. The docstring now says only that `rule_text` is `Matched Rule` when present, else `Violated Rules`, else `None` -- it makes no claim about splitting. |
| **LH3** | **`_parse_command_field` is the one function in either module with no return annotation.** Declared `def _parse_command_field(raw: str):`, returning `Tuple[str, str]`. Every other function in both files is annotated. Cosmetic, but it is why the docstring has to state the return shape in prose that a signature would carry. | `toolguard/tools/log_harvest.py:108` | One-line fix; strings-and-signatures are code, so not touched here. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **"per-sub-command entries fold provenance back into `Matched Rule` in the pre-R3 bracketed format (e.g. ``git *  [project: /path]``) and there is no separate Provenance field at all"** (`log_harvest.py` module docstring) -- **FALSE, and deleted rather than corrected.** Executed end-to-end through the real hook in a `toolguard.testing.sandbox` experiment, 2026-08-12: `git status && ls -la` under `allow = ["Bash(git *)", "Bash(ls:*)"]` produced **two** sections, each with a clean `- **Matched Rule**: `git *`` / `` `ls:*` `` **and its own** `- **Provenance**: project: /tmp/.../toolguard_hook.toml`. `hook._unit_matched_rule_for_log` returns `unit.matched_rule` (or the fallback placeholder) and nothing else; `hook._log_allowed_command:573` passes `_provenance_brief(unit.provenance)` separately; `log_writer._render_markdown_entry:271-274` renders `Provenance` as its own bullet. The bracketed form is `permission_resolution.py:77`'s **prompt reason** text, which never reaches the log. The paragraph also pointed at `hook.py::_log_allowed_command`'s docstring for the claim, and that docstring does not make it -- a dangling pointer on top of a false statement. Replaced by one sentence about what the parser reads and ignores.
- **"For a NEW toolguard user (cold start) or an auto-mode user there are NO toolguard logs at all"** (`transcript_harvest.py` module docstring) -- **the auto-mode half is false; cut, and the cold-start half kept.** The hook runs under every permission mode: `hook.py:1274` reads Claude Code's `permission_mode` and records it on the log entry "purely for diagnosis", and auto-memory `project_toolguard_ask_is_enforced_in_auto_mode` records that toolguard's `ask` IS enforced in auto mode. A mode in which toolguard runs is a mode in which toolguard logs. The trailing "which makes this harvester a prerequisite for onboarding/migration as well as ongoing maintenance" went with it as unverifiable purpose narrative.
- **"Non-text blocks are stringified"** (`transcript_harvest._extract_text`) -- **false in the dict case.** Executed: `_extract_text([{'type':'text','text':'hi'}, {'type':'image','source':{'a':1}}, 'raw'])` -> `'hi  raw'`. A dict block with no `text` key contributes an empty string, not its repr; only a **non-dict** block is stringified whole. Corrected in place, with the empty-string case named.
- **"Malformed sections (missing fields, bad dates, unknown status values, etc.) are silently skipped"** (`log_harvest.py` module docstring) -- **self-contradictory as written**, since the next sentence says an unknown status is preserved as-is. It is: `_parse_section` skips only on an unreadable heading, a missing `Status`, or a missing `Command`. Corrected.
- **"Unlike daily logs, transcript files are not per-day, so the floor is applied per ENTRY (by its timestamp), not per file"** (`harvest_transcripts`) -- **false about daily logs.** `log_harvest.harvest` applies **both** filters: the file-name date at `:307` and then the entry timestamp at `:323`. The contrast was cut rather than re-stated.
- **"tool: ... or the raw tool name if not recognised"** (`LogEntry`) -- **false.** `_parse_command_field` returns a name only when it is in `FILE_TOOLS`; every other shape returns `'Bash'`. Executed: `'Grep(foo)'` -> `('Bash', 'Grep(foo)')`, `'mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command(ls)'` -> `('Bash', 'mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command(ls)')`. Corrected.
- **"Common values are ``'EXECUTED'`` and ``'REFUSED'``"** (`LogEntry.status`) -- **incomplete**: the hook also writes `status="ask"`, rendered `ASK` (`hook.py:946`, confirmed in the sandbox run). The enumeration was **deleted rather than extended** -- `constants.py`'s `STATUS_*` docstrings are that vocabulary's home, and this file also carries transcript-sourced `ERROR`/`UNKNOWN` values it does not parse.
- **"Discovery and conflict entries are silently skipped"** -- **half wrong, half redundant.** Conflict entries are never seen at all: they live in `toolguard-conflict-YYYY-MM-DD.md`, and `_LOG_NAME_RE` rejects that name (executed: `False`, as for `toolguard-error-*.md` and `toolguard-*.jsonl`). The Discovery half is true but is already stated on the writing side, in `log_writer.py:508-510`'s frozen comment; per one-fact-one-home this file now says only that a section with no `Status` field is skipped.
- **The `harvest` "sorted by timestamp (oldest first)" guarantee.** Not restated. Unlike `harvest_transcripts`, which sorts explicitly, `log_harvest.harvest` never sorts -- the ordering is a consequence of one file per date plus append-only writes. The docstring now says what the code does (file-name date order, then each file's own order) and claims nothing about timestamps. `corpus.harvest_corpus` sorts the merged result anyway.
- **Deleted with the gap left open: the per-entry re-filter comment.** The old text said "the very first and last files may contain entries outside the window" -- false, since the floor is a lower bound only and there is no ceiling, so no *last* file can be out of window. Nothing replaced it: the second filter is belt-and-braces (a file dated on the floor can only hold entries at or after that day's midnight), and the code is two self-evident lines.

### Refactoring candidates surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **LH-R1** | `log_harvest._parse_section` | Five parallel `_*_RE` module constants and a five-branch `if m: ... continue` chain over them, then five locals reassembled into one record. The chain is a field table written out longhand; three of the four comments deleted from this file lived in it, marking the joins between phases (`# Parse header`, `# Skip sections without a Status field`, `# Skip sections without a Command field`). | A `(_FIELD_RES: tuple[(name, regex)])` table and a `dict` accumulator, leaving `_parse_section` as header-parse + table-walk + validate-and-build. Adding a sixth field (`Provenance`, `Note`, `Permission Mode` and `Context` are all written today and all ignored) is then one table row instead of a constant, a branch and a local. |
| **LH-R2** | `transcript_harvest.harvest_transcript_file` | 40-line body, four levels of nesting, and seven `continue` guards across two nested loops -- an outer walk over transcript entries (message shape, timestamp, agent) and an inner walk over `tool_use` items (type, tool gate, input shape, command). The two loops share nothing but the `LogEntry` they build. | Extract the inner loop as `_entries_for_message(content, timestamp, agent, results, path)`, or a `_iter_tool_uses(entries)` generator yielding `(entry, item)` pairs. LH1's gate sits inside the inner loop and would become a one-line predicate on that generator. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/rule_apply.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `RA*`; the numeric sequence and `AE`/`C`/`D`/`DG`/`E`/`EL`/`GA`/`HR`/`INS`/`IU`/`LH`/`M`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`UR`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the module on this branch, 2026-08-12.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **RA1** | **A dry run's `diff` shows changes no proposal asked for, and can be non-empty with nothing applied.** `_apply_to_file` always re-renders through `_render_via_writer`, and the writers default to `auto_sort=True`, so the diff carries the writer's normalisation as well as the proposals' edits. Executed 2026-08-12 with an unsorted TOML allow list and a proposal whose `removed_patterns` were all absent (`total_applied=0, total_skipped=1`): `diff` was non-empty, showing `Bash(aaa:*)` moved above `Bash(zzz:*)` **and** the file's `deny = []` / `ask = []` lines deleted (`rule_sort`'s `if not entries: continue`). Only `written` is gated on `applied`, so nothing was written -- but the moment one proposal does apply, that unrequested normalisation is written with it, and it is what the skill shows the user for approval. | `toolguard/tools/rule_apply.py:_apply_to_file` (render at `:324`, `written` gate at `:338`) | Two separable questions: whether `--apply` should re-sort and drop empty lists at all, and whether the previewed diff should be restricted to the proposals' own edits. The docstrings now say only that re-rendering is not identity and that the diff can be non-empty with nothing applied; they make no claim that it shows only what the proposals changed. |
| **RA2** | **`render_change_report` is documented as ASCII-only and is not.** Every variable part is caller data -- `str(fchange.path)`, `prop.kind`, and `wrap_tool_pattern(prop.tool, body)`. Executed 2026-08-12 with allow patterns `Bash(cat café-a:*)` / `Bash(cat café-b:*)`: `render_change_report(report).encode("ascii")` raised `UnicodeEncodeError: 'ascii' codec can't encode character '\xe9' in position 197`. There is no project-wide ASCII rule to appeal to either (`README.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `llms.txt`, `technical-notes.md`, `docs/**` have none). | `toolguard/tools/rule_apply.py:render_change_report` | Prose fixed here (the claim was deleted, not narrowed). If ASCII output is actually wanted -- e.g. for a terminal with a narrow encoding -- it needs a code change at the render site, not a docstring. `mining.py:331` makes the same promise and was flagged separately. |
| **RA3** | **The equality guard around the added-pattern rewrite has no observable effect.** `if list(resolved_added_entries) != existing_at_added:` skips a remove-and-re-append whose only difference is position in `allow` -- and `_render_via_writer` calls `write_toml_config` / `write_json_config` without `auto_sort`, i.e. at its `True` default, so position in `allow` never reaches the file. Both branches produce the same multiset of entries and therefore the same rendered text. | `toolguard/tools/rule_apply.py:310` | Harmless, and cheap. Flagged because its comment claimed a benefit it does not deliver ("avoids reordering/diff noise for the common case of a brand-new pattern"), and the brand-new-pattern half was false besides: executed 2026-08-12, a brand-new pattern gives `existing_at_added == []` against a one-entry `resolved`, so the guard is *taken*, never skipped. The comment was deleted with the gap left open rather than replaced with a different rationale. |
| **RA4** | **Native settings files are read with `is_native=False`.** `_read_raw_permissions` passes `is_native=False` unconditionally, but a `ConsolidationProposal`'s `layer_provenance` can name a native `settings.json`. `config.py:1483-1487` documents the opposite convention for its own preserving read -- "`is_native=False` is always correct here: native layers were already skipped above". Effect looks benign: a structured `{match = ...}` entry in a native file would be re-emitted verbatim by `RuleEntry.to_source()` and so survives the write guard. The reachable difference is that such an entry's metadata can be union-merged into a written entry by `_resolve_added_entry`, in a file where toolguard ignores structured entries at load time. | `toolguard/tools/rule_apply.py:_read_raw_permissions` | Low severity, and possibly correct as written for a round-tripping read. Worth a decision rather than a fix: either pass the layer's real `is_native`, or state in the code why a write path deliberately ignores it. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **"applying it would require silently resolving a genuine metadata CONTRADICTION ... would silently discard one side of that contradiction"** (`_resolve_added_entry`) -- **FALSE, and deleted rather than corrected.** `merge_entries` case 3 discards nothing: executed 2026-08-12 on two structured entries for `Bash(git diff:*)` disagreeing on `additionalContext` plus a bare entry for the same pattern, the outcome was **both** structured entries preserved (`[('Bash(git diff:*)', {'additionalContext': 'one'}), ('Bash(git diff:*)', {'additionalContext': 'two'})]`) with one `MergeConflict`. The only entry dropped is the bare one -- case 1 -- which is the proposal's own contribution, not a side of the contradiction. What the code does is refuse when `merge_entries` reports a conflict; the docstring now says that and stops. The `skip_reason` **string** still reads "would lose rule enrichment" and is left unedited under the comments-only rule, so it now overstates what the refusal prevents; `test_tools_rule_apply.py` asserts that phrase, so changing it is a test change too.
- **"deciding *which* proposals to apply, refusing to run on a dirty git tree, and obtaining the user's approval are the SKILL's responsibility"** (module docstring) -- **the dirty-tree half is false; the whole sentence was cut.** The refusal is in code, one caller up: `maintenance.py:630` runs `migration_preflight(Path(args.dir))` and returns before `apply_proposals` at `:641` when `preflight.blockers` is non-empty. The skill relays that refusal (`SKILL.md:307-309`), it does not perform it. Replaced by an in-file negative -- this module applies what it is given -- with no claim about who does the rest.
- **"a loaded `Configuration`, whose layer content may have been takeover-filtered"** (module docstring / `_read_raw_permissions`) -- **false as stated, and cut rather than corrected.** `ConfigLayer.content` is the raw parsed dict (`config_types.py:65`); takeover filtering happens in the *accessors*, `Configuration.permission_levels_with_provenance` at `config.py:1067`, and only for native layers and one tool at a time. Rather than restate someone else's mechanism, the module docstring now carries the in-file reason that makes any narrower read wrong: the whole `permissions` section is rewritten from what `_read_raw_permissions` returns, so an entry missing from that read is deleted from the user's config.
- **"see its `added_pattern` docstring"** (`_resolve_added_entry`, for the claim that a proposal always adds a plain metadata-free pattern) -- **dangling pointer.** `ConsolidationProposal.added_pattern`'s docstring reads "Wrapper-free body of the replacement rule, or `None` for a pure drop (every static-subsumption proposal)" and says nothing about metadata. The field is a `str`, so metadata-free is a consequence of the type; the pointer was deleted and the fact stated in one clause.
- **The `real_patterns` paragraph in the write block.** Not restated. It is a five-line copy of `rule_entry.real_patterns`' own docstring and of `RuleEntry.synthesized_pattern`'s, both of which state the write-path contract in full; per one-fact-one-home the call site now carries only why the write goes through the guard at all. (It also claimed a synthesized `repr(raw)` pattern "can never appear in `new_text`" -- unfalsifiable as an absolute, since a config could legitimately contain the string `12`.)
- **"reports exactly what changed"** (module docstring). Not restated -- RA1 is why. The diff reports what re-rendering changed, which is a superset of what the proposals changed.

### Refactoring candidate surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **RA-R1** | `rule_apply._apply_to_file` | 110-line body doing five separable jobs in sequence: the `path is None` early return, a per-proposal skip/apply loop with three `continue` guards, the reassembly of `new_permissions`, diff computation, and the guarded write. **Every inline comment in the module is inside this one function** (four after this pass, one per phase, plus the one deleted under RA3) -- the classic "these would be the docstrings of the extracted functions" shape. The loop body alone carries the whole apply semantics (drift check, enrichment resolution, removal, addition). | Extract the loop body as `_apply_one(prop, allow) -> Optional[str]` returning a skip reason, and the tail as `_write_if_changed(path, file_format, new_permissions, dry_run)`. `_apply_to_file` then reads as read -> fold -> render -> write. Note RA1 and RA3 both live in the tail, and separating render-for-preview from render-for-write is easier once it is its own function. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/redundancy.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `RD*`; the numeric sequence and `AE`/`C`/`D`/`DG`/`E`/`EL`/`GA`/`HR`/`INS`/`IU`/`LH`/`M`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RA`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`UR`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Both rows were executed live against the module on this branch, 2026-08-12.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **RD1** | **A "static duplicate" can be two rules that match disjoint command sets.** `_normalised_body` lowercases every body and, for DEFAULT bodies, collapses runs of two or more spaces. Neither reduction is one the matchers make: `fnmatch`, `re.search` and `PurePath.full_match` are all case-sensitive, and `match_command` compares the pattern's inter-token spacing literally. Executed 2026-08-12: `Git *` and `git *` share the key `('default', 'git *')`, and `match_command('git status', ...)` is `False` for the first, `True` for the second; likewise `[regex]^Git ` / `[regex]^git ` and `[glob]/Home/**` / `[glob]/home/**`. The space rule cuts the other way -- `a  b:*` and `a b:*` share the key `('default', 'a b:*')`, and `a  b x` matches only the first while `a b x` matches only the second. `find_static_duplicates(['Git *', 'git *'], ...)` duly reports `git *` as a "Duplicate of 'Git *'", and the maintenance skill's job is to propose dropping it. | `toolguard/tools/redundancy.py:_normalised_body`; consumed by `find_static_duplicates` and by `hierarchy.find_cross_layer_redundancies` | Two separable questions: whether case folding buys anything at all (a config with `Git *` and `git *` is more likely two different rules than one typo), and whether the space collapse should be dropped now that the colon rule -- which is sound, since `match_command` strips both sides of the `cmd:args` split -- covers the fixture it was written for. Prose fixed here: `_normalised_body` now states the gap with both measured counterexamples, and says a shared key is grounds for review rather than a safe deletion. **`hierarchy.py` inherits this and says nothing about it** -- its own "candidate for review, not a verified-safe deletion" paragraph is about intermediate deny layers, a different cause. |
| **RD2** | **A corpus-redundant finding can name a layer whose copy of the rule was never removed -- and report a live rule as redundant.** `find_corpus_redundant_allows` iterates `per_layer_rules(config, tool)`, whose allow lists are **takeover-filtered**, but `_config_without_allow` re-discovers the owning layer by scanning **raw** `layer.content`. Under takeover mode the two disagree about who owns a blanket allow. Executed 2026-08-12 on an in-memory two-layer config -- native (`specificity 0`) with `Bash(*)`, toolguard_hook (`specificity 1`, `takeover_mode.enabled = true`) with `Bash(*)` and `Bash(ls:*)`, corpus `['git status']`: `per_layer_rules` gives the native layer `allow=()` and the hook layer `allow=('*', 'ls:*')`, so the loop reaches `'*'` at the **hook** layer; `_config_without_allow` then strips `Bash(*)` from the **native** layer (already neutralised by takeover, so a no-op in effect) and leaves the hook's copy in place. The replay is trivially identical and the output is `FINDING pattern='*' attributed to toolguard_hook` -- the one rule actually granting the blanket allow, reported as safe to delete. | `toolguard/tools/redundancy.py:find_corpus_redundant_allows` / `_config_without_allow` | The caller already knows which layer it is iterating. Fix is to pass `lr.provenance` into `_config_without_allow` instead of having it search, which also removes the search loop entirely (see RD-R1). Severity is bounded by takeover mode being the only known way to make the filtered and raw views disagree, but that is toolguard's headline configuration. No docstring in this file claims the attribution is right; the pass added none. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **"Both patterns resolve identically in the toolguard matcher (the trailing whitespace in the command portion is ignored when `args` is `*`)"** (`_normalised_body`) -- **the parenthetical is false and the sentence invites a false general reading; both were deleted.** `match_command` applies `cmd_pattern.strip()` and `args_pattern.strip()` unconditionally at `permissions.py:140-141`, not only when `args` is `*`, so the stated condition is not the mechanism. And read generally -- "patterns this function collapses match identically" -- it is false: see RD1. What is true is narrow enough to be worth nothing: the two *example* patterns do match identically, because the strip erases exactly the difference between them.
- **"This means every command that R would have matched is already covered by another rule"** (`find_corpus_redundant_allows`) -- **false, and contradicted three lines below by the function's own `Note:`** ("A corpus that does not cover the rule's match-set can give false 'redundant' signals"). A zero diff means no corpus entry distinguished the two configs; a corpus that never exercises R produces exactly the same zero diff. The same over-claim sat in the module docstring ("-- meaning another rule already covers every command R would have matched"). Both deleted; the honest form the `Note:` already carried is now the only statement, folded into the body of the docstring. The `note` **string** built into each finding is fine as written -- it says "another rule already covers its match-set (or no corpus entry exercises it)" and is left untouched.
- **"Deny and ask rules cannot generally be removed safely without a deeper analysis (removing a deny might unexpectedly allow something via the cascade)"** -- **unsupported, and deleted rather than replaced.** A removal that lets something through is precisely what `replay` classifies as `broadened`, and the redundancy test already requires `broadened_count == 0`; the stated hazard is the one thing the existing check would catch. Whatever the real reason for the allow-only scope is, it is not this, and per "do not supply a different one" the docstring now says only that deny and ask are not tested.
- **"Internal whitespace sequences are collapsed to a single space"** (`_normalised_body`) -- **false twice over.** The regex is `r"  +"`, so it collapses runs of two or more *spaces* and nothing else: executed 2026-08-12, `_normalised_body('a\tb:*')` -> `('default', 'a\tb:*')` and `_normalised_body('a \t b:*')` -> `('default', 'a \t b:*')`, both untouched. And it is inside the `ptype.value == "default"` branch, so it never applies to `[regex]`/`[glob]`/`[native]` bodies -- `_normalised_body('[regex]a  b')` -> `('regex', 'a  b')`. Restated accurately and scoped to DEFAULT.
- **"An element that fails to normalize is simply not a match here -- never raises"** (`_config_without_allow`) -- **true but deleted**, as a claim about another module. Verified 2026-08-12: `normalize_entry` returned `(None, issues)` for `None`, `42`, `[]`, `{}`, `{'match': 1}`, `''`, `b'x'` and a bare `object()`, raising on none of them. It is `rule_entry`'s contract to state, and this file is not where anyone will look for it.
- **"Mirrors the exact `normalize_entry`-then-compare-pattern idiom used by `with_layer_rules_replaced`"** -- **true** (`config_access.py:307-311` does exactly that) **and cut anyway**: rule 1, a cross-reference an IDE finds and prose cannot keep current. The hazard it decorated was kept, minus its incident history.
- **The `Scope / deferred work` and `Design note on per_layer_rules interaction` sections** (module docstring) -- cut. The first is a phase label ("deferred to P2") wrapped around a two-bullet restatement of the two strategies named directly above it. The second says cross-layer redundancy is not detected here, which is true, but that subject has a home: `hierarchy.find_cross_layer_redundancies` documents both the scan and its limits. Per one-fact-one-home the gap was left open rather than filled with a pointer.
- **Self-caught on the cold re-read: "Subsumption between two *different* patterns (literal alternation, glob expansion) is not attempted by either."** This pass wrote that sentence and it is **false of the corpus strategy**, which detects one rule covering a differently-spelled rule -- that is the whole point of the replay. Narrowed to "No *static* subsumption between two different patterns is attempted", which is what the original text meant by "static family-based subsumption". Recorded because it is the failure mode the brief predicts: a scope sentence widened while being shortened.

### Refactoring candidate surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **RD-R1** | `redundancy._config_without_allow` | Two thirds of the body is a search for a layer the sole caller is already iterating, and that search is where RD2 lives: it reads a different view of the config (raw `layer.content`) from the one that produced the pattern (`per_layer_rules`). The remaining third is a one-line delegation to `with_layer_allow_replaced`. The kept hazard comment is entirely about the search. | Take `provenance` as a parameter and delegate directly, letting `with_layer_allow_replaced`'s own "returns `config` unchanged when nothing matched" path serve the identity check the caller already relies on. The function then has no branches, the raw-vs-filtered disagreement cannot arise, and the hazard comment moves out with the loop -- `with_layer_rules_replaced` already carries its own version of it. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/mining.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `MI*`; the numeric sequence and `AE`/`C`/`D`/`DG`/`E`/`EL`/`GA`/`HR`/`INS`/`IU`/`LH`/`M`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RA`/`RD`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`UR`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the module on this branch, 2026-08-12.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **MI1** | **The `declined` signal conflates "the user said no" with "toolguard denied it", and the two want opposite advice.** `_classify` returns `SIGNAL_DECLINED` for any entry whose status is `REFUSED`, and `REFUSED` has two unrelated producers: `hook.py`'s deny path writes `LogRecord(status="refused", ...)` for every command *toolguard itself* blocked (three sites: the deny branch of `_log_non_allow_decision`, plus the two "no command/file_path provided" guards), and `transcript_harvest` separately infers `STATUS_REFUSED` from a rejection marker in a `tool_result`. `constants.STATUS_REFUSED`'s own docstring already names both ("toolguard denied it, or transcript harvesting inferred the user declined it"); mining collapses them. Executed end-to-end 2026-08-12 on a temp-dir fixture log holding a single `- **Status**: REFUSED` / `` `rm -rf /tmp/zzz` `` section, mined against a config denying `Bash(rm -rf *)`: `signal: declined \| verdict now: deny \| observed: {'REFUSED': 1}`, and `MiningReport.declined` returns it. So a rule working exactly as intended is filed under the signal the module documents as the user having said no. | `toolguard/tools/mining.py:_classify`; producers in `toolguard/hook.py` and `toolguard/tools/transcript_harvest.py` | Second-order effect: because a deny-verdict entry recorded as `REFUSED` is taken by the `REFUSED` branch first, `SIGNAL_DENIED` only fires for a deny-verdict entry whose status is something *else* -- in practice `ASK` (the hook's own prompt log, `hook.py:946`), `ERROR`, or `UNKNOWN`. The `denied` bucket is therefore much thinner than its name suggests. The fix needs a distinguishable status, not a smarter classifier: `LogEntry.status` is the only signal `_classify` gets, and both producers spell it `REFUSED`. Prose fixed here: `declined` is now documented as "corpus recorded `REFUSED`, whatever the config says", with no claim about who refused. |
| **MI2** | **`render_mining_report` was documented as ASCII-only and is not; the TOO-15 plan states the same requirement, and nothing enforces it.** Executed 2026-08-12: a `CommandGroup` with `distinct_commands=('grep café /tmp',)` renders to a string with `isascii() == False`, and `.encode("ascii")` raises `'ascii' codec can't encode character '\xe9' in position 169`. Every variable part of the output is caller data -- `group.tool`, `group.command_key`, `group.distinct_commands`, `group.current_verdict` -- all of which originate in a harvested command line, which can hold any UTF-8 the shell accepted. Only the title, the separator rule and the count line are fixed. The identical claim in `rule_apply.py:467` was measured false by a concurrent pass for the same reason. | `toolguard/tools/mining.py:render_mining_report`; requirement text at `toolguard-memories/TOO-15/TOO-15 Toolguard Skills and Config Tooling - Requirements and Plan.md:804` ("`render_mining_report(report, fmt)` ASCII") and `:841` ("Three ASCII renderers") | Two ways out and they are not equivalent: either drop the requirement -- no consumer found needs ASCII; `maintenance.py:274` splices the rendered text straight into `render_maintenance_report`'s line list, in text or Markdown mode alike -- or make it real by transliterating/escaping the caller data at render time. Do not leave the requirement filed and unimplemented. The docstring here now describes the output structure and makes no encoding promise. |
| **MI3** | **`min_occurrences` and `CommandGroup.occurrences` count corpus entries, and the corpus double-counts anything present in both harvesters.** `harvest_corpus` concatenates `harvest(logs)` and `harvest_transcripts(transcripts)` and sorts by timestamp with no de-duplication step -- documented there in terms ("Entries are NOT de-duplicated -- a command recorded in both sources appears twice"), and both harvesters emit naive local datetimes so the duplicates land adjacent rather than distinguishable. A command that both the hook logged and the transcript recorded therefore contributes 2 to `occurrences`. That is the number a human uses to decide "is this frequent enough to become a rule", and it is inflated by an arbitrary factor between 1 and 2 depending on how much transcript history happens to overlap the log window. | `toolguard/tools/mining.py:mine_rule_candidates`; source in `toolguard/tools/corpus.py:harvest_corpus` | Not a mining bug -- mining counts what it is given -- but the threshold parameter is where the consequence lands. Options: de-duplicate in `harvest_corpus` on `(timestamp-to-the-second, tool, command)`, or have mining count distinct `(source-file, timestamp, command)` triples. No prose about this was added to `mining.py`: the fact has a home in `corpus.py`'s own docstring and restating it here would be a second copy to drift. The docstring for `min_occurrences` now at least says it counts entries rather than distinct commands. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **"`tightened_count`: ... should be 0 for a pure allow addition; non-zero signals an interaction worth inspecting"** (`AddRuleEffect`) -- **deleted rather than restated, because I could not verify it and had no basis for a replacement.** It is a guarantee about `permission_resolution`'s behaviour under a layer edit, asserted from a file that does none of that resolution; the standard's "when a justification turns out to be unsupported, delete it -- do not supply a different one" applies directly. The field is now documented as what it is: "Number of corpus entries that became stricter." **If the invariant is real it is worth a test, not a docstring** -- `evaluate_added_allow_rule` never asserts on it, so a non-zero value today would be reported and believed with no signal that it is anomalous.
- **"the successor to the ad-hoc `denied-summary` prototype"** (module docstring) -- cut as a claim about the past, per the standard. `denied-summary` is a slash command that still exists in this repo; whether it was a prototype, whether this replaced it, and whether it is still in use are three separate facts none of which this file can keep current, and none of which help a reader of `mine_rule_candidates`.
- **"pattern generation/generalisation is agent judgement (and, later, the curated-tool advisor)"** -- the negative half was kept and rewritten as "It never invents or generalises a permission pattern", which is verifiable inside the file (`pattern` is a caller argument at every site; nothing constructs one). The rest is a forward-looking claim about a component that does not exist, and a claim about what happens outside the module.
- **"The allow/deny asymmetry applies: allow candidates are conservative (the user must approve adding them, and replay quantifies the blast radius); declined/deny signals bias toward fail-safe."** -- cut whole. Nothing in this module makes a user approve anything, and "biases toward fail-safe" names no mechanism here; `_classify` is a four-branch dispatch on two strings.
- **"it quantifies EXACTLY which corpus commands the rule would newly admit -- the perfect input for a risk note and the guard against an over-broad suggestion"** (module docstring) and **"the blast radius a user needs to approve the suggestion responsibly"** (`evaluate_added_allow_rule`) -- **the mechanical half is true, the "guard against an over-broad suggestion" is the `redundancy.py` falsehood in a new costume.** `evaluate_added_allow_rule` iterates `corpus` and nothing else, so a rule whose match-set the corpus never exercises reports an empty `newly_allowed` and reads as safe. Replaced with the scoping statement the mechanism actually supports: "Only commands the corpus contains are measured, so the result is a lower bound on what the rule admits, not its full reach."
- **"Bash commands group by their leading executable token; file tools group by their target's parent directory"** (`_command_key`) -- **false by omission, the exact tool-kind error the standard flags.** The branch is `if tool in FILE_TOOLS`, so *every* non-file tool takes the token path. Executed 2026-08-12: `_command_key('mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command', 'git status --porcelain')` -> `'git'`. Rewritten as "A file tool groups by its target's parent directory; every other tool -- `Bash` and any MCP terminal tool alike -- groups by the command's leading token." (Whether such an entry can reach the corpus at all is a separate matter: `transcript_harvest` gates on `BUILTIN_TOOLS` and drops MCP tool calls, but `log_harvest` does not gate, and `_command_key` is a public-ish grouping primitive either way.)
- **"An ASCII-only sorted summary, allow-candidates and declined commands first"** (`render_mining_report` `Returns:`) -- **both halves false, and the second was contradicted by this file's own constant comment.** The sort key is `(-occurrences, _SIGNAL_ORDER[signal], tool, command_key)`, so frequency dominates and signal only breaks ties -- which `_SIGNAL_ORDER`'s comment said correctly ("lower sorts first when occurrences tie") three hundred lines above the docstring that said otherwise. Executed 2026-08-12 on a corpus of nine `find` entries (config `ask`, status `ERROR`) and two `curl` entries (config `ask`, status `EXECUTED`): the rendered report prints `[asked] Bash 'find' x9` before `[allow-candidate] Bash 'curl' x2`. The ASCII half is MI2.
- **"Commands the config already allows and that ran fine are `consistent`"** (module docstring) and **"`consistent` commands (already allowed and executed) are omitted"** (`mine_rule_candidates`) -- **narrower than the code twice over.** `_classify` returns `SIGNAL_CONSISTENT` as the residual branch, so `('allow', 'ERROR')`, `('allow', 'UNKNOWN')` and `('allow', 'ASK')` are all consistent too, not only `('allow', 'EXECUTED')`; "ran fine" excludes the case where the tool was permitted and then errored. Restated as the residual it is.
- **"sorted by occurrences (desc) then signal priority then command_key"** (`MiningReport.groups`) -- dropped `tool` from a four-element sort key, and said the same thing the producing function's `Returns:` said. One home kept, on `mine_rule_candidates`, with `tool` restored; the dataclass now points at it.
- **`SIGNAL_CONSISTENT = "consistent"  # omitted from the report`** -- true, and kept, but promoted to a `#:` docstring per the standard's rule 8 and made specific about *where* it is dropped (`mine_rule_candidates`, before grouping), since "omitted from the report" reads as a rendering choice and is not one.
- **The `# Accumulate per (tool, key, signal).` inline comment** -- cut. The next statement is `key = (entry.tool, _command_key(...), signal)`.
- **A note in `mining.py` about the corpus double-count (MI3)** -- **considered and rejected as a second home.** `corpus.harvest_corpus`'s docstring already states it in terms. Per one-fact-one-home the gap was left open here rather than filled with a restatement or a pointer.

### Refactoring candidate surfaced by the comment sweep

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **MI-R1** | `mining.mine_rule_candidates` | The accumulator is `Dict[Tuple[str, str, str], Dict]` -- an untyped dict of four string-keyed slots (`"commands"`, `"occurrences"`, `"verdicts"`, `"observed"`) built inline, read back by the same literals eleven lines later, and finally transcribed field-by-field into `CommandGroup`. Nothing checks a key spelling, and the type annotation says only `Dict`. It is also the one place in the file that needed an explanatory comment ("Accumulate per (tool, key, signal)"), which is the signal. | A small mutable `_Bucket` dataclass with the same four fields, `defaultdict(_Bucket)` for the get-or-create, and a `to_group(tool, command_key, signal)` method. That deletes the four string literals, the `bucket is None` branch, and the seven-line `CommandGroup(...)` transcription, and lets the reader see the shape at the top instead of inferring it from the writes. Matches the codebase's own preference for a frozen/typed record over a bare container. |

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/decision_ledger.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `DL*`; the numeric sequence and `AE`/`C`/`D`/`DG`/`E`/`EL`/`GA`/`HR`/`INS`/`IU`/`LH`/`M`/`MI`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RA`/`RD`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`UR`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the module on this branch, 2026-08-12, in a temp tree with `HOME` redirected.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL1** | **A decision's `level` is read from the file's own body, not from the file it was found in -- so a mislabelled ledger round-trips into the *other* level's file.** `load_ledger` does `level = data.get("level", "project")` and stamps every entry with it; `record_decision` then routes by `decision.level` through `ledger_path_for_level`. Executed 2026-08-12: a project ledger at `<root>/.claude/toolguard_decisions.json` whose top-level `"level"` was edited to `"user"` loads with `{d.level for d in load_ledger(path)} == {'user'}`, and re-recording any of those entries writes them to `~/.toolguard/decisions.json` -- silently migrating a project decision to the user level, where it then suppresses the same suggestion in every other repo. The missing-key default compounds it in the other direction: a hand-written or older user ledger with no `"level"` key loads as `project`. | `toolguard/tools/decision_ledger.py:load_ledger` (`level` resolution), consumed by `record_decision` via `ledger_path_for_level` | The path already knows the answer at both call sites in `load_merged`, so the fix is to pass the level in rather than read it out -- keeping the file's `"level"` field as a written-only provenance tag, or validating it against the caller's expectation and raising `LedgerError` on a mismatch (the module's own policy for a ledger that is present but wrong). Prose added here: `load_ledger` now states where `level` comes from; nothing states the round-trip consequence, which belongs with the fix. |
| **DL2** | **The schema tag is written into every ledger file and never read back, so the format change it exists to catch would not be caught.** `ledger_to_dict` writes `"schema": LEDGER_SCHEMA`; `load_ledger` checks only that the payload is a dict containing a list under `"decisions"`. Executed 2026-08-12: a ledger rewritten with `"schema": "totally-bogus/99"` loads its 2 entries without complaint, and so does the same file with the `"schema"` key deleted entirely. Repo-wide, `LEDGER_SCHEMA` appears in this module and in `test/unit/test_tools_decision_ledger.py` only. | `toolguard/tools/decision_ledger.py:LEDGER_SCHEMA` / `load_ledger` | Cheap to make real: one `data.get("schema")` comparison in `load_ledger` raising `LedgerError` on an unrecognised tag, which is the behaviour the module already commits to for a file that is present but not readable as a ledger. Until then the tag is provenance, not a guard. The constant's doc-comment said "so a future format change is detectable" and now says `load_ledger` does not check it. |
| **DL3** | **`record_decision` is an unlocked read-modify-write onto the live file, and the write itself is not atomic.** It calls `load_ledger`, filters, appends, then `path.write_text(...)` directly over the existing ledger -- no lock, no temp-file-and-rename. Two concurrent maintenance runs against the same level lose one side's entry outright; a crash or a full disk mid-write leaves a truncated JSON file, which `load_ledger` then correctly refuses to parse, so *every* settled decision in that ledger is unavailable until a human repairs it. The module's stated policy makes this worse rather than better: a corrupt ledger raises rather than degrading to empty, which is right for a silent-loss bug and painful for a truncation. | `toolguard/tools/decision_ledger.py:record_decision` | TOO-45 item 15 gave `migrate()` an OS file lock for the same read-modify-write shape; this is the other writer in the tool tree and did not get one. Write-to-temp-then-`os.replace` closes the truncation half on its own and is a three-line change. No prose about this was added -- it is a property of the code, not something a docstring should promise away. |

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **The brief's suggestion that `technical-notes.md`'s "the whole batch is validated before any entry is written, so a malformed member aborts atomically" contradicts the code** -- **rejected; the sentence is true as written.** `_run_record_decision` builds the entire `decisions` list through `new_decision` inside one `try` (`maintenance.py:1049-1067`) and only then enters the write loop at `:1069`, so a malformed or enum-invalid member returns 2 with nothing written. What is *not* atomic is an `OSError` during the write loop, which leaves earlier entries persisted -- a different condition from "a malformed member", and queue row M5 already states it in those terms. No `technical-notes.md` change proposed on this point.
- **"(git-tracked, so a project's settled decisions follow the code)"** (module docstring, on the project ledger) -- **cut: unverifiable in general and false in this very repo.** `.gitignore:12` is `/.claude`, so a project ledger written here would never be committed; `git ls-files .claude` returns nothing. Whether a user's `.claude/` is tracked is that user's choice and this module cannot know it. The surviving text says only where the file is.
- **"Resolves the project root the same way config discovery does (nearest `.git`/`pyproject.toml`)"** (`project_ledger_path`) -- **the parenthetical is an incomplete enumeration and was cut.** `find_project_root` -> `require_project_root` searches `CONFIG_ROOT_INDICATORS`, which is `STRONG_PROJECT_ANCHORS + ("pyproject.toml",)` = `.git`, `.hg`, `.jj`, `.claude`, `CLAUDE.md`, `pyproject.toml` (`path_utils.py:65,70`). Executed 2026-08-12: `project_ledger_path(<tmp>/cmroot/sub)` with only a `CLAUDE.md` at `cmroot/` returns `<tmp>/cmroot/.claude/toolguard_decisions.json`. Only the fallback -- anchor at `project_dir` when nothing is found -- survives, since that is the part a caller cannot see from the signature.
- **"Append (or replace in place)" / "Writes replace an existing same-id entry in place"** (`record_decision` and the module docstring) -- **"in place" is false positionally and was replaced by what the code does.** The body rebuilds the list without the matching id and appends the new entry at the end. Executed 2026-08-12: recording `famA`, then `famB`, then a second `famA` yields file order `['famB', 'famA']`. The docstrings now say a same-id entry is replaced rather than duplicated, and the replacement is written last. Note `technical-notes.md` carries the same phrase ("same-id replaces in place"); read as "replaces rather than duplicates" it is fine, and no change is proposed there.
- **"Schema tag written into every ledger file so a future format change is detectable"** -- the second clause was cut and replaced by the mechanical fact (DL2). It is the exact shape the brief warns about: a true mechanical statement whose stated *meaning* does not hold, sitting where a reader will take it as a guarantee that loading validates the tag.
- **The module docstring's second and third paragraphs** ("deliberately NOT outsourced to an arbitrary human-memory system", "re-parses it mechanically on every run", the ledger-versus-annotation argument in full) -- **cut as a second copy of `technical-notes.md`'s "Prior-decision ledger" section**, which states all of it and is the pointer the docstring now carries. The one-sentence version of the contrast was kept, because it is the reason the module exists and a reader hitting this file first needs it.
- **"``accept`` records that a change was taken; ``defer`` parks it for later"** (`VALID_DECISIONS`) -- narrowed rather than kept. Both glosses are conventions owned by the maintenance skill; inside this module the three values differ only in that `is_suppressed` tests for `reject`. The comment now says that and nothing about what the other two mean elsewhere.
- **"Decision kinds the skill records"** (`VALID_KINDS`) -- the outward attribution was dropped (now "Recognised decision kinds") but **the claim was checked before dropping it and is true**: all six kinds appear in `.claude/skills/toolguard-maintenance/SKILL.md:329-330` and `passes/4-discuss-and-apply.md:108-109`. The per-value glosses were kept -- they are the shared vocabulary and are not derivable from the strings.
- **Self-caught on the cold re-read: this pass wrote "Most decisions taken during a maintenance dialogue attach to a *surviving* rule"** where the original said "Some of those decisions attach to a surviving rule". A hedge widened into a majority claim about a dialogue this module never sees, for no gain. Rewritten without a quantifier: "A decision ... that attaches to a *surviving* rule is recorded on it". Recorded because it is the predicted failure mode and it survived the first two re-reads.
- **A note in this file about `--record-decision`'s batch behaviour or its exit codes** -- considered and rejected. It is `maintenance.py`'s contract, it is already in `technical-notes.md`, and queue rows M5 and EL cover the defects; a third copy here would drift.

## Code-level defects found during #07 on `toolguard/tools/config_access.py` (2026-08-12)

Comments-only pass -- **nothing below was fixed.** Rows are lettered `CA*`; the numeric sequence and `AE`/`C`/`D`/`DG`/`DL`/`E`/`EL`/`GA`/`HR`/`INS`/`IU`/`LH`/`M`/`MI`/`N`/`OP`/`P`/`R`/`RA`/`RD`/`RS`/`S`/`SA`/`T`/`TCV`/`TL`/`UR`/`V` are already claimed by concurrent per-file passes. Every row was executed live against the module on this branch, 2026-08-12, in a temp tree with `HOME` redirected.

| # | defect | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CA1** | **`per_layer_rules` hides every native `permissions.ask` rule, and those rules are live at runtime.** The line is `ask = tl.ask if (tl is not None and not layer.is_native) else ()`. `Configuration.permission_layers` extracts `permissions.get("ask", [])` from every layer with no native/toolguard distinction, and `permission_levels_with_provenance` -- which `resolve_command_permission` consumes at `permission_resolution.py:381` -- passes each level's `ask` straight into `decide_command_at_level_detailed(..., ask_patterns=list(ask))`. So a native ask rule DOES decide, and the tooling view of the config says the layer has none. Executed 2026-08-12 on a two-layer in-memory config whose native layer holds `permissions.ask = ["Bash(git push:*)"]`: `permission_layers('Bash')` returns `ask=('git push:*',)` for the native layer, `per_layer_rules(config, 'Bash')` returns `ask=()` for it, and `audit_context(...).tools[...].layers[...].ask` inherits the empty tuple. The pre-sweep docstring justified the drop with "native Claude settings have no `ask` concept", which is not true of Claude Code's `settings.json` and is not what `permission_layers` implements. | `toolguard/tools/config_access.py:per_layer_rules`, the `not layer.is_native` guard; inherited by `LayerContext.ask` and by `security_audit --with-context` | The consequence lands on the security audit and the maintenance skill: a native ask rule is invisible to both, so neither can report it, reconcile it against a toolguard rule, or notice it shadowing one. Fix is to delete the guard -- one expression -- and **nothing in the suite pins the current behaviour**: `test_per_layer_rules_native_layer_has_no_ask` (`test/unit/test_tools_config_access.py:178-204`) writes a native `settings.json` of `{"permissions": {"allow": ["Bash(ls:*)"]}}`, with no `ask` key at all, and then asserts `assertEqual((), lr.ask)`, which passes either way. Its Given/When/Then carries the false premise verbatim -- "(native settings have no ask concept)" -- and `test/` was outside this pass's scope, so it is flagged, not fixed. Prose fixed here: `LayerRules.ask` and `per_layer_rules` now state that this view drops them **and** that `permission_layers` returns them, with no claim that native ask does not exist. |
| **CA2** | **`_layer_comment_map` guards `OSError` only, and a non-UTF-8 layer file raises `UnicodeDecodeError`, which is a `ValueError`.** `Path(provenance.path).read_text(encoding="utf-8")` decodes as well as opens; only the opening half is caught. Executed 2026-08-12 on a `[permissions]` file written as latin-1 (`"Bash(echo café:*)",  # réason`): `_layer_comment_map(prov)` raised `UnicodeDecodeError: 'utf-8' codec can't decode byte 0xe9 in position 40: invalid continuation byte`. Every other degraded case returns `{}` -- non-TOML layer, missing file, no `[permissions]` section, and a section that fails to parse as TOML (a multi-line structured entry, verified in the same run) -- so this is the one input that escapes a function whose whole contract is best-effort enrichment. **Reachability is narrow, and I first got this wrong in the other direction.** I wrote that the failure was asymmetric (rules load, enrichment explodes) and then measured it: `load_configuration` over a project holding that same latin-1 file returns `layers = []` and `parse_failures = ((<path>, "'utf-8' codec can't decode byte 0xe9 ..."),)` after warning "Failed to load ... Fix or remove ...". A file that cannot be decoded therefore never becomes a `ConfigLayer`, so no provenance for it ever reaches this function on the normal path. What is left is the re-read window -- this function opens the file again, later, from a provenance produced by an earlier successful load -- plus any caller that synthesises a `Provenance` for a file it did not load through `load_configuration`. | `toolguard/tools/config_access.py:_layer_comment_map`, the `except OSError` at `:442` | Downgraded to a robustness gap rather than a live bug, on that measurement. Still worth closing, because the blast radius is disproportionate: the exception propagates through `rule_comments_for_tool` into `audit_context`, aborting a whole `security_audit --with-context` run over one mis-encoded comment, and CA3's per-(tool, layer) re-reading widens the window it can be hit in. Two candidate fixes, not equivalent: `except (OSError, UnicodeDecodeError)` degrades to no comments for that layer, matching the rest of the function; `read_text(encoding="utf-8", errors="replace")` keeps the comments and mangles the non-ASCII ones, which for a `#NOSECURITY` *reason* is arguably the better loss. The docstring's pre-sweep "returns an empty map, **and never raises**" was false on exactly this input and now enumerates the guarded cases without the universal. |
| **CA3** | **`audit_context` re-reads and re-parses each TOML layer file once per (tool, layer) pair.** `rule_comments_for_tool` calls `_layer_comment_map(provenance)`, which does a fresh `read_text` + `find_section_boundaries` + `parse_permissions_section_with_comments` on every call, and `audit_context` calls it inside a `for tool in discover_tools(config)` / `for lr in layer_rules` double loop. Measured 2026-08-12 by counting `Path.read_text` calls on one layer file during a single `audit_context`: **2 reads for 2 discovered tools and 1 layer** -- i.e. `len(tools) * len(layers)`, so a routine 4-tool / 5-layer hierarchy re-reads and re-parses the same handful of files 20 times. `nosecurity_reason_for` has the same shape per rule, and `security_audit` calls it per finding. | `toolguard/tools/config_access.py:_layer_comment_map`, called from `rule_comments_for_tool` and `nosecurity_reason_for` | Correctness is unaffected and the files are small, so this is a design smell rather than a bug -- but it is the reason CA2 is a whole-run abort rather than one bad lookup. A per-`Provenance` memo built once in `audit_context` and passed down, or an `@lru_cache` on `_layer_comment_map` keyed by the frozen `Provenance`, removes both the repetition and the repeated exposure. Prefer the explicit parameter: a module-level cache in a per-tool-call process is fine at runtime but leaks across cases in the in-process test/replay harness. No prose about this was added -- it is a property of the call graph, which is exactly what rule 1 says not to write down. |

### Comment defect in a file outside this pass's scope

- `test/unit/test_tools_config_access.py:178-183` -- `test_per_layer_rules_native_layer_has_no_ask`'s Given/When/Then ends "**(native settings have no ask concept)**", which is false: Claude Code's `settings.json` has an `ask` list, `Configuration.permission_layers` extracts it from native layers like any other, and `resolve_command_permission` decides on it. The Then is also weaker than it reads -- the fixture writes no `ask` key, so the assertion cannot distinguish the guard being present from it being absent. Both halves want fixing with CA1, and the test tree was frozen for this pass.

### Rejected during this pass, with evidence

- **The brief's headline defect -- the "TOO-19 correctness fix" narrative paragraphs at `:107-121` and `:210-231`** -- **real, but already gone before this pass started.** Those line numbers are `git show HEAD:toolguard/tools/config_access.py` (796 lines); the working tree was already at 688 lines and both paragraphs had been cut by an earlier pass, along with the six-line "mirrored here exactly as allow/deny's own takeover/native handling lives in `permission_layers()` itself" block inside the loop body. Verified against the HEAD blob rather than assumed. Nothing was left to remove; what survived of the current behaviour was re-verified instead (CA1 is what that verification turned up).
- **"Bundles what the deterministic analyzers see into one **serializable** object" (`AuditContext`) and "An `AuditContext` **ready for serialization**" (`audit_context` `Returns:`)** -- **false, and both were cut rather than qualified.** `AuditContext` has no `to_dict`/`to_json`, and `json.dumps(dataclasses.asdict(ctx))` raises `TypeError: Object of type PosixPath is not JSON serializable` (executed 2026-08-12) because `ConfigSummary.start_dir`/`project_root` are `Path`. The one real consumer proves it: `security_audit.main` hand-assembles an ~80-line dict literal, calling `str()` on both paths and on `takeover.conflict`. The rest of the paragraph was an enumeration of the four attributes listed immediately below it, so the whole thing went and the `Attributes:` block now stands alone.
- **"it returns an empty map, **and never raises**"** (`_layer_comment_map`) -- false; see CA2. Replaced with the enumerated guarded cases. Note the trap in the wording: the old text said "a file that is absent **or unreadable**", and a latin-1 file is fair to call unreadable, so the sentence read as though it covered the case it did not. The replacement says "missing or **fails to open**", which is what `except OSError` actually guards.
- **"a structured entry names its tool exactly as a plain string does -- but only in a `toolguard_hook` layer"** (`discover_tools`) -- **false by narrowing, and this pass fixed it.** `normalize_entry`'s gate is `is_native`, and `ConfigLayer.is_native` is `source_type == "claude"`, so a `toolguard_hook_rules` layer (a rules-directory file) recognises structured entries exactly as a `toolguard_hook` layer does. Naming one of the two non-native source types excluded the other. Now stated as the negative the code implements: "except in a native layer".
- **"the raw lists are the only place they still exist"** (`audit_context`, on the takeover-suppressed patterns) -- **a false universal introduced by compression, cut.** `takeover.ignored_allow_patterns` holds the same strings, and `TakeoverConfig` is right there in the same object. What is load-bearing is only the contrast, which survives: the value comes from raw `layer.content`, not from `per_layer_rules`, "which has already dropped the takeover-suppressed patterns". Verified in the same run -- native `allow` under takeover is `('ls:*',)` from `per_layer_rules` while `neutralized_allow_patterns` is `('*',)`.
- **"Such a pattern is still written in the config file but **grants nothing**"** (`neutralized_by_takeover`) -- the mechanism is true and was kept; the meaning claim was replaced by the mechanism that supports it ("takeover mode strips it from the native layer's allow list before any resolution reads it"). Checked before rewriting, because the sentence would have been the file's only outward guarantee: both resolution entry points reach it -- `permission_levels_with_provenance` (`config.py:1126`) and `allow_deny_for` (`config.py:1381`) each iterate `permission_layers`, which applies the filter at `config.py:1067-1072`. So the stronger form was defensible; it is still a *meaning* stated where a *mechanism* says the same thing and cannot rot.
- **The three-condition enumeration in `neutralized_by_takeover`** -- already cut by the earlier pass, and **the cut was right**: the body is a single three-term `and`, and the numbered list was rule 2 (explaining short simple code) plus rule 0 (it also asserted this was "the single source of truth" for a rule `danger.py` "formerly applied inline"). Re-verified that exactly three conditions are checked, matching the correction the brief reports on `danger.py`.
- **`RuleComment.pattern` "the same form as `LayerContext.allow`/`deny`/`ask`"** -- **kept, after executing it**, because the `#NOSECURITY` lookup silently returns `None` if the two ever disagree. `RuleComment` keys on `_split_tool_pattern` (split at the first `(`, require a trailing `)`); `LayerContext` patterns come via `rule_entry._strip_tool_wrapper` (`fullmatch` on `[A-Za-z0-9_]+\((.*)\)`). Executed 2026-08-12, the two agree on `Bash(git *)`, `Bash([regex]^git (push|pull))`, `Read(/a/(b)/c)`, `mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command(git *)` and `Bash(echo )x)`; end-to-end, `nosecurity_reason_for(prov, 'allow', 'Bash', '[regex]^git (push|pull)')` returns `'reviewed'`. They diverge only where the "tool" is not `[A-Za-z0-9_]+` (`_split_tool_pattern('(foo)')` -> `('', 'foo')` where `_strip_tool_wrapper` leaves `'(foo)'` intact), which no tool-scoped list can reach. Not filed as a defect: no evidence it can bite.
- **The `is_native` gloss "(`provenance.source_type == 'claude'`)", repeated on `LayerContext.is_native` and on `neutralized_by_takeover`'s parameter** -- considered as a one-fact-one-home violation and **kept in both places.** `neutralized_by_takeover` takes a bare `bool` a caller must compute, so telling that caller how is the point; `LayerContext.is_native` is the stored value. Deleting either would send the reader to `ConfigLayer.is_native` in another module for a five-token fact.
- **A note here about `with_layer_rules_replaced` matching `Provenance` by whole-dataclass equality** -- considered and rejected as a second home. This *is* the site of the comparison, but queue row `AE3` already records the defect and `edit_proposal.RuleEdit.provenance`'s docstring already states the requirement to the caller who can act on it. A third copy would drift.
- **Trivial `Args:`/`Returns:` blocks on the thin delegators** -- `effective_takeover` and `config_summary` are now one-line docstrings. Their `Returns:` blocks described `TakeoverConfig` and `ConfigSummary`, which is those types' own job, and their `Args:` block said "config: The resolved configuration" for a parameter annotated `config: Configuration`. `with_layer_allow_replaced`'s five-entry `Args:` block was a verbatim copy of the delegate's and is now one line pointing at it. Matches the accepted exemplars, where `Provenance.describe` and `ConfigLayer.is_native` carry a single line and no sections.
