- Wrote "Decided by `is_test_path`, plus `_root_forces_test_context` for a root that is itself inside a test-like directory" -- **false for git-diff mode**, which calls `is_test_path` alone (there is no analysis root; paths are repo-relative). Qualified to the two tree modes, with git-diff named as the exception.
- Wrote "Files present only in OLD are reported as removed" (compressing the pre-existing wording, which had the same defect) -- **false**: `removed` subtracts `claimed_move_sources`, so an old path whose content matches a new path is diffed as a move, not reported as removed. Rewritten to say so.
- Wrote "Stdlib only, like the rest of this project" -- the *runtime* is stdlib-only; dev tooling is not. Narrowed to "like toolguard's own runtime".

### CRC: verdict on the file's own standing

**Live, demoted, and truthful about itself -- but silent on the demotion.** The file claims retirement for exactly one thing, the CONDUIT-to-DECISION ratio, and that claim is true and consistent everywhere it appears. It nowhere claims to be retired, deprecated or unused, so there was nothing of that kind to correct. Evidence that it is live: `test/unit/test_change_role_classifier.py` imports it and runs inside the 2733; `reports/micro-canary-protocol.md:197` assigns it a current job -- *"The classifier's job is now to produce the complete, exact, auditable list of changed locations"*. Evidence that it is demoted: the same document's `## STOP -- mechanical scoring is abandoned` section (line 174) and M1's own amendment (line 83), *"M1 is demoted from headline to supporting"*. Its continued existence in the tree is an open question, not a settled one -- `proposed-tickets/06-measurement-tools-keep-or-remove.md` records that it was tracked by an accidental `git add -A` and lists remove / keep / keep-with-README as live options. **None of that is stated in the file, and per rule 0 none of it was added**: every sentence of it is a claim about documents outside the module, which is where this codebase's falsehoods reliably live. It belongs to the ticket.

### CRC: the anti-correlation question -- the finding is real and I found its source

The brief records that `touch_set_inventory.py` pointed at `touch_set_score.py` for a finding that "role labels anti-correlated with code quality", that the pointer resolves to nothing, and that the finding has no locatable source. **The pointer is still wrong, but the finding has a source, and this module states the mechanism itself.**

- **Source:** `toolguard-memories/TOO-45/proposed-tickets/06-measurement-tools-keep-or-remove.md:20` -- *"The role classifier's **headline number was anti-correlated with code quality** -- factored code scored worse than copy-paste."* That is the claim, almost verbatim, about this module, in a document neither previous editor looked in.
- **Corroborated inside the file, as a mechanism rather than a slogan:** `_governing_role`'s docstring describes a terminal CONDUIT causing `if bool(x):`, `if x.enabled:` and `if x.is_ready():` to score as pure transport -- *"the tool preferred copy-pasted inline checks over factored predicates"*. That sentence is the anti-correlation, named at the defect that produced it, and it is stated as **fixed** (the dual CONDUIT+DECISION role). The ratio was retired anyway, on a separate argument that survives the fix: a mechanical `sed 's/entry\.subject)/entry.subject or None)/g'` moves a tree from the worst reading to the best.
- **The "M2 still uses it" claim needs correcting on both sides.** `micro-canary-protocol.md:71` sits under `### M1 -- Role profile (mechanical)` (line 69), not M2 (line 85). So the surviving dependency is M1's, not M2's -- and M1 was explicitly demoted to supporting after failing to discriminate on the auto-mode canary (identical 9 CONDUIT / 2 DECISION / 5 WRITE on both trees). Queue lines 980 and 1041 attribute it to M2; that attribution is wrong, though the underlying point -- that the classifier is still named in a live protocol -- holds.

**Nothing was written into the module about any of this.** The sourced anti-correlation statement is a claim about a proposed ticket and a protocol document; the mechanism was already in `_governing_role` and stayed there.

### CRC: rejected from the brief, with evidence

- **"If it claims ASCII output"** -- no such claim, and none needed: `grep -in ascii tools/change_role_classifier.py` -> no match, and the file is pure ASCII (0 bytes > 127, measured). Nothing to correct in either direction.
- **"a component documented elsewhere as removed-for-anti-correlating is still feeding a live protocol, and the anti-correlation finding has no locatable source"** -- half rejected. The pointer in `touch_set_inventory.py` does aim at nothing, but the finding itself is sourced (see above), and the live protocol dependency is M1's, not M2's. Nothing was invented to make a pointer true, and nothing was added to this module.
- **"expect several [refactoring candidates] in 2628 lines"** -- partly. Two are offered below rather than several: the role-classification core is a flat `match` over AST parent/field pairs and reads well at any length, and the heaviest comment clustering in the file is inside `KNOWN_LIMITATIONS`, which is a string list, not a function.

### CRC: refactoring candidate

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CRC-R1** | `change_role_classifier._find_opaque_hops_in_scope` | The docstring **numbers** what the function does -- "1. an assignment ..." and "2. a return/yield ..." -- and the body is a single loop with a mid-body `continue` acting as the divider between the two unrelated detectors, plus a `scope_is_tracked` flag that only the second half reads. This is the shape the standard names: two things in one body, each needing its own paragraph. CRC1 (the yield triple-count) hid in the second half precisely because the two detectors share a loop and a result list, so the `ast.walk` in one of them is not visibly different from the `isinstance` in the other. | Two private functions -- `_assignment_opaque_hops(flat_stmts, tracked)` and `_return_opaque_hops(flat_stmts, tracked)` -- and a three-line caller that concatenates them and drops the second when the scope is tracked. Each numbered paragraph becomes the docstring of the function it describes, and the yield scan's traversal becomes reviewable on its own. |
| **CRC-R2** | `change_role_classifier._analyze_tree` | 278 lines, **14 top-level `for` loops over `_TreeIndex` lists, 12 of them ending in a `record(...)` call**, each differing only in which index list it walks, which attribute holds the name (`.id`/`.attr`/`.arg`/`.name`/`.asname`/`.rest`/`.names`), the `kind` label, the fixed role set and a one-line note string. Two of the twelve carry real logic (Name, with alias tracking; alias nodes, with the `name`-or-`asname` choice); the other ten are the same four lines transcribed. That transcription is what let `def-name` fall out of the `Occurrence.kind` enumeration (CRC-F6) -- the kind is written once per loop and nowhere collected. | A table of simple extractors -- `(index_attr, name_getter, kind, roles, note)` -- driving one loop, with the two loops that carry logic left as they are. The table then becomes the single source for the kind vocabulary, which the `Occurrence.kind` comment could point at instead of restating. |

## AF — from the comment sweep of `tools/architecture_fitness.py` (2026-08-12)

Editor pass on the architecture-fitness enforcer, the last file in the `tools/` tier and the largest in the repo after the generated parser. 4069 -> 3515 lines. `--compare-against HEAD` is unusable on this file (`is_generated_file()` was extracted into `tools/generated_files.py` earlier in the ticket, a real code change), so the gate was a **private `code_shape()` baseline taken before the first edit and re-taken after the last: byte-identical, 300,345 bytes**. `ruff format`/`check` clean, suite 2733 OK. Every claim below was executed against the live tree, not read off the module.

### AF: code-level defects (comments-only sweep could not fix these)

| # | defect | evidence | where |
|---|---|---|---|
| **AF1** | **`R2_UNCHECKED_CLAUSES` is incomplete, and a docstring asserted the missing entry was in it.** R2's predicate has three clauses (execution plan line 156: "no parallel arrays on `ToolPatternLayer`; stripped patterns are a derived property of `RuleEntry`; no prose-defended index-alignment invariant remains"). The register holds one entry, for clause 2. Clause 3 is covered only by `find_drift_guards`, which is explicitly a one-directional proxy -- and its own docstring said *"see `R2_UNCHECKED_CLAUSES` for that explicit admission, rather than a silent gap"*. The admission is not there: the tuple's single member's `clause` key is `"stripped patterns are a derived property of RuleEntry"`. **A register of known blind spots that is itself missing an entry, with a pointer claiming otherwise.** The docstring is fixed (the limit is now stated where it is true); adding the clause-3 entry is a code change and was not made. | `tools/architecture_fitness.py:R2_UNCHECKED_CLAUSES`, `find_drift_guards` |
| **AF2** | **Frozen string `fan_in_caveat` states a fan-in figure that has since moved.** It reads *"permissions/compound/resolve show fan-in 2 (looking like leaves)"*. Executed `af.fan_in(af.build_import_graph())`: **`permissions` is 4**, `compound` 2, `resolve` 2. The argument the caveat makes (import-graph fan-in disagrees with co-change; do not read it alone) is unaffected and still correct -- only the parenthetical figure is stale. Frozen under the comments-only rule, unchanged. | `tools/architecture_fitness.py:compute_metrics`, the `fan_in_caveat` value |
| **AF3** | **`--metrics` and `--predicates` disagree about the parser cycle, and nothing says so.** `compute_predicates` calls `find_import_cycles(graph, out_of_scope_packages=R5_OUT_OF_SCOPE_PACKAGES)`; `compute_metrics` calls `find_import_cycles(graph)` with the default `()`. Executed: R5-scoped `[]`, unfiltered `[['parser.multiline', 'parser.command_extractor']]`. So one mode reports `import_cycle_count: 1` while the other reports R5 PASS with zero cycles, from the same graph in the same run. Defensible (metrics measure raw structure, predicates score a step) and now stated in `find_import_cycles`' docstring, but it is a genuine reporting trap for anyone reading both sections. | `tools/architecture_fitness.py:compute_metrics` vs `compute_predicates` |
| **AF4** | **The CLI `--help` description is a truncated sentence fragment.** `main()` does `description=__doc__.strip().splitlines()[0]`, so `--help` prints only the module docstring's first physical line. At HEAD that was *"Dev-only instrument (repo-root ``tools/``, NOT shipped -- see ``pyproject.toml``'s"* -- it ends mid-possessive. Mitigated as a side effect of this sweep (the first line is now a complete sentence), but the mechanism is unchanged: any future first-line rewrap silently breaks `--help` again, and no test covers it. | `tools/architecture_fitness.py:main` |

Changed to "live false positives ... e.g.", avoiding both a false singular and a count that would need maintaining.

### AF: refactoring candidate

| # | function | signal | shape of the fix |
|---|---|---|---|
| **AF-R1** | `architecture_fitness.compute_predicates` | The function assembles a **125-line nested dict literal** in a single `return`, with five sibling predicate blocks that share a shape (`pass`, the component lists, `out_of_scope_excluded` with a hand-written `reason` string) and diverge only in detail. Three of the five spell out a near-identical `out_of_scope_excluded` block with three near-identical prose reasons -- that triplication is where the inline comments clustered, and all three were narrating the same exclusion. `render_predicates_text` is the matching signal from the other side: a single 190-line function whose body is one `if/elif` chain over the same five ids, each branch reaching into the dict shape by string key with nothing checking the two agree. | One small builder per predicate returning its own block (`_r1_block(...)`, `_r2_block(...)`, ...), with the shared `out_of_scope_excluded` built once by a helper taking the predicate id. `compute_predicates` becomes the dict of five calls plus the footprint. The renderer's branches then have an obvious per-predicate counterpart to sit next to, rather than being keyed to a literal written 200 lines away. |

## TQ — from the comment sweep of the first 11 `test/unit/` files (2026-08-12)

Editor pass over `test_tools_environment_audit.py`, `test_tools_migration_gate.py`, `test_session_warnings.py`, `test_tools_working_tree.py`, `test_static_analysis_coverage.py`, `test_git_helper.py`, `test_recommended_protections.py`, `test_permission_resolution.py`, `test_tools_corpus.py`, `test_tools_self_permission.py`, `test_error_log.py`. 1,305 -> 1,171 lines. Comments and docstrings only; `--compare-against HEAD` reports zero drift on all eleven, `ruff format`/`check` clean, suite **2733 OK** (unchanged).

### TQ: vacuous or self-satisfying tests (code changes, NOT made here)

| # | test | why the assertion cannot fail |
|---|---|---|
| **TQ1** | `test_git_helper.py:TestSharedConstants` — **all three tests**: `test_update_check_dist_name_is_the_shared_constant`, `test_install_provenance_dist_name_is_the_shared_constant`, `test_run_git_default_timeout_is_the_shared_constant` | Each is an `assertIs` against an **interned literal**. `DIST_NAME = "toolguard"` is identifier-shaped, so CPython interns it at compile time; `GIT_TIMEOUT_SECONDS = 10` is inside the small-int cache. **Measured**: a fresh module doing `x = "toolguard"` / `y = 10` gives `x is constants.DIST_NAME` -> `True` and `y is constants.GIT_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` -> `True`. The class's whole stated purpose — "the SAME constant objects, **not re-declared copies**" — is exactly the thing `assertIs` cannot see here. Replacing the three `from toolguard.constants import ... as _DEFAULT_*` aliases with re-declared literals leaves all three green. **Shape of a real fix**: assert the import binding, not the value — e.g. `self.assertIs(sys.modules["toolguard.install_update"].__dict__["_DEFAULT_DIST_NAME"], ...)` does not help either; the honest check is an AST/`ast.parse` scan for a literal assignment, or drop the tests as untestable and rely on the import. Docstrings were corrected to claim only what is asserted. |
| **TQ2** | `test_session_warnings.py:test_does_not_call_log_warning` | Asserts an absence the fixture cannot produce. `toolguard/session_warnings.py` imports **only `sys`** — it never references `toolguard.error_log` in any form, so `patch("toolguard.error_log.log_warning")` installs a mock on a module the code under test never reaches. `mock_log.assert_not_called()` is unconditionally true. Deleting `issue_takeover_warning`'s body entirely leaves it green; so would adding a `log_warning` call under a `from ... import` binding, since patching the *source* module would still miss it. |
| **TQ3** | `test_tools_environment_audit.py:test_defaults_to_os_environ_when_env_not_given` | Asserts `audit_environment() == []` under `patch.dict(os.environ, {}, clear=True)`. The empty result is what **every** possible default produces once PYTHONPATH is gone (`os.environ`, a literal `{}`, `None`-guarded early return). The stated claim, "it delegates to `os.environ`", is not pinned by anything. **A fixture that would pin it**: set `PYTHONPATH` in the patched environ to a temp dir holding a `toolguard/__init__.py` and assert one HIGH finding — presence, not absence. The docstring was narrowed to what the assertion establishes. |

### TQ: near-vacuous, kept as a note

- **`test_tools_self_permission.py:test_no_hook_entry_points_present`** — three `assertNotIn`s that are wholly implied by its sibling `test_only_audit_and_maintain_are_declared`, which already asserts the command set is **exactly** `{toolguard-audit, toolguard-maintain}`. Not vacuous (a table change fails it), but it cannot fail without the sibling failing first. Redundant rather than defective; listed only so a future reader does not mistake it for independent coverage.

### TQ: stale or wrong Given/When/Then and prose, corrected

- **`test_recommended_protections.py:9-14` — a RED-PHASE NOTE for a module that has existed for months.** Verbatim: *"as of this commit toolguard/tools/recommended_protections.py does not exist yet. Every test in this module is expected to fail (ImportError at collection time...)"*. `toolguard/tools/recommended_protections.py` is present (5,357 bytes) and all five tests pass. Cut.
- **`test_permission_resolution.py` class docstring — a stale line reference.** It located `_apply_ask_floor` at *"`permission_resolution.py:160`"*; the function is defined at **line 130**. The line number is cut rather than corrected — it is pure drift risk. The incident it records (deleting the `or resolved.decision == "deny"` guard left the suite green) was verified against the module and kept, compressed.
- **`test_git_helper.py` module docstring — the TOO-19 archaeology the brief flagged.** 27 lines narrating five pre-refactor `subprocess.run` call sites, then a second paragraph correcting the first ("TOO-45 R5c split `update_check.py` ... so the `update_check` side now targets `install_update`"). A docstring that has to correct itself is two facts where one belongs. Reduced to 6 lines naming the two live callers.
- **`test_git_helper.py` — three "(not a re-declared duplicate)" / "(not a re-declared '10')" claims**, disproved above (TQ1). Rewritten to "Then it is that value".
- **`test_git_helper.py` — "proving install_update.py no longer runs its own subprocess.run for this"** (twice). Overclaim: the spy proves the delegation happened, not that no other `subprocess.run` exists in the module. Narrowed to the argv assertion.
- **`test_session_warnings.py:64-73` and `:75-79` — two malformed Given/Then docstrings with no When.** Both restructured; the `(TOO-8 Phase 4)` ticket reference was dropped per the docstring rule.
- **`test_tools_self_permission.py:7` — "All tests use stdlib unittest with BDD Given/When/Then docstrings."** A restatement of `.claude/rules/testing.md` inside a test file. Cut.

### TQ: deleted (noise, no factual defect)

- **`test_session_warnings.py` — a 30-line module docstring that was 60% an inventory of eight deleted tests**, each with a parenthetical describing the behaviour it used to pin (`test_cleanup_skipped_when_none`, `test_warns_when_sqlite_unavailable`, ...). This is git's job; the file is now a 1-line docstring. Largest single deletion in the batch.
- Module-docstring essays reduced to one line each in `test_tools_environment_audit.py` (cross-referenced `test_tools_takeover_audit.py`'s coverage pattern), `test_tools_migration_gate.py`, `test_tools_working_tree.py`.
- `test_static_analysis_coverage.py` — the 19-line incident narrative compressed to 5 (the mistake is kept: one `except A, B, C:` clause silently reduced a whole-package pyscn score to 58 of 59 modules); the 5-line `#:` comment on `SANCTIONED_EXCLUSION` to 2; one inline comment restating the list comprehension below it, cut.
- `test_permission_resolution.py` — module docstring "~28 existing cascade call sites ... deliberately NOT a relocation" reduced to where the older coverage lives; `_project_bash_config`'s citation of `test/unit/CLAUDE.md` and a precedent in another test file cut (the fact that matters — zero file I/O, so no mixin — kept).
- `test_error_log.py`, `test_tools_corpus.py` — one inline comment each restating the assertion on the next line.

### TQ: rejected from the brief, with evidence

- **"Delete every other comment", applied to class docstrings and helper docstrings.** Not done. `~/.claude/rules/python.md` requires doc comments on every function and class, and one-line class docstrings (`"""resolve_logs_dir maps a project to its daily-log directory."""`) are the only thing naming what a `TestX` class groups. I read the rule as aimed at prose *beyond* the Given/When/Then, and trimmed those to one line rather than removing them. Same for one-line module docstrings.
- **Two inline comments kept** where deletion would invite a wrong "simplification": `test_static_analysis_coverage.py`'s *"The section name is pyscn's; a pyscn upgrade could move it"* (guards the fallback search against being hard-coded back to `config["analysis"]`) and `test_permission_resolution.py`'s negative-control note (explains why the sibling tests use different `no_match_fallback` values — without it the pair looks like an inconsistency). Both compressed to their load-bearing sentence.
- **`test_tools_migration_gate.py` and `test_tools_working_tree.py`: nothing substantive.** Two of the eleven were already clean — one module-docstring sentence each, no stale claims, no vacuous assertions, no inline comments. Reported rather than improved.

## TX — from the comment sweep of the second `test/unit/` batch (6 files, 2026-08-12)

Editor pass over `test_tool_spec.py`, `test_self_integrity.py`, `test_tools_project_root.py`, `test_hook_error_reporter.py`, `test_tools_hierarchy.py`, `test_command_extractor_inline_code.py`. 1,340 -> 1,270 lines. Comments and docstrings only; `--compare-against HEAD` reports zero drift on all six, `ruff format`/`check` clean, suite **2733 OK** (unchanged).

### TX: vacuous or self-satisfying tests (code changes, NOT made here)

| # | test | why the assertion cannot fail |
|---|---|---|
| **TX1** | `test_tool_spec.py:TestRegistryIntegrity.test_tools_by_name_has_one_entry_per_registry_entry` | Asserts `len(TOOLS_BY_NAME) == len(_REGISTRY)`, and its docstring claimed to catch the case where *"a duplicated name would collapse silently in a plain dict comprehension"*. It cannot. `TOOLS_BY_NAME` is built by `tool_spec._index_by_name`, which **raises `ValueError` on a duplicate name at import time** — so a duplicated `_REGISTRY` entry makes the whole module fail to import and this test *errors on collection*, never reaching the comparison. With no duplicate, the equality is an invariant of the function, not evidence about it. **Measured**: rebuilding the index as the naive `{spec.name: spec for spec in _REGISTRY}` the docstring names gives `len == 5 == len(_REGISTRY)` — i.e. the assertion is equally green with and without the guard it claims to test. The real guard is its sibling `test_duplicate_name_raises_at_index_time`, which passes a hand-built duplicated tuple to `_index_by_name` directly. **Shape of a fix**: delete this test (the sibling covers the failure mode), or, if the intent is "the module still imports and indexes the whole registry", assert `set(TOOLS_BY_NAME) == {s.name for s in _REGISTRY}` and say so. Docstring corrected here to claim only what is asserted. |

### TX: near-vacuous, kept as notes

- **`test_tool_spec.py:test_config_validation_known_supported_tools_equals_derived_view`** — `config_validation.py:12` is `KNOWN_SUPPORTED_TOOLS = KNOWN_TOOL_NAMES`, a bare alias, so the test is literally `assertEqual(x, x)`. It is not unfalsifiable (re-declaring the constant as a drifting literal would fail it), but as written it pins nothing about *today's* code. Its sibling `test_constants_module_constants_are_the_same_frozensets` does this properly with `assertIs` — and `assertIs` is sound there, unlike the interned-literal case in **TQ1**, because frozensets are never interned. Suggest `assertIs` here too.
- **`test_tool_spec.py:test_default_governed_tools_matches_builtin_tools_content`** — compares `set(DEFAULT_GOVERNED_TOOLS)` to `BUILTIN_TOOLS`, which are two textually identical comprehensions (`tool.name for tool in _REGISTRY if tool.is_builtin`) ten lines apart in the same file. Falsifiable only by editing one predicate and not the other. Low value beside the two literal pins that already exist.
- **`test_self_integrity.py:test_entries_are_frozen_dataclass_instances`** — `with self.assertRaises(Exception)`. A typo'd attribute name, or the dataclass losing `frozen=True` but gaining `__slots__`, both pass. Should be `assertRaises(dataclasses.FrozenInstanceError)`, which is what the sibling file `test_tool_spec.py:test_tool_spec_is_frozen` already uses.

### TX: coverage gaps found while checking claims against the modules

- **`test_self_integrity.py` pins none of the module's own documented escapes.** `toolguard/tools/self_integrity.py`'s docstring says outright: *"Defense in depth, not a guarantee -- e.g. `sudo rm -rf ~/.toolguard` and `/bin/rm -rf ~/.toolguard` match neither pattern"* (both regexes are `^`-anchored on `rm`/`find`). Nothing in the test file records that boundary, so a future reader has a file of green "is hard-denied" tests and no marker of where the protection stops. The compound case is the reverse — leaf decomposition **does** catch `foo && rm -rf ~/.toolguard` — and is also untested. **Fix shape**: two `subTest` blocks, one asserting the known escapes are NOT denied (a characterisation test, so tightening the pattern later fails loudly and visibly), one asserting a compound form IS.
- **`test_tools_hierarchy.py` has no test for either blind spot the module documents** — the same-specificity duplicate that `_nearest_broader_cover`'s strict `>` comparison cannot see (ticket 22), and the measured case in `find_cross_layer_redundancies`'s own docstring where removing a reported-redundant rule flips `git push` from `allow` to `deny` because an intermediate deny takes over. The class docstring was corrected (below) but the corresponding tests do not exist.
- **`test_tools_project_root.py` depends on `TMPDIR` not being under `$HOME`.** `path_utils.iter_dirs_upward` stops the walk at `Path.home()`, and this machine's `$HOME` holds `.claude` — a `STRONG_PROJECT_ANCHORS` member. With `TMPDIR` unset (the current state) the temp trees are under `/tmp` and the walk runs to `/`, so the fixtures are honest; set `TMPDIR=$HOME/tmp` and `test_no_marker_refuses` would resolve `$HOME` as `RESOLVED_ANCHOR` instead of `NONE`. Not a defect today, but it is an unstated environmental precondition in a file whose whole premise is *"a temporary directory tree so the walk-up is fully controlled"*.

### TX: stale or wrong Given/When/Then and prose, corrected

- **`test_hook_error_reporter.py` module docstring named a symbol that does not exist.** Verbatim: *"report_fault's only caller is `_report_crash_fault`, in the three except handlers -- there is no longer a module-level report_fault an arbitrary call site can reach"*. `grep -rn report_fault toolguard/ tools/` returns **nothing**; the live API is the `Reporter.fault()` method (`error_reporter.py:125`). The sentence also documented a call-graph fact, which the comment rules forbid outright. The whole 20-line docstring (two ticket/punch-list citations, a "follow-up:" change narrative, the caller enumeration) is now 6 lines stating what is under test and why the tests raise from inside `main()`'s try block.
- **`test_tool_spec.py` — three Thens naming a deleted constant.** *"then it equals the prior GOVERNED_TOOLS"*, *"the prior FILE_TOOLS"*, *"the prior KNOWN_SUPPORTED_TOOLS"*. There is no `GOVERNED_TOOLS` anywhere in the package; `FILE_TOOLS` and `KNOWN_SUPPORTED_TOOLS` exist but are aliases of the very things being compared, so "the prior X" reads as archaeology about a pre-refactor state. Rewritten to state the literal set each derived view must equal.
- **`test_self_integrity.py:test_rm_variants_targeting_toolguard_are_hard_denied` — the Given counted wrong.** It said *"three different literal command strings"*; the body iterates **five** (`rm -rf ~/…`, `rm -rf $HOME/…`, `rm -rf /Users/arnon/…`, `rm ~/.toolguard/backups/foo.toml`, `rm -r ~/.toolguard`). The `-r`-vs-`-rf` and the file-inside-the-directory cases were undescribed. Corrected to five, naming what each axis varies.
- **`test_tools_hierarchy.py:TestCrossLayerRedundancy` claimed the safety the module explicitly disclaims.** Class docstring, verbatim: *"A specific rule already present in a broader layer is redundant."* `find_cross_layer_redundancies`'s own docstring says the opposite — *"A finding is a candidate for review, not a verified-safe deletion... it cannot see that dropping the specific copy hands the decision to a layer that denies or asks the same command"* — with a measured `git push` counter-example. This is the exact over-claim the brief predicted. Rewritten to say a finding means a broader layer repeats the body, and that an intermediate deny can flip the verdict. (The three method docstrings were already accurate: all say "flagged", not "is redundant".)
- **`test_command_extractor_inline_code.py` module docstring carried a live red/green TDD instruction.** Verbatim: *"These assert the CORRECT (post-fix) behaviour and are expected to **FAIL** before the fix is applied and PASS after."* The fix has been in for a ticket; all 24 tests pass. Cut, along with *"Before this ticket the function had ZERO direct test coverage"* and the class docstring's *"must be True post-fix"*.
- **The same file's eleven identical `-- previously a bypass` clauses**, one per Then in `TestForeignInlineCodeBypasses`. Change history, restated eleven times, already covered once by the class docstring. Cut; the class docstring now names what the group is about structurally (intervening, attached and bundled flags) rather than by its position in one ticket's timeline.

### TX: deleted (noise, no factual defect)

- `test_command_extractor_inline_code.py:test_python_dash_capital_x_dev_dash_c_KNOWN_LIMITATION` — a 16-line docstring with a paragraph of scope-decision rationale and a pointer to "the TOO-19 implementation report". Reduced to 8: the structural reason `-X dev` is indistinguishable from `-m mymod`, and the one-line shape of a fix.
- `test_tools_project_root.py:test_strict_mode_nearest_marker_wins_over_tiered_default` — a 5-line meta-argument about which *other* tests a wrong implementation would still pass. Cut; the Then already states the differentiator. Same file: a cross-reference to a sibling test by name (drift risk), a `(TOO-15: ...)` ticket parenthetical (the substantive claim — `.claude` is the same trust tier as a VCS root — kept), and "calibrated" as a qualifier on a `reason` string nothing calibrates.
- `test_self_integrity.py` module docstring — 16 lines, of which 9 retold the install incident (*"going further for a true clean slate"*, the contradiction with `docs/uninstall.md`) already narrated at greater length in `toolguard/tools/self_integrity.py`'s own docstring. Reduced to 7; the one claim worth keeping — behaviour goes through the real `decide()` because a pattern that looks right but does not match is worse than none — kept.
- `test_tools_hierarchy.py` module docstring — a four-bullet inventory of what the tests cover, i.e. the class and method names one screen below. Cut to 2 lines.
- Cross-file convention citations cut: `test_self_integrity.py`'s *"matching self_permission.py's and recommended_protections.py's frozen-dataclass convention"*, `test_hook_error_reporter.py`'s three punch-list item numbers and `(see toolguard.hook.main)`.
- One inline comment restating the assertion below it (`test_hook_error_reporter.py:194`, *"What must NOT be on stderr is the decision itself."*).

### TX: rejected from the brief, with evidence

- **"Delete every other comment", applied to the fixture comment at `test_hook_error_reporter.py:168`.** Kept, compressed 6 lines -> 3. It explains why `toolguard.log_writer.require_project_root` is patched on top of the module's `TOOLGUARD_LOG_DIR` isolation: `get_env_config()` raises *before* `load_configuration()`, so the Reporter falls back to a path the module-level isolation does not cover, and an editor who removes the patch as redundant trips `_real_log_dir_guard` — the exact TOO-19 failure `.claude/rules/test-config-isolation.md` was written for. This is CLAUDE.md's "justify by the mistake, not by the code" case: simple code, easy and costly error.
- **`test_tools_hierarchy.py:240`, `json.dumps(d)  # must not raise`.** Kept. It is the only thing distinguishing a deliberate serialisation check from a dead expression statement; without it the line reads as removable.
- **Section-banner comments** (`# --- Helpers ---`, `# --- migrate_config ---`, `# --- Negative cases: must stay False ---`). Kept in both files that use them: they are navigation, not explanation, they match the convention in the production modules under test, and removing them is churn with no accuracy gain.
- **Adding the missing helper docstrings in `test_tools_hierarchy.py`.** Not done, and flagged instead. `_layer`, `_config` and `_entry` have no docstring at all, which `~/.claude/rules/python.md` requires — but the brief is explicit that adding prose to a test file is a failure, and these three are two-line literal builders whose bodies are the documentation. Noted so the omission is a decision on the record rather than an oversight.

## TB — from the comment sweep of the third `test/unit/` batch (5 files, 2026-08-12)

Editor pass over `test_tools_uninstall_readiness.py`, `test_tools_clarity.py`, `test_bash_parser.py`, `test_ask_resolution.py`, `test_zz_real_log_dir_guard.py`. 1,380 -> 1,313 lines. Comments and docstrings only; `--compare-against HEAD` reports zero drift on all five, `ruff format --check`/`check` clean, suite **2733 OK** (unchanged).

### TB: vacuous or self-satisfying tests (code changes, NOT made here)

| # | test | why the assertion cannot fail |
|---|---|---|
| **TB1** | `test_tools_clarity.py:TestCrossLayerInteraction.test_same_specificity_layers_are_not_cross_layer` | **The allow is stripped upstream before the analyzer sees it.** The fixture builds two `ConfigLayer`s with `_make_provenance(0)` twice — `Provenance` is a frozen dataclass, so the two are `==` and hash equal. `config_access.per_layer_rules` builds `prov_to_tool_layer = {tl.provenance: tl for tl in tool_layers}`, so the second layer's entry **overwrites the first**. **Measured**: `per_layer_rules` returns two `LayerRules` both carrying `allow=()`, `deny=('git push:*',)` — the allow `git:*` is gone from every layer, and `find_confusing_interactions` returns `[]` for *all four* kinds, not just cross-layer. Deleting the `if guard_spec == allow_spec: continue` guard in `_cross_layer_findings` — the only thing this test exists to pin — leaves it green, because there is no allow left to pair with a guard. **Shape of a fix**: give the two provenances different `path`s (`/fake/a/...`, `/fake/b/...`) at the same specificity. Measured with that fixture: the allow survives (`allow=('git:*',)` in layer a, `deny=('git push:*',)` in layer b), the result is still `[]`, and removing the guard would then produce a `cross-layer-dependent` finding. That fixture also matches the Given, which said "two DIFFERENT files" while building one. |
| **TB2** | `test_bash_parser.py` — **13 of its 18 tests** (`test_simple_command_structure`, `test_pipe_structure`, `test_and_operator_structure`, `test_or_operator_structure`, `test_semicolon_structure`, `test_subshell_in_compound`, `test_nested_brace_groups`, `test_simple_dollar_paren`, `test_simple_backtick`, `test_nested_substitution`, `test_subshell_with_pipe`, `test_brace_with_and`, `test_substitution_in_subshell`) | Three unfalsifiable idioms, used alone. (a) `assertIsNotNone(tree)`: the generated `Parser.parse` either returns a node or **raises `ParseError`** — it has no `None` return, so a grammar break surfaces as a test *error*, never a failure. (b) `assertTrue(hasattr(tree, "compound_command"))` and the deeper `hasattr(compound, "pipeline")` / `hasattr(pipeline, "pipeline_element")`: these are canopy-generated node attributes from `program <- line_ws compound_command:statement ...`, `compound_command <- pipeline ...`, `pipeline <- pipeline_element ...` — every successful parse of every input has them. **Measured** over all nine inputs used in the file: `text==input`, `has compound_command`, `cc has pipeline`, `pl has pipeline_element` are all `True` for `ls -la` through `{ { cmd1; }; }`. (c) `assertIn("$(", tree.text)` / `assertIn("`", tree.text)`: `parse` only returns when `self._offset == self._input_size`, so `tree.text` **is** the input string the test just wrote — the assertion is about the literal, not the parse. Four of the thirteen (`test_nested_brace_groups`, `test_subshell_with_pipe`, `test_brace_with_and`, `test_substitution_in_subshell`) contain nothing else at all. **Shape of a fix**: they are smoke tests wearing assertions — either say so (call `parse` bare, with the docstring stating that not raising is the check) or assert something a wrong parse would change, as the five surviving tests do (`assertIn("&&", inner.text)`, `assertIn("cmd2", inner.text)`, `hasattr(elem, "compound_command")` on a subshell element, which a `simple_command` parse would fail). The strongest version asserts the extracted leaf commands from `command_extractor`, which is what the grammar exists to feed. |
| **TB3** | `test_tools_clarity.py:test_non_default_guard_is_skipped` | The asserted `[]` is what **every** alternative produces. The fixture's deny is `[regex]^uv run`, and `default_prefix_tokens` returns `None` for it twice over: once at the `ptype != PatternType.DEFAULT` check (the branch the test names) and again because the body has no `:*` args part. **Measured**: `default_prefix_tokens('[regex]^uv run')` -> `None`, `'uv run'` -> `None`, `'uv run:*'` -> `['uv','run']`. Even with the DEFAULT check removed the tokens would be `['[regex]^uv','run']` (or `['^uv','run']`), whose first token differs from the allow's, so `prefixes_overlap` is `False` and the finding still never appears. **Shape of a fix**: use a guard whose marker-free body *is* prefix-comparable — `[glob]uv run:*` or `[native]uv run:*`. Then the ptype check is the only thing suppressing the finding, and deleting it fails the test. The docstring here was narrowed to state only that no finding is made. |

### TB: near-vacuous, kept as notes

- **`test_ask_resolution.py:test_blanket_ask_with_no_allow_collapses_to_no_match_fallback` and `:test_blanket_file_ask_collapses_to_no_match_fallback`** — both assert `'ask'`, which is *both* what the blanket-ask exclusion plus the default `no_match_fallback` produces *and* what a blanket ask would produce if it were matched normally. The test name's distinction is therefore unpinned. They are not unfalsifiable (the old, wrong "collapses to deny" model would fail them), which is why they are here and not above. **Fix shape, measured**: add `no_match_fallback='deny'` to the layer and assert `'deny'` — `blanket ask only, fallback=deny -> deny`, `fallback=allow -> allow`, while `specific ask only, fallback=deny -> ask`. Three lines, and the exclusion is then pinned in both directions.
- **`test_zz_real_log_dir_guard.py:test_guard_fires_for_is_claimed_against_the_unpatched_real_store`** — `assertFalse(result)` cannot fail: with the guard removed, `is_claimed` against a synthetic kind/scope returns `False` anyway (and **measured**, `~/.toolguard/once_per.db` does not exist on this machine at all). The leak-event assertion beside it is the real check.
- **The same file's `test_guard_fires_for_claim_against_the_unpatched_real_store`** — its `before_exists == after_exists` check discriminates *today* only because the real db is absent; once that file exists, an unguarded `claim` would write rows into it without changing existence, and the assertion goes silent. Its sibling `test_guard_records_and_suppresses_a_real_dir_write_attempt` already handles that case by falling back to an mtime comparison when the file pre-exists. Same treatment (mtime, or a row count) would keep this one honest.
- **`test_tools_uninstall_readiness.py:test_fully_seeded_config_needs_no_action`** — its `ask = [... for p in perms if p.list_type == "ask"]` is always empty, because the sibling test asserts every entry is `allow`. A dead branch in a fixture, harmless but misleading about what the seeded config contains.

### TB: coverage gaps found while checking claims against the modules

- **Nothing in the suite pins the single-token/multi-token distinction that `uninstall_readiness.py`'s docstring now describes** (ticket 18). `grep` for `commit-tree`, `-BACKUP`, `toolguard-other` across `test/unit/` returns nothing. **Measured against the real matcher**: `rm -rf <skilldir>:*` matches `rm -rf <skilldir>`, `rm -rf <skilldir> /home/x/projects` **and** `rm -rf <skilldir>-BACKUP`, all `True`. **Fix shape**: a characterisation test in `test_tools_uninstall_readiness.py` asserting the three matches above (so tightening `match_command` later fails loudly and visibly), plus one asserting `cd:*` and the two literal `Write`/`Edit` paths do *not* over-grant. That is the whole of the over-grant claim, pinned in one `subTest` block.
- **`test_tools_clarity.py` has no test where two layers at the same specificity come from different files** — TB1's fix fixture is also the missing coverage: it is the only shape that exercises `per_layer_rules` returning two distinct rule sets at one level, which is what the real hierarchy produces (`toolguard_hook.toml` + `toolguard_hook.local.toml`).

### TB: stale or wrong Given/When/Then and prose, corrected

- **`test_ask_resolution.py:1-17` — the module docstring the brief flagged, on both its citation and its model.** It cited *"Toolguard's documented within-file model (see the clarity analyzer docstring)"* and then stated that model as *"a broad `ask` with no matching allow is excluded from level-matching"*. That is not the rule: `decide_command_at_level_detailed` excludes an ask iff `is_universal_pattern` says it is a blanket `*`-class rule, and it does so **before** looking at the allow list — whether an allow matches has nothing to do with it. The file's own `test_specific_ask_with_no_allow_prompts` disproves the old wording (a specific ask with no allow prompts by *matching*, not by falling through). Rewritten to the corrected model, which now agrees with `clarity.py`'s docstring verbatim in structure: deny wins in its layer however specific, otherwise more-specific-of-allow-and-ask with the tie to ask, blanket `*`-class ask excluded. The citation is now accurate; the `is_universal_pattern` reference points at the code rather than at another docstring.
- **The same docstring's change history** — *"(regression: it was silently ignored)"* and *"Before the fix the `ask` list was never consulted during matching, so a rule in `[permissions.ask]` had no effect at all"*. Cut per the docstring rule; git holds it.
- **`test_ask_resolution.py:TestFilePathAskResolution` named the wrong module.** It said the tests *"drive resolve.py's file-path level resolver"*. `grep -rn decide_file_path_at_level_detailed toolguard/*.py`: it is defined in `file_matching.py:131` and called from `permission_resolution.py:425`; `resolve.py` does not appear. Corrected to name `toolguard.file_matching.decide_file_path_at_level_detailed`.
- **`test_ask_resolution.py:test_more_specific_layer_ask_gates_less_specific_layer_allow` — "(user)" and "(project)" layers that the fixture does not build.** `_layer` passes only `specificity` to `_prov`, whose `level` argument defaults to `"project"`, so both layers are project-level and only the specificity index differs. Reworded to say that.
- **`test_ask_resolution.py:test_allow_only_still_allows_and_deny_only_still_denies` — "Then behavior is unchanged by the ask fix".** A claim about a past commit, unobservable from the test. Rewritten to what it asserts. Its Given also said *"only a deny"* where the second config has an allow **and** a deny; corrected.
- **`test_tools_uninstall_readiness.py:test_every_entry_is_allow` asserted the safety the module now disclaims.** Verbatim: *"each pattern here is a literal, single-purpose action, not a broad grant"*. `uninstall_readiness.py`'s docstring now says the opposite under a bolded heading — *"Every `rm`/`uv tool uninstall` pattern grants more than it names"* — with the measured examples. This is the brief's over-grant case, and the test asserts nothing about it either way. The clause is cut; the `ask`-is-not-a-substitute reason (which the module still makes, and which the assertion does support) is kept, compressed.
- **`test_tools_clarity.py:1-7` — "These verify the first detector".** The file has covered `multi-section-interaction` and `cross-layer-dependent` for some time (two of its three test classes). Rewritten to name all three families.
- **`test_tools_clarity.py:test_same_specificity_layers_are_not_cross_layer` — "two DIFFERENT files"**, disproved in TB1. Corrected to "two layers of the same specificity", which is what the fixture builds and what the code checks.
- **`test_tools_uninstall_readiness.py:test_fresh_unconfigured_config_needs_every_entry` — "there is no ask-satisfies-me exemption anymore"**. Change history plus a claim about a deleted code path. Replaced with the two facts the test asserts (eight entries, verdict `ask`).

### TB: deleted (noise, no factual defect)

- **`test_bash_parser.py` — ten inline comments, every one a restatement of the line under it**: `# Pipeline should have multiple elements`, `# Should have pipeline with pipeline_element` (x2), `# pipeline_element should have compound_command (for subshells)`, `# The inner compound_command should have the command` (x2), `# Get to the subshell's compound_command`, `# Outer subshell`, `# Inner compound should contain another subshell`, `# Should have multiple parts connected by &&`, `# Basic parse should succeed`, and the red/green pair `# If we get here, grammar was fixed!` / `# Expected to fail with current grammar`. The last two are the closest thing in this batch to a live TDD instruction; the `skipTest` beside them says the same thing in code.
- **`test_zz_real_log_dir_guard.py` — three punch-list narratives.** The module docstring's design rationale for `_real_log_dir_guard.py` (which that module's own docstring carries), `test_guard_fires_for_log_command_via_config_log_dir`'s *"TOO-45 R1d changed log_command's signature from 12 loose parameters to one LogRecord + log_dir/config/log_format"*, and `test_guard_fires_for_claim_store_reap_via_logs_dir`'s *"TOO-45 R2: claim/is_claimed/release no longer take a logs_dir argument at all"*. In both tests the load-bearing sentence — *why this call site and not the sibling's* — is kept: the guard extracts `log_dir`/`config` by `inspect.signature` and must resolve against more than one signature; `reap` is the one claim-store entry point still taking a project `logs_dir`. Two class docstrings of 8 and 12 lines reduced to 2-3.
- **`test_zz_real_log_dir_guard.py:149` — "The resolution log is date-partitioned (unlike the discovery log the sibling test above uses), so build today's filename the same way log_command itself does."** Three lines explaining an `strftime` on the next line.
- **"All tests use stdlib unittest with BDD Given/When/Then docstrings"** — restatement of `.claude/rules/testing.md`, cut from `test_ask_resolution.py` and `test_tools_uninstall_readiness.py` (same line the TQ batch cut from `test_tools_self_permission.py`; it appears to have been copied between test files).
- Module-docstring inventories reduced to one or two lines each in `test_bash_parser.py` and `test_tools_uninstall_readiness.py`; the `TestAskAllowTieBreak` class docstring's `permissions._literal_prefix_specificity` citation and the `TestUninstallReadinessEvaluation` docstring's *"and is risk-aware"* (nothing in the class is about risk) cut.

### TB: rejected from the brief, with evidence

- **"Delete every other comment", applied to class and helper docstrings.** Same call as TQ/TX: `~/.claude/rules/python.md` requires them, so they were trimmed to one line, not removed. Module docstrings likewise.
- **Correcting the vacuous Given/When/Thens in `test_bash_parser.py`.** Not done, deliberately. Unlike TQ1/TX1, those docstrings are already *accurate*: "Then a non-None tree with a compound_command node is produced" is an exact description of `assertIsNotNone` + `assertTrue(hasattr(...))`. The defect is in the assertions, which are code. Rewriting the docstrings to say "Then the parse does not raise" would be honest but would also *hide* TB2 behind a plausible-looking Then — better that the docstring keeps describing the assertion, and the queue records that the assertion is the problem.
- **Adding the two missing docstrings in `test_tools_clarity.py`** — `TestCrossLayerInteraction._cross_layer` and the nested `mk` inside `_two_layer` have none, which `python.md` requires. Flagged, not added, per the no-added-prose constraint; both are three-line comprehensions.
- **"Nothing substantive" as a per-file verdict.** Claimed for none of the five: every file had at least a stale claim or a vacuous assertion. `test_bash_parser.py` is the closest — no *false* statement anywhere in it, only ten redundant inline comments and thirteen tests that cannot fail.

## TSO — from the comment sweep of the fourth `test/unit/` batch (4 files, 2026-08-12)

Editor pass over `test_tools_mining.py`, `test_config.py`, `test_architecture.py`, `test_tools_sorters.py`. 1,237 -> 1,070 lines. Comments and docstrings only; `--compare-against HEAD` reports zero drift on all four (it still reports `toolguard/__init__.py` and `tools/architecture_fitness.py`, the known non-sweep changes), `ruff format --check`/`check` clean, suite **2733 OK** (unchanged).

### TSO: vacuous or self-satisfying tests (code changes, NOT made here)

| # | test | why the assertion cannot fail |
|---|---|---|
| **TSO1** | `test_tools_sorters.py:TestSortPatterns.test_unknown_tool_sorts_after_edit` | **The asserted order is what the alphabetical tie-break produces on its own**, so the priority table the Then names is unpinned. Fixture is `["Bash(git:*)", "UnknownTool(some:*)", "Edit(file.py)"]` and the assertions are only `result[0].startswith("Bash(")` / `result[-1].startswith("UnknownTool(")` — Edit's position, the thing the Then talks about, is never checked. **Measured** by re-sorting the same fixture with the tool priority collapsed to a constant (`key=lambda p: (0, p.lower())`, i.e. `tool_priorities.get(tool_name, 4)` replaced by `0` for every tool): result is `['Bash(git:*)', 'Edit(file.py)', 'UnknownTool(some:*)']` and **both assertions still pass**, because `bash(` < `edit(` < `unknowntool(` lexically. **Shape of a fix**: use an unknown tool whose name sorts alphabetically *before* `Bash` — `AaaTool(some:*)` — and assert its index against `Edit`'s, not against the ends. Then only the priority rank can put it last. The Then was left alone deliberately (see the rejected section). |
| **TSO2** | `test_tools_sorters.py:TestStableRuleKey.test_key_ordering_consistent_with_sort` | **Both sides of the `assertEqual` are the same computation.** `stable_rule_key(pattern)` is a one-line `return get_tool_priority(pattern)` (`toolguard/tools/sorters.py:48`), and `sort_patterns` is `return sorted(patterns, key=get_tool_priority)` (`toolguard/rule_sort.py:77`) — so the test compares `sorted(p, key=get_tool_priority)` with `sorted(p, key=get_tool_priority)`. Any change to the key, right or wrong, changes both sides identically; the fixture's content is irrelevant. It fails only if someone decouples the two, which is a narrower guard than the Then implies. **Shape of a fix**: assert the expected order as a literal (`['Bash(git:*)', 'Read(/home/*)', 'Write(/tmp/out.txt)', 'Edit(file.py)']`) — that pins both functions against a stated contract instead of against each other. A separate one-line `assertIs(stable_rule_key.__wrapped__ ...)`-style identity check is not available (it is a wrapper, not an alias), so the literal is the whole fix. |

### TSO: near-vacuous, kept as notes

- **`test_tools_sorters.py:test_stable_sort_preserves_equal_key_order`** — the fixture cannot distinguish stability from case-sensitivity. It puts `"Bash(Git Status:*)"` *before* `"Bash(git status:*)"` and asserts that order survives. **Measured** with a case-SENSITIVE key (`(rank, pattern)` instead of `(rank, pattern.lower())`): the result is unchanged, because ASCII `G` (0x47) < `g` (0x67) — the very defect a stability test sits next to produces the same answer. CPython's `sorted` is stable by language guarantee, so with any key-based sort the assertion holds either way. **Fix shape**: swap the fixture order (lowercase first) and assert `idx_lower < idx_upper`; a case-sensitive key then fails while a stable case-insensitive sort passes.
- **`test_tools_sorters.py:test_extended_syntax_prefix_sorts_within_tool_bucket`** — its first assertion, `assertTrue(all(p.startswith("Bash(") for p in result))`, is over a fixture that is all-Bash, and `sort_patterns` reorders without rebuilding elements, so it cannot fail. The second (`lowered == sorted(lowered)`) is the real check and does pin that `[regex]`/`[glob]` prefixes stay inside the sort key.
- **`test_architecture.py:test_configuration_stays_in_config_module`** — `assertFalse(hasattr(config_types, "Configuration"))` is near-unreachable: `config_types` importing `toolguard.config` is what the sibling `test_leaf_modules_do_not_import_config` forbids, so only an outright redefinition could trip it. The `__module__` assertion beside it already covers the move-the-class case.
- **`test_config.py:test_discover_without_project_root`** — `assertIsInstance(configs, list)` is a does-not-crash test wearing an assertion. Not filed above because the Then says so out loud ("does not crash and returns a list"); nothing is concealed.

### TSO: coverage gaps found while checking claims against the modules

- **Nothing passes a `RuleEntry` to `sort_layer_rules`.** Answering the brief's question directly: no test in `test_tools_sorters.py` asserts a contract the module does not have — `TestSortPatternsWithRuleEntry` exercises only `sort_patterns`, which really is annotated `List[RuleEntryOrStr]`. The narrow `List[str]` on `sort_layer_rules`/`stable_rule_key` is therefore neither contradicted nor pinned by any test. **Fix shape**: one `sort_layer_rules([...RuleEntry...], [], None)` case would decide whether the annotation is a bug (it is, given the callee accepts the union) or the intended contract.
- **`test_config.py` has zero tests touching `Configuration.validation_issues`** (`grep -c validation_issues` -> 0), so the brief's false "in the detection order shown above" claim has no test asserting issue ordering here, in either direction. Whatever pins that belongs in the config-validation tests, not this file.
- **Two hand-maintained layer specs describe overlapping invariants.** `test_architecture.py:LAYERS` and `.pyscn.toml`'s `[[architecture.layers]]`/`[[architecture.rules]]` are edited independently. They are not redundant — `.pyscn.toml` allows intra-layer imports, so `config_types`/`rule_entry`/`rule_sort`/`config_write_guard` (all in its `config` layer) and `permission_resolution` (in `engine`, which may import `config`) importing `toolguard.config` would pass `check_layers` and fail only here — but a module added to one spec and not the other silently loses a guard.
