## SH — from the comment sweep of the fifth `test/unit/` batch (5 files, 2026-08-12)

Editor pass over `test_symlink_hierarchy.py`, `test_tools_annotate.py`, `test_normalization.py`, `test_tools_decision_ledger.py`, `test_tools_edit_proposal.py`. 1,769 -> 1,660 lines. Comments and docstrings only; `--compare-against HEAD` reports zero drift on all five (still `toolguard/__init__.py` and `tools/architecture_fitness.py`, the known non-sweep changes), `ruff format --check`/`check` clean, suite **2733 OK** (unchanged).

### SH: vacuous or self-satisfying tests (code changes, NOT made here)

| # | test | why the assertion cannot fail |
|---|---|---|
| **SH1** | `test_symlink_hierarchy.py:TestProjectRootThroughSymlink` — **both** tests (`test_symlinked_claude_still_anchors_the_project_root`, `test_symlinked_claude_matches_the_real_directory_control`) | **The subject of the test is not what decides the outcome.** `_SymlinkLayout.__init__` runs `(self.project / ".git").mkdir(parents=True)` on **every** layout, and `find_project_root` -> `require_project_root` stops at the nearest of `.git`/`.hg`/`.jj`/`.claude`/`CLAUDE.md`/`pyproject.toml`. The `.claude` symlink is therefore never load-bearing: `.git` alone anchors the root. **Measured** on three freshly built layouts (symlinked `.claude`, real `.claude`, **no `.claude` at all**), calling `find_project_root(project/src/pkg)`: `found == project` is `True` in all three. Delete `with_symlinked_claude()` from the first test and it still passes. The first test's Given — *"a project whose only marker is a SYMLINKED .claude directory"* — is false of the fixture, and "only marker" is precisely the property that would have made the assertion meaningful. **Shape of a fix**: add a `_SymlinkLayout(..., git_marker=False)` option and build the project with the symlinked `.claude` as its sole anchor; then removing symlink support from `require_project_root`'s anchor scan fails the test. The class is the one place in the file where that matters — the level-attribution and end-to-end classes genuinely depend on the symlink, because discovery has to read a file through it. |
| **SH2** | `test_symlink_hierarchy.py:test_symlinked_claude_matches_the_real_directory_control` (second, independent defect) | **Half the fixture the docstring describes does not exist.** Given: *"two otherwise identical projects, one with a real .claude and one with a symlinked .claude"*; Then: *"both return their own project directory"*. The body builds `real_layout` only and asserts once. There is no symlinked layout, no second call, and no comparison — the "control" the test name promises cannot fail because it is never taken. **Shape of a fix**: either build both layouts and assert the pair (which, given SH1, also needs the `.git` marker gone to mean anything), or delete the test as a duplicate of its sibling. Docstring deliberately left uncorrected — see the rejected section. |
| **SH3** | `test_symlink_hierarchy.py:test_symlinked_claude_still_anchors_the_project_root`, the second assertion | `self.assertNotEqual(found, layout.store / ".claude")` is dead: `layout.project` is `<root>/project` and `layout.store / ".claude"` is `<root>/store/toolguard/.claude`, so once the preceding `assertEqual(found, layout.project)` has passed the two cannot be equal. For this line to fail, the line above it must have failed first. Nothing to fix beyond deleting it — but it reads as a second, independent guard, and it is not. |

### SH: near-vacuous, kept as notes

- **`test_normalization.py:test_normalize_relative_path_with_project_root`** — **`project_root` can be ignored entirely and the test still passes.** The fixture creates the file, so the existence gate that is the *only* thing `project_root` does is satisfied, and `normalize_path`'s `else:` branch produces the identical answer. **Measured**: `normalize_path("test.txt", root)` with the file present -> `'./test.txt'`; `normalize_path("test.txt")` with no root -> `'./test.txt'`; the discriminating case, `normalize_path("nope.txt", root)` -> `'nope.txt'` (unprefixed) against `normalize_path("nope.txt")` -> `'./nope.txt'`. Nothing in the file covers that case, so the whole `project_root` parameter is unpinned. **Fix shape**: one extra assertion in this test — a name that does not exist under the root stays unprefixed. Two lines. (It is here rather than above because an *inverted* existence check would fail it; it is not strictly unfalsifiable, it just cannot fail for the reason it names.)
- **`test_tools_decision_ledger.py:test_user_level_uses_toolguard_namespace`, first assertion** — `assertEqual(ledger_path_for_level("user", Path(".")), dl.USER_LEDGER_PATH)` compares the function's return against the very module constant the function returns unchanged. It pins the link and nothing else; the second assertion (the `.toolguard/decisions.json` suffix) is what pins the location.
- **`test_tools_decision_ledger.py:test_written_file_carries_schema_and_level`, first assertion** — `data["schema"] == dl.LEDGER_SCHEMA` round-trips the writer's own constant back to itself. The `level` assertion beside it is against a literal and is the real check.

### SH: coverage gaps found while checking claims against the modules

- **`load_ledger` stamps `level` from the file BODY, not from the path it read** (`decision_ledger.py:292`, `level = data.get("level", "project")`), so a project ledger containing `"level": "user"` yields decisions that `record_decision` will write into `~/.toolguard/decisions.json` — a project file redirecting a write to the user's home. Nothing in the suite pins this either way: `test_record_then_load_roundtrips` asserts `level == "project"` from a file whose body says `project`, which is consistent with both readings. **Fix shape**: a test writing a project-path ledger whose body claims `"level": "user"`, asserting whichever behaviour is intended — with `HOME` redirected into a temp tree, since the wrong answer writes to the real `~/.toolguard`.
- **The `schema` tag is written and never validated.** `LEDGER_SCHEMA`'s own comment says so (*"``load_ledger`` does not check it"*), and `TestCorruptLedger`'s five cases cover JSON syntax, a missing/typed-wrong `decisions` array, a missing field and a bad enum — but never a foreign or absent `schema`. A future schema 2 will load silently as schema 1.
- **`apply_edits`' partial-enactment defect is unpinned.** `edit_proposal.py`'s docstring documents it (*"a `removed_patterns` entry that is no longer present is a no-op while the same edit's `added_patterns` still apply"*), but no test in `test_tools_edit_proposal.py` builds a `RuleEdit` whose removal misses. **Fix shape**: a `REPLACE` proposal removing `git:*` from a layer that no longer has it and adding `git status:*`, asserting the layer ends up with *both* the original rule and the addition — the characterisation that makes the defect visible to whoever changes it.
- **`RuleEdit.provenance` whole-`Provenance` equality is unpinned in its interesting case.** `test_unmatched_provenance_returns_config_unchanged` and `test_stale_edit_is_skipped` both use a ghost provenance differing in `level`, `specificity` **and** `path` at once, so neither shows that a one-field difference is enough to skip the edit. **Fix shape**: one case where only `specificity` differs.
- **`clarity_annotations` skips every non-`toml` layer** (`annotate.py:78`) and nothing in `test_tools_annotate.py` builds a `json` layer — `_config`'s `Provenance` is hardcoded `file_format="toml"`. The skip is the reason a native `settings.json` finding is silently dropped from the annotation map, and it is untested.
- **Nothing pins that a hand-written `# toolguard:` comment is dropped.** The module drops every line starting with the marker before re-inserting, human-authored or not (`annotate.py`'s docstring now says so). `test_preserves_human_comments_and_untouched_sections` uses `# human note: keep me`, which does not carry the prefix and so cannot exercise the case.

### SH: stale or wrong Given/When/Then and prose, corrected

- **`test_symlink_hierarchy.py:1-25` — a module docstring naming a function that no longer exists.** It listed *":func:`toolguard.config._level_for_path` attributes a config file to the `user` or `project` level by comparing RESOLVED paths"* as one of three pieces of machinery under test. `grep -rn _level_for_path toolguard/` returns nothing — the function was deleted under TOO-19, which the file's own `TestLevelAttributionThroughSymlink` docstring said in the next breath (*"now removed"*). The whole three-bullet motivation plus the TOO-19 Part-4 narrative is cut; the **isolation exception is kept** (compressed to three lines), because `.claude/rules/test-config-isolation.md` requires a hand-rolled isolation to carry a comment saying why. Its citation was also wrong — it said *"per test/unit/CLAUDE.md"*, and the rule lives in `.claude/rules/test-config-isolation.md`.
- **`test_tools_annotate.py:1-8` — "the idempotent, minimal-diff, human-comment-preserving section writer".** The same retracted guarantee the module tier removed from `annotate.py` (its bolded *"Design guarantee: Never clobbers human comments"* is gone, because a hand-written `# toolguard:` line is dropped like any other). Cut. `TestAnnotateSectionText`'s class docstring carried the short form — *"idempotent, minimal-diff, and human-safe"* — and lost `human-safe` for the same reason.
- **`test_tools_edit_proposal.py:test_layer_metadata_survives_rebuild` — "(defect B)".** A label from a review document nobody reading this file has. Cut; the Then already names the three fields.
- **`test_tools_edit_proposal.py:test_allow_wrapper_still_delegates` — "Then it edits the allow list exactly as before (single implementation)".** Both halves are unobservable from the test: "exactly as before" is a claim about a previous revision, and "single implementation" is a claim about `config_access.py`'s internals, not about the assertion. Replaced with what the assertion checks. (Not a laundering case: there is no defect hiding behind it — `with_layer_allow_replaced` really is a one-line delegation, and the test really does check the resulting list.)

### SH: deleted (noise, no factual defect)

- **`test_symlink_hierarchy.py` — four rationale paragraphs inside test docstrings.** `test_store_under_home_claude_is_still_a_project_level_config`'s seven-line footgun history (what the removed path-shape derivation did and that restoring it would fail the test), `test_symlinked_rules_file_is_loaded`'s *"mirrors Arnon's real setup, where gh.rules.toml is symlinked... and the TOO-19 near-miss"*, and two "why this test is not a duplicate of its sibling" notes compressed to one line each rather than deleted (the TB precedent: the load-bearing sentence is *why this case and not the neighbour's*).
- **`test_symlink_hierarchy.py` — six helper docstrings with `Args:`/`Returns:`/`Attributes:` blocks** on four-line fixture builders (`_SymlinkLayout` and its three methods, `build`, `_project_config_level`, `_verdicts`), reduced to one line each. 47 lines of docstring for 31 lines of fixture code.
- **`test_tools_annotate.py:TestAnnotateSectionTextStructuredEntry`** — the class docstring's TOO-19 corrective-change history (what the class *"previously also covered"* and which exception `parse_permissions_section_with_comments` *"now correctly"* raises) reduced to the surviving fact: single-line only, because a multi-line structured entry is not valid TOML.
- **`test_tools_annotate.py`** — *"All tests use stdlib unittest with BDD Given/When/Then docstrings"* (the copied restatement of `.claude/rules/testing.md` that TQ and TB also found), and the inline `# content outside the section is preserved`, which restates the `assertIn("[meta]", new)` it sits on.
- **`test_normalization.py` — nine inline comments, every one a restatement**: `# Clean up temp directory`, `# Create a file in temp dir`, `# Create a file and a symlink to it`, `# Normalize the symlink path`, the two-line `# The result should be the resolved target / (may be under home, so could be ~ prefixed)`, `# Symlink creation might fail on some systems`, `# Should still normalize format even if path doesn't exist`, `# On macOS, /tmp is a symlink to /private/tmp, so accept either` (the Then says it), and `# Current directory should get ./ prefix if relative` — which is also **wrong**: `normalize_path(".")` returns `'.'`, since a leading `.` skips the prefixing branch entirely (measured).
- **`test_tools_decision_ledger.py:1-8`** — the module docstring's five-item inventory of what the tests cover, and the TOO-15 Phase C citation.

### SH: rejected from the brief, with evidence

- **Correcting the two false Givens in `TestProjectRootThroughSymlink`.** Not done, deliberately, on the precedent TB and TSO both set. *"Given a project whose only marker is a SYMLINKED .claude directory"* (SH1) and *"Given two otherwise identical projects, one with a real .claude and one with a symlinked .claude"* (SH2) are the loudest remaining signals that these tests do not do what they claim. Rewriting them to describe the fixture — "a project with a `.git` marker and a symlinked `.claude`", "a project with a real `.claude`" — would be accurate and would leave two green tests that pin nothing, behind Givens nobody re-reads. The queue carries the defect; the file keeps the flag.
- **Correcting `test_normalize_relative_path_with_project_root`'s Then** (*"the path is normalized relative to the root as './test.txt'"*). Same call. Nothing is "normalized relative to the root" — the `./` is prepended verbatim and the root is consulted only for an `exists()` check. Rewriting it to *"Then the path receives a './' prefix"* would be true and would make the near-vacuity above invisible.
- **"Delete every other comment", applied to class and helper docstrings.** As in TQ/TX/TB/TSO: `python.md` requires them, so they were trimmed to one line, not removed. Module docstrings likewise.
- **Missing docstrings, flagged not added.** `test_tools_decision_ledger.py:TestQuery._decisions` has none, which `python.md` requires. Not added, per the no-added-prose constraint; it is a two-element literal list.
- **A `python.md` violation in test code, not fixed (code, not comments).** `test_normalization.py:tearDown` does `import shutil` inside the function — a local import, which `~/.claude/rules/python.md` prohibits without a documented circular-import reason. One line to move to the module header, but it is code.
- **"Nothing substantive" as a per-file verdict.** Claimed for **`test_tools_edit_proposal.py`**: two unobservable clauses in one Then and a stray review label, no false claim about behaviour anywhere, and its fixtures build exactly what its Givens describe. It is also the only file of the five whose comment volume was already proportionate. Not claimed for the other four.

## FL — from the comment sweep of the sixth `test/unit/` batch (5 files, 2026-08-12)

Editor pass over `test_file_lock.py`, `test_once_per.py`, `test_takeover_mode.py`, `test_config_write_guard.py`, `test_verdict_corpus.py`. 1,925 -> 1,746 lines. Comments and docstrings only; `--compare-against HEAD` reports zero drift on all five (still `toolguard/__init__.py` and `tools/architecture_fitness.py`, the known non-sweep changes), `ruff format --check`/`check` clean, suite **2733 OK** (unchanged).

### FL: vacuous or self-satisfying tests (code changes, NOT made here)

| # | test | why the assertion cannot fail |
|---|---|---|
| **FL1** | `test_once_per.py:TestOncePerInternalHousekeeping.test_successful_claim_triggers_reap` | **The predicate cannot see the thing the Then names.** The fixture takes the decoy claim with `ttl=timedelta(seconds=-1)`, so its `expires_at` is in the past the instant it is written, and `once_per_store.is_claimed` ends in `return datetime.fromisoformat(row[0]) >= datetime.now()` — an already-expired row reports False whether or not it was ever deleted. **Constructed**: ran the test body with `once_per_store.reap` replaced by a `Mock`, then queried the `claims` table directly against the patched `_STORE_PATH`. Result: `reap called: True` (the real one never ran), **`expired row still in table: True`**, and `assertFalse(is_claimed(...))` still **passes**. Delete `_maybe_sweep` entirely and this test stays green. The Then — *"Then the expired row is gone -- housekeeping ran as a side effect"* — asserts a row deletion with a liveness check. **Shape of a fix**: assert on the row, not on `is_claimed` — a raw `SELECT 1 FROM claims WHERE kind='other'` against the patched store path, which is the only observable that distinguishes "reaped" from "expired". |
| **FL2** | `test_once_per.py:TestOncePerInternalHousekeeping.test_already_satisfied_call_does_not_reattempt_housekeeping` | **The fixture's own first call provides an alternative route to the asserted outcome** (shape 14). `_maybe_sweep` claims `_SWEEP_KEY` under the same daily scope before it will call `reap`; the first `warn()` already took that claim, so the second call reaches `reap` under no circumstances — the `HELD_BY_SOMEONE_ELSE` early return in `_claim` is not what makes the assertion pass. **Constructed**: patched `OncePer._claim` to call `self._maybe_sweep(...)` on **every** call — i.e. housekeeping *is* reattempted, the exact regression the test name forbids — and `mock_reap.assert_not_called()` still **passes**. **Shape of a fix**: the observable for "was housekeeping reattempted" is the sweep *claim attempt*, not the reap call — spy on `once_per_store.claim` and assert no call with `kind=_SWEEP_KEY`, or `patch.object(OncePer, "_maybe_sweep")` and assert that. |
| **FL3** | `test_takeover_mode.py:TestBashTakeoverFiltering` — the pair `test_native_blanket_bash_allow_suppressed_when_takeover_enabled` and `test_native_blanket_bash_allow_not_suppressed_when_takeover_disabled` | **Two tests, one flag flipped between them, the same expected verdict, and neither observes the flag.** Both build native `Bash(*)` allow + hook `Bash(rm *)` deny and resolve `rm -rf /`. **Constructed**: resolved that exact fixture with `takeover_enabled=True` and `takeover_enabled=False` — `deny` both times. The hook deny matches directly, so the native allow never decides anything and takeover filtering cannot change the answer. The enabled test's Then — *"Then the native Bash(*) allow is suppressed by takeover filtering so the command reaches no allow at any level and is denied"* — attributes the verdict to a mechanism the fixture makes irrelevant; the disabled test's own (deleted) inline `# NOTE` admitted it, saying the test *"confirms that disabling takeover does not change which level is consulted first"*, which is not takeover filtering at all. **Shape of a fix**: the sibling `test_native_bash_allow_suppressed_specific_command_denied` is built right — no hook deny, and a command only the native `Bash(*)` matches. Its **takeover-disabled counterpart is the missing test**: same fixture, `takeover_enabled=False`, expect `allow`. That pair is the one that fails if filtering fires when it shouldn't, or fails to fire when it should; the current pair is a duplicate of each other. |
| **FL4** | `test_config_write_guard.py:TestPrivateHelpers.test_verify_config_text_toml_matches_tomllib_directly` | **Both sides compute the same thing** (shape 8). `verify_config_text(text, "toml")` is `_parse`, and `_parse`'s TOML branch is `return tomllib.loads(text)` — so *"cross-check against the real parser"* checks `tomllib.loads` against `tomllib.loads`. No text can make one accept and the other reject short of the module changing parsers, and the test contains **no assertion at all**. It is also a verbatim duplicate of `TestVerifyConfigText.test_valid_toml_text_raises_nothing`: same literal text, same call, same "raises nothing". **Shape of a fix**: delete it. If a differential test is genuinely wanted, it has to be against a *different* parser (e.g. that the wrapper does not accept something `tomllib` rejects, via `json`-shaped text) — which is what the invalid-TOML tests already do from the other direction. |

### FL: near-vacuous, kept as notes

- **`test_file_lock.py:TestWindowsBackendViaPatching.test_successful_lock_calls_locking_and_unlocking_before_close`** — **the Then names an ordering the assertions never check** (shape 12). It claims *"unlock before the descriptor closes, and the descriptor is seeked to 0 before BOTH -- acquire and release are symmetric, neither relies on the fd's implicit position"*, but the three assertions are `assertIn(LK_NBLCK, modes)`, `assertIn(LK_UNLCK, modes)` and `assertGreaterEqual(mock_lseek.call_count, 2)` — a set membership test on an unordered list of modes plus a call *count*. **Constructed**: replaced `_try_acquire_windows` with one that locks **without ever seeking** and `_release_windows` with one that seeks **twice**, keeping the total at 2. All three assertions still pass, with zero seeks before the lock call. (The "before close" half is not strictly unfalsifiable — a post-close `os.lseek` would raise `EBADF`, `_release_windows` swallows it, and `LK_UNLCK` would go unrecorded — but that is incidental, not asserted.) **Fix shape**: have the fake record `(mode, os.lseek(fd, 0, os.SEEK_CUR))` and assert the sequence, or interleave `mock_lseek.mock_calls` with `fake.calls` and assert the order.
- **`test_config_write_guard.py:test_expected_patterns_none_skips_content_loss_check`** — asserts only `path.exists()`. It does distinguish `None` from an empty iterable (iterating `None` would raise), but nothing checks the file's *content*, so a write that produced an empty file would pass a test whose Then says *"the write succeeds"*.

### FL: coverage gaps found while checking claims against the modules

- **`test_takeover_mode.py` never exercises `additional_ignored_patterns` with a value.** It appears once, as `assertEqual(tc.additional_ignored_patterns, ())` in the defaults test. It is a documented user-facing knob (the whole point of which is extending the ignored set beyond the four blanket patterns), and nothing here shows a pattern added through it actually being suppressed. **Fix shape**: one hook-toml fixture with `additional_ignored_patterns = ["Bash(git *)"]`, a native `Bash(git *)` allow, and a `git status` resolve expecting the fallback rather than `allow`.
- **`test_config_write_guard.py` never passes `expected_patterns` with `file_format="json"`.** Every content-loss test is TOML; `patterns_in_config_text` is likewise only ever called with `"toml"`, so its documented `json.JSONDecodeError` contract is unpinned and `_patterns_in_parsed` is only ever fed a `tomllib` result.
- **`_entry_pattern`'s None branch is unreachable from this file.** No test builds a structured entry without a (string) `match` — the shape that makes `normalize_entries_preserving` synthesize a `repr(raw)` stand-in and, if a caller lets it into `expected_patterns`, blocks every future write of that file permanently (documented at `toolguard/rule_entry.py:140-165`). The caller-side chokepoint (`real_patterns`) is pinned in `test_rule_entry.py`/`test_migration.py`, but nothing characterises the guard's own half: that an `expected_patterns` member which cannot appear in *any* parsed text refuses forever rather than once. **Fix shape**: one test passing `expected_patterns=["{'additionalContext': 'x'}"]` against valid text and asserting the refusal — which documents why `real_patterns` exists at the place the damage happens.
- **`test_once_per.py` never shows a period rollover re-enabling a throttled thing.** Every test lives inside one `day_scope()`. The store-level equivalent is covered (`test_once_per_store.py:test_different_scope_claims_independently`), but at the facade the coupling between `_Period._scope` and `_Period._retention` is unpinned — which matters because `day`'s retention is `timedelta(days=7)`, seven times its period. That is **not** a bug (`scope` is part of the primary key, so a new day is a different row, and the long ttl only delays reaping), but nothing in the suite says so, and the discrepancy reads as one. **Fix shape**: one facade test using `context`/a patched `day_scope` to advance the day and assert `warn()` prints again.
- **`test_file_lock.py` never exercises `DEFAULT_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`.** Every call passes an explicit `timeout_seconds`; the module's default (10.0) and therefore the default poll budget are untouched. Minor, noted for completeness.

### FL: stale or wrong Given/When/Then and prose, corrected

- **`test_config_write_guard.py:1-8` — a citation to a file that does not exist.** *"per test/unit/CLAUDE.md's isolation checklist"*; `ls test/unit/CLAUDE.md` -> no such file, and the checklist lives in `.claude/rules/test-config-isolation.md`. The **same wrong citation SH found in `test_symlink_hierarchy.py`** — and it is not two occurrences but **nine**, across `test_config_write_guard.py`, `test_resolve.py:2303`, `test_rule_sort.py:45`, `test_rule_entry.py:9,739` and `test_configuration.py:2240,3392,3636,3781`. Fixed in my file; **the other six sites are outside this batch and still wrong**, so this belongs to whoever sweeps those files (a one-line `grep -rn "test/unit/CLAUDE.md" test/` finds them all).
- **`test_file_lock.py:70` Given — "via a separate file descriptor"**, which is the wrong noun and is the exact error the `file_lock.py` module docstring was corrected for: `flock` binds to the open file **description**, not the descriptor. Reworded to say description (the module docstring's own wording), since this is a factual correction, not a rewrite to fit the assertions.
- **`test_verdict_corpus.py:113-124` — a Then plus a rationale paragraph that restates the `self.fail()` message three lines below it.** Five test methods carried "STOP and investigate ... it is NEVER fixed by regenerating goldens.jsonl. See test/verdict_corpus/README.md" in the docstring, and every one of them passes the same sentence to `self.fail()` **as code** — the reader who trips the guard sees the failure message, not the docstring. The docstring copies were cut; the failure strings are untouched (they are code) and the module docstring keeps the one-line HARD/TRACKED statement.

### FL: left uncorrected and flagged, per the settled precedent

- **`test_once_per.py:test_successful_claim_triggers_reap`'s Then** — *"Then the expired row is gone -- housekeeping ran as a side effect, with no caller-visible 'sweep' call anywhere"*. False of the assertion (FL1). Left verbatim: rewriting it to *"Then is_claimed reports the expired claim as not claimed"* would be true, green, and would pin nothing.
- **`test_takeover_mode.py:test_native_blanket_bash_allow_not_suppressed_when_takeover_disabled`'s Then** — *"Then the native Bash(*) allow is NOT filtered, so the allow at the native level wins (unless the hook-level deny matches first at its level) -- specifically the deny fires here because hook level is more specific and deny-first within level wins"*. A Then that predicts its own opposite in a parenthesis and then resolves it is a Then that is not describing a test. Left, with FL3 carrying the defect. Its sibling's Then is left for the same reason.
- **`test_config_write_guard.py:test_write_uses_replace_not_truncate_in_place`'s Then** — *"Then the final content on disk is byte-identical to what was requested"*. The assertion is `json.load(f) == json.loads(new_text)`: **parsed** equality, so a rewrite that reordered keys, reindented or changed whitespace would pass a test that promises byte identity. Nothing checks `os.replace` either, despite the test name. Left.
- **`test_config_write_guard.py:TestPrivateHelpers`'s class docstring** — *"Narrow direct tests for the module's internal parsing helpers."* Neither test calls a private helper: one calls public `verify_config_text` (FL4), the other public `verified_write_config`, reaching `_atomic_write` only indirectly. Left as the flag on a class whose contents drifted away from its name.

### FL: deleted (noise, no factual defect)

- **`test_file_lock.py`** — the module docstring's punch-list citation and *"following test_once_per_store.py's harness"* (repeated again in `TestConcurrentProcesses`, and a cross-reference an IDE answers); two multi-line class docstrings reduced to one line; three `pass  # must not raise ...` inline comments; four Then tails of pure rationale (*"real output, not a shape only ever produced by hand-constructing the exception"* — a jab at `test_reason_is_structured_and_message_carries_detail` two tests above it; *"genuine cross-process mutual exclusion, not merely in-process fd contention"*; *"one project's migration never blocks another's"*; *"exactly why flock (never a hand-rolled O_EXCL lockfile) is used"*, which is `file_lock.py`'s module docstring verbatim).
- **`test_once_per.py`** — the module docstring's move history (*"Moved here from test_session_warnings.py ... which no longer touches this facade at all"*), two class docstrings' punch-list item numbers, and four Then tails, the longest being *"this is the exact defect TOO-45 punch-list #01 item 1 identifies and fixes: project=None used to be silently treated as 'proceed', defeating a caller's fail-closed policy on EVERY invocation"* — five lines of change history under a Then whose assertion is `assertEqual(calls, [])`.
- **`test_takeover_mode.py`** — the module docstring's drop/port decision log (*"Scenarios already covered by test_configuration.py ... were dropped to avoid duplication; see the implementation report for the full drop/port decision log"*, pointing at a document that is not in the repo); `_make_bash_config`'s 12-line `Args:`/`Returns:` block and `_resolve`'s `Returns:` block, on a 20-line and a 2-line helper; the 5-line class docstring; and **eight inline comments, seven of which restate the assertion immediately below them** (`# Blanket * should be filtered`, `# Both patterns should be present (no filtering)`, `# Deny pattern should remain`, `# Allow * should be filtered`, `# * from toolguard_hook should remain`, `# takeover_mode in settings.json is ignored`, `# Default ignored_allow_patterns includes standard blanket patterns`). The eighth is the `# NOTE` block quoted in FL3.
- **`test_config_write_guard.py`** — three Given/Then TOO-19 narratives (*"the exact corruption shape TOO-19's find_section_boundaries bug used to produce"*, *"reproducing what the old, non-line-anchored find_section_boundaries() used to produce"*, *"belt-and-braces regression net for that exact defect class, independent of whether find_section_boundaries() itself is ever broken again"*) reduced to the fixture shape they describe, which is what a reader needs; the 5-line `TestVerifiedWriteConfigCreatesParentDirectory` docstring about `installer.py`'s first-ever call; three inline comments.
- **`test_verdict_corpus.py`** — the module docstring 48 -> 16 lines. Cut: the "Two test classes, two corpora" section (both class docstrings say it), the *"Kept as two separate TestCase classes ... e.g. python -m unittest test.unit.test_verdict_corpus.TestVerdictCorpusEndToEnd"* paragraph, and two thirds of the isolation note, whose argument (*"Sharing one isolation/loading path ... is what makes compare_goldens meaningful"*) survives in one sentence. Kept: the HARD/TRACKED distinction, the README pointer, and the isolation exception, which `.claude/rules/test-config-isolation.md` requires a hand-rolled isolation to carry.

### FL: rejected from the brief, with evidence

- **The brief's description of `verified_write_config` is inaccurate in one word.** It says the function *"recomputes `present` from the text on disk each call"*. It recomputes `present` from the **candidate text being written** (`parsed = _parse(text, file_format)`), not from disk; the *caller* is what typically rebuilds `expected_patterns` from disk on every run (`installer.py:1037` does exactly that via `patterns_in_config_text(original, "toml")`). The conclusion the brief draws is right and the permanent-write-block is real — it just needs both halves to be true: a synthesized pattern in `expected_patterns` + a caller that recomputes it from the file each run. Recorded so the next reader does not go looking for a disk read inside the guard.
- **The brief's expectation that a `test_verdict_corpus.py` comment repeats `corpus_build.py`'s false "the in-process corpus cannot observe override detection" claim.** It does not — searched the file: no such claim, and the module correctly treats `overrides` as a HARD-tier field via `test_no_sub_command_breakdown_changed`. Confirmed against `fixture_loader.py:496` (`decision_to_golden` emits `overrides`) and `BREAKDOWN_FIELDS = ("sub_matches", "overrides")` at line 856. Nothing to fix here.
- **The brief's shape-5 lead on `test_takeover_mode.py`** (a subject stripped upstream by `config.permission_layers`). Checked all nine tests: the four `load_file_path_patterns` tests assert *both* directions of the stripping (`assertNotIn("*")` enabled, `assertIn("*")` disabled), so the stripping is the observable, not a hidden upstream step; and the `TestBashTakeoverFiltering` defect turned out to be shape 14, not shape 5 — the native allow is never stripped in the disabled case and still does not matter. The dead downstream guard in `danger.py` the brief mentions has no test in this file.
- **"Delete every other comment", applied to class, helper and module docstrings.** As in TQ/TX/TB/TSO/SH: `~/.claude/rules/python.md` requires them, so they were trimmed to one line (module docstrings to a short paragraph), not removed. The two `#:` constant labels kept — `_PRELUDE` in `test_file_lock.py` and `_ACCEPT_PROSE_ENV_VAR` in `test_verdict_corpus.py` — are one line each and name a value whose purpose is not on its own line.
- **"Nothing substantive" as a per-file verdict.** Claimed for **`test_verdict_corpus.py`**: its prose was by far the batch's heaviest (48-line module docstring, five rationale paragraphs), but every statement in it was **true**, every Given/When/Then matched its assertions, and both of its non-corpus tests (`TestBuildHookPayloadPayloadKeySeam`) can genuinely fail — checked that `mcp__local-tools__checked_bash` really is absent from `tool_spec.TOOLS_BY_NAME`, so the fallback test exercises the fallback. Its problem was volume and duplication with code that says the same thing, not accuracy. Not claimed for the other four.

## PT — from the comment sweep of the seventh `test/unit/` batch (4 files, 2026-08-12)

Editor pass over `test_patterns.py`, `test_tools_redundancy.py`, `test_error_reporter.py`, `test_tools_transcript_harvest.py`. 1,787 -> 1,663 lines (421->400, 436->396, 439->396, 491->471). Comments and docstrings only; `--compare-against HEAD` reports zero drift on all four (still `toolguard/__init__.py` and `tools/architecture_fitness.py`, the known non-sweep changes), `ruff format`/`check` clean, suite **2733 OK** (unchanged). Every "constructed" below was run in one process that monkeypatches an already-imported module object and re-runs the named test; nothing in the repo was written.

### PT: vacuous or self-satisfying tests (code changes, NOT made here)

| # | test | why the assertion cannot fail |
|---|---|---|
| **PT1** | `test_patterns.py` — **`TestNativePattern` (all 13 tests) and `TestPatternTypeComparison` (both tests)** | **The asserted values are what the *other* pattern type produces too** (shape 4), across the whole NATIVE surface. **Constructed**: rebound `match_pattern` so `PatternType.NATIVE` dispatches to the `GLOB` branch — i.e. NATIVE's hand-written no-backtracking segment scanner is deleted outright — and re-ran both classes. **All 15 tests pass.** The two semantics are genuinely different: `match_pattern(NATIVE, "*id_rsa", "cat id_rsa.pub id_rsa")` is **False** and the GLOB equivalent is **True** — the documented under-match, and the one input that would have separated them. `TestPatternTypeComparison` is the worst of it, because its whole subject is the difference: `test_glob_vs_native_wildcard_semantics` makes three `assertTrue` calls and no `assertFalse`, and its Then (*"Then GLOB's `*` spans spaces within a component while NATIVE finds the segments in order"*) names a distinction none of the three observes. **Shape of a fix**: one `assertFalse(match_pattern(NATIVE, "*id_rsa", "cat id_rsa.pub id_rsa"))` with an `assertTrue` on the GLOB/DEFAULT counterpart pins the end-anchor semantics, the difference between the types, and the filed defect, in two lines. Note this characterises current behaviour — the under-match is a false negative and on a deny rule a bypass, so the test should be written to *fail* if the scanner is ever fixed to backtrack, or written against the fixed behaviour as a RED test. |
| **PT2** | `test_error_reporter.py:TestActiveRegistersTheAmbientReporter.test_active_restores_the_previous_registration_on_exit` **and** `test_active_restores_on_exception` | **The stderr shapes of "restored" and "leaked" are byte-identical by design — and a third test in the same file proves it.** Both assert `buf.getvalue() == "[WARNING] <msg>\nCorrective steps: n/a\n"` after the block. If `active()` never restored, the leaked `Reporter(log_dir=tmpdir)` would route through `error_log._log_entry`, which prints `f"[{level}] {message}"` then `f"Corrective steps: {corrective_steps}"` — the exact bytes `error_reporter._print_fallback` prints. `TestFallbackShapeMatchesTheLoggedEcho` asserts that equality as its entire point, so the file simultaneously proves the observable is blind and relies on it. **Constructed**: replaced `active()` with a version whose `finally` does nothing. Both tests still **pass**; the sibling `test_nested_active_restores_the_outer_reporter_on_exit` **fails**, because it is the only one of the three that looks at the log directory. **Shape of a fix**: copy the nested test's observable — after the block, `self.assertEqual(list(Path(tmpdir).glob("toolguard-warning-*.md")), [])`, i.e. the post-block warning did NOT reach the exited registration's log stream. |
| **PT3** | `test_error_reporter.py:TestActiveRegistersTheAmbientReporter.test_report_notice_routes_through_the_registered_reporter` | **The asserted value is what the unregistered default produces** (shape 4). `notice` has `log_fn_name=None`, so a registered `Reporter(log_dir=tmpdir)` and a bare `Reporter()` both emit exactly `"migration running\n"` and nothing else — the registry is unobservable through stderr for this severity. The Then says *"Then it reaches that Reporter's stderr fallback (notice has no log stream), **not a bare unregistered default**"*, which is the one thing the assertion cannot tell apart. **Constructed**: rebound `report_notice` to `Reporter().notice(message)` — the registry ignored completely — and the test still **passes**. **Shape of a fix**: register a `Reporter` subclass (or `patch.object(Reporter, "notice")` on the instance) and assert the call landed on *that object*; identity is the only observable, since the routing table gives notice no destination that varies. |
| **PT4** | `test_error_reporter.py:TestLogDirectoryUnresolvable.test_unresolvable_log_dir_falls_back_to_stderr_without_raising` | **The named path is never taken** (shape 2): the directory is not unresolvable, and there is no fallback. `error_log._log_entry`'s first statement is `log_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)`, so a merely-missing directory is **created** and the write succeeds; stderr carries the message from `_log_entry`'s own unconditional echo, not from `_print_fallback`. **Constructed**: (a) ran the fixture and inspected the "missing" directory afterwards — `exists: True`, containing `toolguard-warning-2026-08-12.md`; (b) replaced `error_reporter._print_fallback` with a no-op — the stderr fallback deleted entirely — and the test still **passes**, while the sibling `TestLogWriteFailureDegradesToStderr.test_write_failure_still_produces_stderr_and_does_not_raise` correctly **fails**. The class docstring (*"The log directory does not exist / cannot be written to"*) is false of its own fixture. **Shape of a fix**: it is already covered — the file-in-place-of-a-directory test is the real fallback test. Either delete this one, or make it genuinely unwritable (a directory chmod'd `0o500` under the tmpdir) and add `self.assertEqual(list(missing.glob("*")), [])` so "no log was written" is asserted alongside "stderr got it". |
| **PT5** | `test_tools_redundancy.py:TestCorpusRedundancy.test_finding_attributes_populated` **and** `test_combined_static_and_corpus_findings` | **Guarded and dead assertions on the mechanism each names.** The first wraps every assertion in `if corpus_findings:`, so a corpus checker that returns nothing asserts nothing — the exact regression a test named *"finding attributes populated"* exists to catch. The second's Then says *"Then **both** static and corpus findings are returned"*, but its two assertions are `assertIn("static", kinds)` and `assertGreater(len(static_findings), 0)` — the same claim twice (the second can only fail after the first already has, shape 16), and nothing about corpus findings at all; the deleted inline comment admitted it (*"corpus finding may or may not appear depending on config coverage"*). **Constructed**: rebound `redundancy.find_corpus_redundant_allows` to always return `[]`. Both tests still **pass**; `test_corpus_redundant_rule_detected`, which asserts unconditionally, **fails**. **Shape of a fix**: drop the `if` and assert `len(corpus_findings) >= 1` first, then the attributes; and give the combined test a real corpus assertion (`self.assertIn("corpus", kinds)`) or rename it to what it checks. |

Fix shape: the same two equality assertions here.

Before the `_config_without_allow` fix, a structured entry could never produce a modified config, `find_corpus_redundant_allows` skipped it on the identity guard, and `assertNotIn(...)` was satisfied for the wrong reason. It is only meaningful paired with the positive test above it (which does fail on a revert), so it should not be read as independent evidence. No fix proposed; noted so the pair is not split.

Fix shape: keep two in-window entries written out of order and assert the resulting order.

### PT: coverage gaps found while checking claims against the modules

- **`test_tools_transcript_harvest.py` never exercises a registered, non-builtin tool — the gap the brief predicted, and it is papered over.** `harvest_transcript_file` gates on `if tool not in BUILTIN_TOOLS: continue` while `_command_for_tool` resolves the payload key from the registry-wide `TOOLS_BY_NAME`. `mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command` is a fully described `ToolKind.COMMAND` entry with `payload_key="command"` and `is_builtin=False` (`toolguard/tool_spec.py:58-63`), so it is recognised, governable, documented as something users should govern — and **silently never reaches the corpus**. The only test near it, `test_non_governed_tool_skipped`, uses `mcp__basic-memory__search`, which is not in the registry at all, so it cannot distinguish "skipped because not builtin" from "skipped because not recognised"; its Given calls it *"a non-governed tool"*, and the (deleted) module docstring said *"Non-governed tools are skipped"* — both wrong about which set the gate reads. **Fix shape**: one transcript entry for `mcp__jetbrains__execute_terminal_command` asserting the current behaviour and naming it as the gap, plus a decision on whether the gate should be `KNOWN_TOOL_NAMES` (or the configured governed set) rather than `BUILTIN_TOOLS`. That decision is a code change and belongs in a ticket, not in this file.
- **`test_patterns.py` never calls `match_pattern` with `PatternType.DEFAULT`.** `test_parse_default_pattern` only checks that `parse_pattern` reports the type. The DEFAULT branch (`fnmatch.fnmatch`, whose `*` **does** cross `/`, unlike GLOB's) is therefore unexercised here — matching the brief's note that it has no live caller either. Worth one test or a deletion decision, not both.
- **`test_patterns.py` never exercises GLOB `**` anywhere but as a whole path component.** Every globstar test writes `/**/`; `PurePath.full_match` degrades `**` inside a component to a plain `*`, so e.g. `match_pattern(GLOB, "/tmp/a**b", "/tmp/a/x/b")` is **False** (measured) and nothing says so. This is the second filed `patterns.py` defect and it is entirely unpinned.
- **`test_tools_redundancy.py` asserts none of the safety `redundancy.py` explicitly disclaims.** Checked both halves of ticket 22: (a) `_normalised_body` case-folds and collapses double spaces while the matchers do neither, so `Git *`/`git *` share a key and match disjoint commands — no test builds a key-sharing pair whose match-sets differ, so nothing pins that a static finding is *review*, not a safe deletion; (b) `find_corpus_redundant_allows` iterates takeover-**filtered** rules (`per_layer_rules`) while `_config_without_allow` searches **raw** layer content, so it can strip a dead copy and report the live one — **no test in the file builds a takeover-mode layer at all**, so that divergence is completely unexercised. Neither is a false claim in the tests; both are absences.

Verified by construction — with `_print_fallback` disabled that test fails, as it should. ## CS — from the comment sweep of the eighth `test/unit/` batch (3 files, 2026-08-12)

Editor pass over `test_compound_resolve_seam.py`, `test_hook_eval.py`, `test_tools_log_harvest.py`. 1,498 -> 1,364 lines (494->421, 501->459, 503->484). Comments and docstrings only; `--compare-against HEAD` reports zero drift on all three (still `toolguard/__init__.py` and `tools/architecture_fitness.py`, the known non-sweep changes), `ruff format`/`check` clean, suite **2733 OK** (unchanged). Every "constructed" below is a real mutation run: either an in-memory module rebuild (source string rewritten, `exec`'d, swapped into `sys.modules`) or, for the whole-suite checks, a `copytree` of the repo into a `TemporaryDirectory` that was mutated and run there — **the working tree was never written to**. The copied-tree runs carry 4 environment-artifact failures (`test_architecture_fitness.TestSmokeAgainstRealTree` x2, `test_hook_error_reporter.TestOrdinaryInvocationStderr` x2) present in the unmutated baseline copy too; "detected by NONE" below means *no failure beyond those four*.

### CS: vacuous or self-satisfying tests (code changes, NOT made here)

| # | test | why the assertion cannot fail |
|---|---|---|
| **CS1** | `test_compound_resolve_seam.py:TestAskFloorStubOverrideNeverLeaks.test_stub_override_does_not_leak_into_compound_overrides` — **an entire test class, and the only coverage the mechanism has anywhere** | **The fixture's own setup provides a second, unrelated route to the asserted outcome** (shape 17). The class exists to pin `resolve.py:274-283`'s `not unit.audits_as_one` gate, which keeps an inline-code unit's outer-stub probe from contributing a conflict-log entry. But the fixture leaves `undecidable_fallback` unset, so the ask-floor leaf floors to `'ask'`, and `resolve_bash_permission_detailed:332` then executes `if decision != "allow": overrides = []` — which empties `overrides` regardless of the gate. `tools/corpus_build.py:472` documents that clamp in its own comment, so the collision was known at the other end. **Constructed**: rebuilt `toolguard/resolve.py` in memory with `not unit.audits_as_one` deleted from the guard and re-ran the class — **passes**. Then ran the **whole 2733-test suite** against the same mutation in a throwaway repo copy: **nothing anywhere detects it**. Isolated the two routes directly — same config, same command `python -c "import os"`: with `undecidable_fallback='ask'` both real and gate-removed return `overrides=[]`; with `undecidable_fallback='allow'` the real resolver returns `[]` and the gate-removed one returns `[('python -c', ConflictOverride(winning_pattern='python *', ...))]`. **Shape of a fix**: add `"undecidable_fallback": "allow"` to the more-specific layer of the existing fixture and expect `decision == "allow"`. Two lines, same command, same rules — and then the gate is the only thing keeping the list empty. |
| **CS2** | `test_tools_log_harvest.py:TestHarvest.test_harvest_with_since_filter` **and** `test_harvest_with_max_age_days` | **Both of `harvest()`'s two time-window filters are individually invisible to the whole suite.** `harvest` drops out-of-window data twice: once by **file name** (`if floor is not None and log_date < floor: continue`) and once by **entry timestamp** (`file_entries = [e for e in file_entries if e.timestamp >= floor_dt]`). Every fixture in the file gives each log file a name that agrees with the timestamps inside it, so either filter alone produces the asserted result. **Constructed**: deleted each filter in turn and ran the **whole suite** in a throwaway repo copy — **neither deletion is detected by any test**; deleting both is what the tests actually require. (A narrower in-memory sweep over the file's own 18 tests gives the same answer.) **Shape of a fix**: one fixture where the two disagree, in each direction. To pin the file-name filter, put an entry timestamped **inside** the window into a file named for a day **outside** it (`toolguard-2026-06-19.md` holding a `## 2026-06-20 10:00:00` section) and assert it is dropped. To pin the entry filter, do the reverse (`toolguard-2026-06-20.md` holding a `## 2026-06-19 23:50:00` section). Both are shapes a real log can take. |

### CS: near-vacuous, kept as notes

- **`test_hook_eval.py:TestResolveEventAntiDrift` (both tests)** — falsifiable but far weaker than its name. `hook._resolve_event`'s entire non-guard body is `return decide(config, tool_name, target, extended_syntax)`, and `api.decide`'s `extended_syntax` default is `True` — the same value the tests pass — so **both sides of every `assertEqual` compute the same call**, and the five-command loop in `test_bash_verdicts_match_decide` exercises one code path five times. It can fail (patching `toolguard.hook.decide` breaks it), but it cannot detect the drift its docstring names: **constructed** by rebuilding `hook.py` in memory with the delegation replaced by a hand-rolled dispatch straight to `resolve_file_path_permission_detailed`/`resolve_bash_permission_detailed` — the pre-refactor shape — and both tests still **pass**. Fix shape: if delegation is the property, assert it structurally (`patch("toolguard.hook.decide")` and check it was called with the resolved target); if behaviour is the property, the loop should include an input where a guard and the facade could disagree.
- **`test_hook_eval.py`: four `assert_not_called()` on structurally unreachable paths.** `test_eval_allows_and_is_read_only` and `test_eval_malformed_stdin_fails_safe` each assert `log_command` and `run_auto_migration` were not called, but `main()` returns at `if args.eval_mode: _run_eval_mode(); return` before the block containing either call, and `_run_eval_mode` reaches neither; in the malformed-stdin test the exception is raised before `load_configuration` is even attempted. They would only fail if someone added logging *inside* `_run_eval_mode`, which is a narrower guarantee than "read-only" reads as. The load-bearing assertions in those two tests are the decision on stdout and `mock_stderr.getvalue() == ""`.
- **`test_tools_log_harvest.py:test_discovery_entries_are_skipped`** — cannot distinguish which guard skips the section. `_DISCOVERY_ENTRY` has neither a `Status` nor a `Command` field, so `_parse_section`'s two independent `return None` guards each suffice. **Constructed**: with the `if status is None` guard deleted, only `test_malformed_no_status_is_skipped` fails; this test still passes. It is fully redundant with the two `_MALFORMED_*` tests that isolate one guard each. No fix proposed — the redundancy is harmless and the Then's claim ("Discovery sections are skipped") is true.
- **`test_compound_resolve_seam.py:test_undecidable_segment_plus_plain_leaf`** — the fixture's `allow=["ls*", "diff*"]` includes an inert rule. An `'undecidable'` unit has no `parts`, so `_decide` is never called for it and `diff*` is never consulted; the segment's `'ask'` comes from the floor. Harmless, but it invites the reading that the `diff` rule was evaluated and lost.

### CS: coverage gaps found while checking claims against the modules

- **The seam file pins extraction only where extraction is already correct, and none of the three filed splitter bypasses.** It does reach the extractor (`_unit_for`'s PEG re-split, `decompose`'s process-substitution split, `_extract_outer_command`'s stub), so "resolution only" is not the right description — but **measured**, `decompose("while rm -rf /tmp/x; do :; done")` returns a **single** `plain` unit with `parts=(':',)`: the loop condition, the only dangerous command on the line, is gone before any rule is consulted. `until` behaves identically. Nothing in this file, whose subject is exactly `sub_matches` content and order, records that a sub-command can vanish from `sub_matches` entirely. The heredoc-sink theft and the escaped-quote heredoc-hiding bypasses are likewise unpinned here. **Fix shape**: three characterization tests asserting the *current* `sub_matches` for those three inputs, marked as pinning a known bypass so a grammar fix fails them loudly rather than silently.
- **`test_hook_eval.py` never drives `--eval` against a config whose `governed_tools` excludes the probed tool at `main()` level.** `TestResolveEventEdgeCases.test_non_governed_tool_is_allowed` covers `_resolve_event` directly, but no `TestEvalModeMain` test checks that the printed JSON for an ungoverned tool is an allow — which is what the security-audit skill would actually read. Small, one test.
- **`test_tools_log_harvest.py` never gives `harvest` both `since` and `max_age_days`.** `harvest` resolves them with "the later floor applies" (`if floor is None or age_floor > floor`), and that reconciliation is unexercised in either direction.
- **Nothing exercises `_parse_command_field`'s non-file-tool wrapper branch.** A `Bash(...)`- or `Task(...)`-shaped command field is documented to fall through to `("Bash", raw)`, and `_TOOL_WRAPPER_RE` matches it, but no fixture has one — so the `if tool_name in FILE_TOOLS` discrimination is only ever taken on the true side.
